\begin{abstract}\label{sec:abstract}
 
In 2017, Google Research published \textit{Attention Is All You Need} \cite{vaswani2017attentionneed}.  Their paper introduced the Transformer architecture that reshaped modern AI.   Just five years later, OpenAI's ChatGPT \cite{chatgpt} triggered AI's mainstream adoption.

Yet while our screens flicker with progress, space travel and clean energy breakthroughs remain comparatively rare.

\textbf{Dude, Where's My Spaceship?}

Just as attention led to AI breakthroughs, this paper argues precision aim will let us solve grand challenges in the physical world.

Specifically, we propose spacecraft propulsion via momentum transfer from targeted orbital impacts.  Consider a ``PuffSat,'' a single-use microsatellite with a mass of roughly 25–100 kilograms, comparable to the unofficial mass of a 27U CubeSat \cite{cubesat_advanced_standard}.  When triggered, a PuffSat rapidly converts nearly all its mass into a low-density gas, generated explosively or by atomizing cold solids or liquids (see \autoref{sec:puffsat_design}). Ultra-lightweight microthrusters and electronics let it make small navigation adjustments.  Deployed in sequence, these PuffSats gasify and strike a shock-absorbing pusher plate on a target rocket, each impact delivering a pulse of momentum.

Recent and upcoming missions such as Proba-3 \cite{llorente2013proba3,zhukov2025proba3aspiics} and VISORS \cite{visors_formation_flying} demonstrate that millimeter-scale formation flying works. This suggests that many PuffSats can hold a tight formation with little relative spread. Most PuffSat navigation and control tasks can therefore be handled with robust model predictive control (MPC) \cite{visors_formation_flying} and Kalman filtering (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).  These classical techniques are data-efficient and interpretable, with formal performance and safety guarantees. For the more demanding conditions near low periapsis, classical control methods can be augmented with neural networks that warm-start the controller and learn from residual errors.  They also add resilience against lost PuffSats and other anomalies (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).  Formation flying would be paired with cooperative steering algorithms to guide the target in front of the PuffSat stream (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).

This speculative white paper is a brainstorming exploration that could be followed by more rigorous analysis. If the ideas prove too advanced for near-term implementation, they can instead be fodder for hard science fiction. The paper summarizes and updates some of the writings from my own blog, ``Aim Is All You Need.''\cite{aim2024}

\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}
Conceived in the 1950s and recently brought to a new audience by \textit{The Three-Body Problem} \cite{liu2014three}, Project Orion remains one of the most compelling rockets ever proposed. It is the only rocket built on mature technology that combines specific impulses comparable to those of electric propulsion with thrust levels on par with chemical rockets \cite{projorion}.   Orion would propel a spacecraft by directing hypervelocity plasma from nuclear explosions onto a pusher plate. Despite its theoretical promise, Orion faced insurmountable challenges related to political feasibility, radioactive fallout, and the impractical mass requirements stemming from the high minimum yield of nuclear explosions \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}. Even so, Orion validated the physics of hypervelocity pulsed propulsion.

Our approach replaces nuclear bombs with precisely aimed, hypervelocity gas puffs from specialized ``PuffSat'' microsatellites (see \autoref{sec:puffsat_design}) sequentially impacting a rocket's pusher plate.  These gas puffs can be scaled down to practical sizes.  Unlike nuclear explosions, small hypervelocity gas impacts could be efficiently contained within a pulsed propulsion chamber. With gravity assists and the Oberth effect \cite{oberth_effect}, hypervelocity PuffSat impacts could achieve energy densities and specific impulses well above those of chemical rockets.

Picture two rockets. The first rocket deploys a series of these fast-moving PuffSats. As shown in \autoref{fig:PuffSat_impact}, the deployed PuffSats follow precise paths to a shock-absorbing pusher plate (see \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}) on a separate target rocket. Each one gasifies and strikes the plate in sequence. These collisions deliver concentrated pulses of energy and momentum to the target vehicle.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Starship_Impact_ellipse.png}
    \caption{PuffSats from a first rocket (not shown) crash their gas puffs into a second target rocket, transferring momentum for propulsion. \cite{earth_image}}
    \label{fig:PuffSat_impact}
\end{figure}

Our goal is to stimulate fresh thinking and new research directions. At minimum, the concept suggests new uses of formation flying.

The sections that follow open with the business case (\autoref{sec:business_case}), then explain how PuffSats work (\autoref{sec:how_puffsats_work}) and assess the near-term architecture's feasibility (\autoref{sec:engineering}) before surveying applications. \autoref{tab:applications} previews the grand challenges each section takes on.

\section{To The Moon by Smart Gas Balloon:  The Business Case For PuffSat Pulsed Propulsion}\label{sec:business_case}

\subsection{Making Starship's Excess Capacity Useful To Satellite Customers} \label{sec:starship_safelaunch}

SpaceX's Starship may be the first fully reusable rocket. At scale, this could reduce space flight costs.   However, the rocket's payload capacity probably exceeds today's space industry needs. For example, \textit{The New York Times} reported:

\begin{quote}
Carissa Christensen, the chief executive of Bryce Tech, an analytics firm that tracks the launch market, says launching Starship frequently will be key to closing SpaceX's business case, but finding customers to fill the rocket's giant payload capacity will be challenging. 
``Starship's payload capacity is huge; it's very, very big, and there aren't that many commercial uses today for a rocket that big,'' she said.  ``Maybe it'll be so cheap that it makes sense to launch satellites on it if it's not full or near full.''  \cite{nyt_starship_size}
\end{quote}

Although many satellites might be too small to fill up Starship, they can still be very expensive to build and test. For an extreme example, the James Webb Space Telescope \cite{james_webb_space_telescope} cost \$9.7 billion \cite{jwst_cost} to build. Losing a payload that costly to a launch failure would be catastrophic, so reliability matters as much as raw capacity. McKinsey \& Company argues:

\begin{quote}
Safety and reliability will continue to be overarching concerns, suggesting excellent execution will be table stakes for a competitive launch company. \cite{mckinsey_reliability}
\end{quote}

Once rockets start ferrying astronauts, reliability becomes non-negotiable. Fully stacked, Starship weighs \SI{5}{\kilo\tonne} at launch \cite{starship}, yet its empty mass is only \SI{300}{\tonne}. That's an awfully thin soda can strapped to a \SI{5}{\kilo\tonne} bomb that we're entrusting with billion-dollar satellites or human passengers.

By contrast, a suborbital rocket that merely reaches \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} in altitude might have a propellant mass fraction under 60 percent \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That lower propellant fraction leaves a larger mass allowance for structure and payload-protection systems. Of course, such a suborbital rocket cannot reach orbit on its own.  It needs Starship to ``push'' it with PuffSat pulsed propulsion.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/new_push_picture.png}
    \caption{A fun and of course very technically accurate cartoon about a suborbital rocket's $\Delta V$ limitations}
    \label{fig:suborbital-cartoon}
\end{figure}

Starship launches PuffSats for pulsed propulsion into a highly eccentric orbit around Earth, with a deliberately low \SI{50}{\kilo\meter} perigee that deorbits spent hardware, and a high apogee (see \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}). These PuffSats move near Earth's escape velocity at their roughly \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude. The suborbital rocket rides the PuffSats'  momentum pulses to low Earth orbit. The PuffSat mass this takes is modest, comparable to the target rocket's own mass (see \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios}). While this approach reduces payload capacity compared to a single rocket system, most satellites are unable to take advantage of Starship's enormous payload volume anyway.

\subsection{The 200 Mile High Club} \label{sec:200_mile_high}
SpaceX proposes direct Earth-to-Earth travel \cite{earth_to_earth}, ballistically launching passengers directly between cities with Starship from offshore platforms. This idea faces several challenges:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Marine Environmental Impact:} Noise pollution from sea launches could disturb marine mammals. Launch noise and sonic booms at coastal ranges already trigger federal marine-mammal harassment findings for nearby seals and sea lions \cite{nmfs_vandenberg_2024}.
\item \textbf{Noise Over Populated Areas:} Any supersonic descent routed over a city would expose populated areas to sonic booms.
\item \textbf{Community Noise:} Musk names noise as the reason for going offshore and sets the distance himself, at ``\textasciitilde20 miles / 30km offshore for acceptable noise levels, especially for frequent daily flights, as would occur for point to point flights on Earth'' \cite{musk_offshore_noise, futurism_musk_offshore}. Measurement does not show that distance carries much margin. Far-field microphones between \SI{1.0}{\kilo\meter} and \SI{35.5}{\kilo\meter} recorded a maximum overall sound pressure level of \SI{145.7}{\decibel} at \SI{1}{\kilo\meter} and \SI{125.2}{\decibel} at \SI{10}{\kilo\meter} during Flight 5 \cite{gee2024starship, gee2025starship_acoustics}. The A-weighted sound exposure level at \SI{10}{\kilo\meter} is about \SI{115}{\dBA}, which the same authors equate to a thousand Boeing 777-300ER departures \cite{gee2025starship_acoustics}. The booster's flyback sonic boom averaged \SI{450}{\pascal} (\SI{9}{\psf}) at \SI{10}{\kilo\meter}. At \SI{20}{\kilo\meter} its perceived level was still 1.5 times the loudness of a Concorde boom \cite{gee2025starship_acoustics}. Residents near Starbase report shaking and cracked walls. Roughly 60 to 80 households in Port Isabel, Laguna Vista, and South Padre Island sued SpaceX in 2026. They allege structural damage from eleven test launches flown between April 2023 and October 2025 \cite{texas_tribune_spacex_suit}. The regulatory record is no more encouraging. In October 2024 the California Coastal Commission voted 6 to 4 against raising Falcon 9 cadence at Vandenberg from 36 to 50 flights per year. Its staff cited blast waves along the mainland coast. The federal government overrode the objection \cite{ccc_vandenberg_2024}. SpaceX's president projects dozens of Earth-to-Earth flights per day from a single vehicle \cite{shotwell_ted_2018}. That is two to three orders of magnitude above the annual cadence a state coastal regulator has already refused. Our own architecture is louder in total, not quieter. It burns about 2.5 times the launch capacity of direct Earth-to-Earth service (see \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}), so it radiates more acoustic energy per passenger. What differs is where that energy lands and when.
\item \textbf{Logistical Inefficiency:} Offshore launch platforms, by necessity located far from land, would add hours to the journey for boarding and departure, undermining the benefit of rapid travel. Federal rules restrict unauthorized aircraft within space-flight NOTAM areas, although air traffic control may authorize access \cite{cfr_91_143}. SpaceX's president projects ``dozens'' of point-to-point flights per day from a single vehicle \cite{shotwell_ted_2018}. At that cadence, with a reentry for every launch, repeated range-safety closures would likely make dependable helicopter service difficult to schedule. Every shuttle would have to fit around launch and reentry operations. Boats avoid that airspace conflict, but the transfer erodes Starship's door-to-door speed advantage.
\item \textbf{Salt-Water Corrosion:} Marine corrosion is a major engineering problem for offshore wind infrastructure \cite{wind_turbine_corrosion}. A sea-based Starship system would require its own corrosion-control, inspection, and maintenance program.
\item \textbf{Strong Offshore Winds:} Wind-driven fatigue is a major design concern for offshore structures in the North Sea \cite{north_sea_wind_fatigue}. Starship's approximately \SI{120}{\meter} height \cite{starship} makes wind loading an important design and operating constraint. Scheduled passenger service would have to absorb weather holds into its timetable rather than assume every departure can proceed.
\item \textbf{Range-Safety Sprawl:} Supporting dozens of daily departures and arrivals may require several pads, depending on turnaround time. NASA spaced Kennedy's pads 39A and 39B \SI{2.7}{\kilo\meter} apart for Saturn V \cite{nasa_sp29_1963}, whose entire \SI{2800}{\tonne} liftoff mass \cite{nasa_saturn_v_overview} was less than the roughly \SI{4500}{\tonne} of propellant carried by a fully fueled Starship stack \cite{gunter_starship}. If a high-cadence Starship complex required several pads with kilometer-scale separation, it would spread across many square kilometers of sea and widen the marine noise footprint.

\end{itemize}



A suborbital rocket plane is a more practical alternative. To reach orbit, the suborbital rocket requires propulsion PuffSats launched by Starship.   Starship can be launched from remote locations where noise pollution is acceptable.  This costs more launches overall than direct Earth-to-Earth service (see \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}), but they fly from a few dedicated spaceports on infrastructure schedules, because the formations are in orbit before any passenger boards. Earth-to-Earth service instead brings its full launch cadence, with the airspace closures and pad clusters above, to every city it serves.  Our suborbital rocket plane could take off from conventional urban airports using standard aircraft engines, then switch to rocket propulsion at high altitudes over remote areas to ``skip'' above the atmosphere and intercept Starship's propulsion PuffSats.

Every departure is an ordinary runway takeoff, so the loudest event over the city is a turbofan the airport already handles thousands of times a day. The rockets never fire below \SI{12}{\kilo\meter}. That removes the liftoff roar, which dominates the complaint record at both Starbase and Vandenberg. A pad burn puts full thrust at zero altitude over a hard reflecting surface. It radiates for tens of seconds while the vehicle is still close. Two Raptor engines on a \SI{200}{\tonne} plane give \SI{2.3}{g}, inside the acceleration limit of \autoref{sec:pregnant_women}. The empirical model that reproduces the Starship measurements to within \SI{0.5}{\decibel} \cite{gee2025starship_acoustics} puts that pair, fired at an ignition point \SI{150}{\kilo\meter} away, near \SI{91}{\decibel} at the shoreline. In A-weighted sound exposure the event is comparable to one wide-body departure. Lompoc sits \SI{15}{\kilo\meter} from Vandenberg's Falcon 9 pad and already absorbs about \SI{21}{\decibel} more than that.

The ascent boom is weak for a structural reason rather than a lucky one. The PuffSat formation supplies all of the horizontal velocity, so the plane climbs steeply and carries its weight on thrust instead of on wings. The lift term that makes a heavy airliner boom hard is therefore absent. In a steep climb the Mach cone also points outward, so much of the remaining energy refracts upward rather than reaching the ground. Falcon 9 ascent booms recorded across 132 ground stations in Ventura County, 150 to \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} downrange, peaked at \SI{1.90}{\psf} and mostly fell below \SI{0.5}{\psf} \cite{gee2026falcon9boom}. A \SI{200}{\tonne} plane in a steeper climb should sit at or under those numbers. The boom still travels \SI{150}{\kilo\meter}, so the claim is a Falcon 9 class boom, not the absence of one.

The standoff distance is what separates the two architectures. An offshore platform has to be the passenger terminal and the ignition point at once. Those two roles want opposite distances from shore, which is what fixes the compromise near \SI{30}{\kilo\meter}. Splitting the roles costs ten minutes of subsonic cruise and no new structure. Coastal cities fire their rockets offshore. Inland cities fire over rural terrain, which opens hubs no offshore platform can serve, Phoenix and Chicago among them.

Normal airports already handle kerosene, but most lack the infrastructure for cryogenic liquid oxygen.

However, two solutions address the liquid oxygen challenge:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Air-breathing Scramjets (Optimistic Scenario):} If economical passenger scramjets become feasible, rockets could accelerate to around Mach 7 \cite{nasa_x43a} before a steep climb, eliminating the need for liquid oxygen. This would also reduce propellant mass. Unfortunately, the extreme aerodynamic stress on a scramjet airframe, and its cost, keep plain suborbital rockets the better near-term bet.
\item \textbf{Mid-air Oxygen Refueling:} Suborbital rocket planes could receive liquid oxygen from specialized aircraft launched from airports equipped with liquid oxygen storage. This avoids retrofitting every urban airport, requiring only a single airport near major destinations.   Mid-air refueling also reduces our rocket plane's takeoff weight.  The concept has prior art. Burnside Clapp's Black Horse spaceplane studies chose hydrogen peroxide as the transfer oxidizer because it is not cryogenic, but judged in-flight liquid oxygen transfer workable \cite{clapp1995_propellant_transfer}. His later venture, Pioneer Rocketplane, patented a mid-air LOX transfer system \cite{clapp2000_lox_patent}, and an independent Indian study of the same class of vehicle concluded it requires only limited new technology \cite{jayan2004aptsp}. Most of the hardware is mature. A flexible LOX line is a convoluted stainless-steel bellows with a braided steel overwrap, a standard item in ground oxygen service rather than the rubber hose of military drogue refueling, and a rigid telescoping boom like the KC-135's works as well \cite{clapp2000_lox_patent}. Pumps carry over from the industrial gas and rocket industries. The part needing development is the coupling, a cryotolerant seal that holds at \SI{90}{\kelvin} under the relative motion of two aircraft and disconnects dry, because even the small spills routine in military fuel transfer are unacceptable with LOX. Transfer plumbing must be insulated or budgeted for boil-off, then chilled down before liquid flows. A heater at the coupling keeps atmospheric moisture from frosting the seal \cite{clapp2000_lox_patent}. Orbital transfer and storage of liquid oxygen is under parallel development for propellant depots \cite{nasa_loxsat_cfm}.
\end{itemize}

A formation of propulsion PuffSats launched in an eccentric Keplerian orbit pushes the rocket plane onto a ballistic arc. In a two-body model, that arc lies in a plane through Earth's center, and the plane intersects an ideal spherical Earth along a great circle. Any two points on the globe share a great circle, whose shorter arc is the geodesic between them \cite{docarmo_diffgeo,bowditch_navigator}. This establishes a geometric reference plane for any airport pair, not the exact Earth-fixed ground track. Earth's rotation during flight still enters the targeting solution.

The transit leg out to the ignition point is flown subsonically on air-breathing engines, so it carries no orbital-mechanical meaning and need not follow this great circle. The formation's plane must contain the ignition point and the destination, not the departure airport. That freedom matters because the great circle often heads inland. A plane leaving New York for Tokyo would otherwise light its engines over the Catskills, and one bound for London would light over central Connecticut. Turning out over the Atlantic first moves the ignition point to open water for about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter} of extra track. Chicago has Lake Michigan for its eastbound departures. Where the terrain is already rural the plane simply flies the great circle, and where a single town sits under it the standoff can be lengthened instead.

Earth rotation and the $J_2$ oblateness perturbation are deterministic inputs to launch targeting \cite{vallado_astrodynamics}. Connecting departure and arrival at a fixed flight time can be formulated as a two-point boundary-value problem, called Lambert's problem in the Keplerian limit \cite{battin_astrodynamics}. We design each formation to supply the nominal trajectory without a dedicated plane-change burn. The vehicle carries only a small terminal trim to absorb navigation error, supplied by onboard motors or a second PuffSat formation.

The launch targeting also fixes the rocket plane's mass in advance, and flight day may not match it. Passenger and cargo load varies from flight to flight. A formation sized for one mass will over-accelerate a lighter plane, and nothing already in orbit can add impulse for a heavier one. We therefore size each formation for the plane's maximum takeoff mass and trim the delivered impulse downward on lighter flights. Three trims work in sequence. The coarse trim withholds surplus PuffSats from the train; a withheld unit deorbits with a small burn from its own microthrusters or holds in orbit for a later flight. The fine trim detunes atomization timing, releasing a PuffSat's gas slightly early or late so the cloud reaches the plate in a less favorable shape and delivers a little less momentum. A preliminary single-code hydrodynamic analysis finds the delivered impulse falls off gently and without cliffs as the arriving cloud's shape departs from the design point \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The mapping from release timing to the arriving shape has not yet been simulated, so we treat this trim as a fine adjustment between whole PuffSats, not a large-authority knob. The last trim is onboard. The plane tops off fuel or ballast toward the design mass and pumps fuel between tanks so its center of mass sits where the plate geometry of \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick} assumes it, whatever the cabin loading. Concorde managed its balance the same way. Through the transonic transition it pumped about 18 tonnes of fuel between trim tanks to track the rearward shift of its center of pressure, the point where the wing's lift acts \cite{rech_leyman_concorde_2003, yoon_concorde_fuel_2004}. The retrograde deceleration formation below inherits the same trims with better information, since the plane's reentry mass is fixed once it takes off.

To avoid a sonic boom upon descent, the plane must decelerate before atmospheric reentry. This deceleration could be provided by another set of propulsion PuffSats traveling in a retrograde orbit relative to the plane. These reentry PuffSats could be in a circular low Earth orbit rather than a high eccentricity orbit, reducing the required mass.

\subsection{Splitting the Rocket Bill}\label{sec:fare_comparison}
SpaceX's proposed Earth-to-Earth Starship targets the same intercity market. Musk has suggested that a high-density point-to-point configuration could carry about 1000 passengers, all in coach seating, with no galley or lavatory \cite{musk_p2p_capacity}, at an estimated SpaceX operating cost of roughly \$2\,million per mission \cite{spacex_starship_cost}. Spread across 1000 seats, that gives an optimistic operating-cost floor of roughly \$2000 per seat before depreciation and ground operations. The rocket-plane architecture of \autoref{sec:200_mile_high} must therefore compete against this baseline.

Musk's capacity and cost assumptions are optimistic, but we adopt them throughout this section as a consistent comparison baseline. The reporting behind the \$2\,million estimate describes a roughly 100-passenger Starship configuration \cite{spacex_starship_cost}. At that capacity, the estimate becomes a \$20{,}000 operating cost per seat. Reaching the \$2000 per-seat operating-cost floor requires 1000 passengers. Under the assumed \SI{100}{\tonne} payload allocation, each passenger gets a total mass budget of \SI{100}{\kilo\gram}. That allowance must cover the passenger, seat, luggage, cabin furnishings, and life-support hardware. This is a demanding mass constraint.

The large dry mass of a reusable Starship favors an operating model that groups nearby flights to amortize it. Starship can be refueled in low Earth orbit and use its full propellant load to deliver a much larger PuffSat payload onto the required elliptical trajectory. The required PuffSat mass exceeds the payload of any single suborbital rocket, so multiple scheduled departures from nearby cities can instead share a common PuffSat formation. The constraint is geometric. Every vehicle sharing a formation must ride an arc that lies in the formation's orbital plane and crosses its perigee on schedule, so the sharing works for tight clusters of endpoints rather than arbitrary pairs. Only modest $\Delta V$ adjustments are needed at apogee, provided the cities are geographically close. For example, departures from New York and Washington bound for Tokyo, Shanghai, and Seoul could share a single formation.

We reuse the mass ratios from \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios} with a fudge factor of $f = 0.8$ to estimate the required PuffSat mass. The formation is first placed into an orbit with a \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} apogee, the value carried through the closed-loop simulation of \autoref{sec:puffsat_control_simulation}. Other apogees would likely work; we adopt this one as a concrete baseline. Raising apogee from low Earth orbit to that altitude requires about \SI{3}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, roughly one third of the velocity needed to reach orbit from the launch pad. The current Raptor Vacuum is reported near \SI{378}{\second} specific impulse, while SpaceX has targeted \SI{380}{\second} for a higher-expansion-ratio design \cite{raptor_vacuum_isp}. We adopt that target as a forward-looking baseline. Its exhaust velocity is approximately \SI{3.73}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the rocket equation gives a departure mass of $e^{\frac{3.0}{3.73}} \approx 2.24$ times the burnout mass. Equivalently, the dry ship and its PuffSat payload together may account for about 45\% of the departure mass.

Now consider the Starship stationed in low Earth orbit. Tanker flights refill its full \SI{1200}{\tonne} methalox capacity \cite{gunter_starship}, while cargo flights fill its payload bay with PuffSats. A full propellant load supports a burnout mass of $\frac{\SI{1200}{\tonne}}{2.24-1} \approx \SI{970}{\tonne}$. The stock Starship dry mass is approximately \SI{85}{\tonne} \cite{gunter_starship}; structural reinforcement and the pusher plate bring the dry ship to \SI{120}{\tonne}, leaving roughly \SI{850}{\tonne} available for PuffSats. This is more than eight times the \SI{100}{\tonne} payload that the same vehicle can deliver to low Earth orbit in a single ascent, the payoff of loading in orbit.

At the boost ratio of 1.28, an \SI{850}{\tonne} PuffSat payload accelerates approximately $1.28 \times \SI{850}{\tonne} \approx \SI{1090}{\tonne}$ of vehicle to low Earth orbit speed. Delivering the PuffSats requires roughly nine cargo launches, while filling the propellant tanks requires another twelve tanker flights. Counting the reinforced Ship's own ascent, that is about 22 Starship launches before departure. Deceleration is less demanding. The return PuffSats remain in retrograde low Earth orbit and therefore require no apogee-raising maneuver. Their head-on mass ratio of approximately 2.3 implies that $\frac{\SI{1090}{\tonne}}{2.3} \approx \SI{470}{\tonne}$ of PuffSats is sufficient to slow the returning fleet. Because retrograde launches forfeit Earth's rotational velocity, these five nominal Starship payloads become roughly six launches. Altogether, the transportation system requires about 28 Starship launches to move \SI{1090}{\tonne} of vehicle, or approximately 11{,}000 passengers if Starship's optimistic mass-per-passenger estimate is accepted.

By comparison, transporting the same \SI{1090}{\tonne} directly requires only about $\frac{\SI{1090}{\tonne}}{\SI{100}{\tonne}} \approx 11$ Starship Earth-to-Earth flights. PuffSat propulsion therefore consumes approximately $\frac{28}{11} \approx 2.5$ times as much launch capacity as direct Starship transportation, implying a ticket price near \$5000 under Musk's optimistic cost assumptions. The whole penalty is the orbital refueling campaign, an expense a direct suborbital flight never pays. Clustering flights keeps the formation full, which is what holds the ratio this low. Clustering cannot drive the ratio below one, because SpaceX spreads its own airframe over a full cabin too.

Even under these optimistic assumptions, such ticket prices are likely beyond what most travelers will pay. The expensive part is launching hundreds of tonnes of PuffSats from Earth, not the propulsion itself. We explore two ways out: producing PuffSats off Earth (\autoref{sec:isru}, \autoref{sec:greedy_phoebe}), and extremely high-velocity PuffSats that need neither giant reusable rockets nor ISRU (\autoref{sec:sorry_isru}). In either case, the \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} formation no longer depends on a fleet of refueled Starships.


\subsection{What to Expect When You're Expecting Bodily G-Force}\label{sec:pregnant_women}\cite{expecting_pregnancy_book}
U.S. Air Force policy bars pregnant aviators from high-performance aircraft and aircraft with ejection seats \cite{usaf_pregnant_aircrew}. The service treats acceleration, vibration, and high G as potential risk factors during pregnancy, while noting that definitive medical research is lacking \cite{usaf_pregnant_aircrew}. Suborbital transit, like military aviation, subjects passengers to high, sustained accelerations.  A safe way to carry these vulnerable passengers would help suborbital transit become mainstream.  The obvious fix is to reduce peak acceleration, but there are limits to this strategy.  Because of gravity losses, lower accelerations increase the $\Delta V$ (delta-v) required for a given trip.

One promising technique is to seat passengers so that forces act chest to back along the body's $Gx$ axis.   FAA guidance and pilot experience suggest this  force is well tolerated in healthy non-pregnant pilots \cite{lateral_acceleration}.  The FAA advisory also mentions that forces directed from the feet towards the head ($Gz-$) are the most dangerous for adults.   Babies are frequently oriented head down in the womb, so at Earth's 1 g they already endure a continuous force in the most dangerous direction, though fluid buoyancy ameliorates it. If a fetus tolerates the worst direction at 1 g, the tentative hypothesis is that it can tolerate 2 g in the $Gx$ direction adults find most benign.  Neck pillows and back supports can comfort infants and adult passengers.  While the rocket's pusher plate reduces vibrations, infants may also need seats designed to provide additional protection against shaking.

Unfortunately, I found no studies directly evaluating the safety of even moderate (2 g) $Gx$ acceleration for pregnant women or infants. We must validate safety protocols with multi-stage trials, beginning with mice and then non-human primates.  Because human infants' brains are larger and develop differently than those of non-human primates, only tests on human subjects can confirm that the acceleration mitigation procedures prevent harm.  If we can't establish that suborbital flight is safe for these groups, they must be excluded from suborbital flights.  

\section{How PuffSats Work}\label{sec:how_puffsats_work}

\subsection{PuffSat Design And Gas Generation Details}\label{sec:puffsat_design}
The optimal PuffSat design depends on the launch scenario and mission requirements.  Explosive PuffSats fit terrestrial launch, where their chemicals are easy to manufacture.  Ice and liquid-oxygen designs that follow use volatiles mined in space (see \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel} and \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}).
\subsubsection{Explosive PuffSats} \label{sec:explosive_puffsat}
Explosive PuffSats detonate at a precisely calibrated standoff distance from the pusher plate. This ensures rapid conversion of their mass into a high-velocity gas plume, shaped to distribute momentum evenly across the plate surface.  To minimize aerodynamic drag during flight, each PuffSat adopts an elongated cylindrical geometry aligned with the direction of travel (see \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}). This shape also extends the duration of gas-plume interaction, reducing peak stress on the pusher plate without compromising momentum transfer efficiency. A small shaped section of the PuffSat's explosive charge can also eject non-volatile solid components away from the rocket before impact, as described in \autoref{item:discard_before_impact}.  A failure to detonate at the intended moment could damage the pusher plate or the rocket, so redundant initiation systems are essential.

As shown in \autoref{fig:explosive_puffsat}, each PuffSat carries its own detonation system. As a backup to ensure reliable triggering, the rocket emits a sparse layer of volatile material (e.g., water ice) a few meters ahead of the pusher plate just before each PuffSat arrives. Passing through this ice layer produces a shock that reliably triggers detonation.  The flight explosive is chosen with a shock-initiation threshold the ice-layer impact comfortably exceeds. The geometry precludes a  PuffSat from reaching the rocket without first crashing through its ice layer. To minimize mass, the layers can be prefabricated as a sparse lattice or parallel wires. For instance, parallel ice wires with square \SI{500}{\micro\meter} cross sections  spaced \SI{5}{\centi\meter} apart have an areal density of only $\SI{20}{wires\per\meter} \times (\SI{500}{\micro\meter})^2 \times \SI{917}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} \approx \SI{5}{\gram\per\square\meter}$.  To improve handling and tensile strength during deployment, the ice wires can be coated with a micron-thin layer of metal or plastic. The coating needs to be light enough for orbital drag to deorbit it quickly, and thin enough that it doesn't pose a major collision hazard to other satellites. The coatings must also avoid toxic elements like fluorine, because they'll be vaporized upon impact.  

If frozen materials prove impractical, the wires could instead be constructed from low-mass plastic explosives, designed to detonate on PuffSat impact.  If the rocket misses a PuffSat interception after reaching  minimum orbital speed, the explosive wires would unfortunately become space debris. This debris could  persist for hours or days until eroded by atomic oxygen or slowed enough by atmospheric drag to re-enter. Another alternative is to replace ice wires with liquid sprays that induce a shock detonation of the PuffSat's explosives upon impact.

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Explosive PuffSat.png}
    \caption{Cross sectional diagram of the key parts of an explosive PuffSat}
    \label{fig:explosive_puffsat}
\end{figure}

This paper does not attempt to identify optimal explosive chemistries. That selection entails complex trade-offs in sensitivity, handling safety, cost, and environmental impact. These issues are better addressed in applied engineering work than in conceptual mission design. For modeling purposes in \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}, we adopt ANFO \cite{fabin2021anfo} as a shock-insensitive, deliberately unsuitable proxy. Its low density increases drag for a given PuffSat mass, helping ensure our performance estimates remain conservative while avoiding dual-use implications.   On a real PuffSat, a liquid emulsion is preferred because sensitizers can be added very late in flight to prevent accidents \cite{maranda2025comprehensive}. At apoapsis, deployment actuators could squeeze or knead the PuffSat bag to mix in the sensitizers, avoiding a dedicated pump. Until then, the emulsion would remain unsensitized and less susceptible to accidental initiation during launch. Primary detonators can be separated from the main charge until late in flight as well.  Recent advances in fluoro-substituted primaries reduce their sensitivity to accidental initiation while eliminating lead, which we wouldn't want in the atmosphere \cite{Yadav2025}. The bulk liquid can also tamp the initiation, confining the charge behind a thin membrane so the detonation stays coupled, without the heavy metal a conventional tamper would otherwise send into the pusher plate.

To prevent outgassing, the PuffSat's explosives are encased in very thin layers of high performance polymers or metals. These liners can also reflect sunlight, further reducing temperatures to minimize the escape of volatile materials. A standoff Mylar bumper (see \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}) shields the body from small micrometeorites, breaking up grains below about \SI{90}{\micro\meter} across a centimeter of vacuum so that a diffuse debris cloud reaches the explosive rather than a focused shock. This disperses the impact better than a cushioning foam and covers a wider size range. The thin film stays transparent to the larger, faster ice wire we use as a detonator backup, which punches through intact, so the shield does not defeat the detonator. Larger micrometeorite impacts simply result in PuffSat losses.

\subsubsection{Solid Ice Based PuffSats} \label{sec:icy_puffsat}
PuffSats filled with lunar ice (see \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}) do not primarily rely on chemical reactions to produce gas. Their high tensile skins absorb sunlight, melting the ice to water under mild pressure. Small explosives or an ice wire impactor (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}) rupture the skin to release the water. However, this release is too slow for complete vaporization before impact. Even if the water fully boiled, the steam expanding into vacuum would cool so rapidly that ice crystals would form.

Instead, the water is atomized into fine droplets, a process requiring much less energy than vaporization. The design goal is to deposit enough energy throughout the liquid to break it into droplets while keeping nearly all of the PuffSat mass as locally mined water. A water fraction near 99\% is a target for testing, not a result that follows from the geometry alone.

A microscopic emulsion is one candidate rather than a settled design. The energetic material can range from a fine emulsion-like dispersion to a sparse set of encapsulated charges on the order of centimeters. A fine dispersion spreads the impulse uniformly, but very small isolated inclusions release their energy poorly and demand a large mass of binder or capsule per kilogram of energetic material. Larger charges cut that encapsulation mass and drive stronger local shocks. The cost is coarser coverage, since a charge may overwork the water next to it while leaving large fragments in between. What decides the outcome is the largest surviving water fragment, not the radius at which a pressure wave is still detectable. Underwater charge-array experiments confirm that charge number and spacing reshape the combined shock and bubble fields \cite{hu2022_underwater_array}, though they do not fix the droplet distribution in the PuffSat geometry. Gas filled plastic bubbles in the water guarantee the shockwave produces uniform cavitation and sheer and that all atomized water droplets are small.  

The charges can ride on a scaffold of waterproof closed-cell foam. The scaffold is a plain \SI{1}{\milli\meter} monofilament of closed-cell polyethylene foam, extruded and spooled rather than cut to shape. Closed-cell polyethylene is cheap and takes up almost no water. At \SI{30}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} it is about \SI{97}{\percent} gas by volume \cite{rodriguez_perez2005_polyolefin_foams}. Threading such strands through \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} of water on a \SI{5}{\milli\meter} grid takes about \SI{4}{\kilo\meter} of filament and \SI{94}{\gram} of foam, which leaves the water fraction alone.

Extruding foamed polyethylene as a continuous millimeter-scale profile is ordinary industrial practice rather than a new process. Coaxial cable is made this way at production volume, with mean cell sizes in the tens to low hundreds of \si{\micro\meter} \cite{moradian2020_foamed_coax}. Cell size matters more here than density does. A cell has to be small against the \SI{1}{\milli\meter} section, or the strand is a few broken cell faces instead of a skin over a wall. What the cable case does not yet demonstrate is a free-standing filament at \SI{30}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter}, since the dielectric expands onto a conductor and stops at several times that density. Foam at \SI{30}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} is routine in backer rod, but at diameters of \SIrange{6}{50}{\milli\meter}. Holding both at once, a \SI{1}{\milli\meter} free strand at \SI{30}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter}, is a specification to be demonstrated rather than an item to be ordered. Density is the parameter to concede if it does not hold, because the mass budget below has room to spare and the acoustic argument does not depend on the exact value.

The foam also works on the water rather than only holding the charges. Its acoustic impedance is far below water's, so a charge shock reflects off each strand as tension, and water cavitates rather than carry tension \cite{cole1948underwater}. The unwetted strand surfaces hold gas in their crevices, the stabilized nuclei that cavitation grows from \cite{atchley1989crevice}. Those nuclei scale with wetted area, and a plain cylinder is the shape that maximizes it at a fixed section. Any string of beads of the same diameter must space them at more than one diameter of pitch, so it carries less surface per unit length than the cylinder it was cut from. The \SI{4}{\kilo\meter} of \SI{1}{\milli\meter} strand presents about \SI{12.6}{\square\meter} of foam surface to the water. Grid spacing then caps the largest surviving fragment by geometry instead of by an assumed atomization radius.

Gas voids in water are also the standard way to attenuate an underwater shock, so the scaffold has to be shown not to swallow the impulse it is meant to spread. What bounds that loss is the foam's capacity, not its stiffness. The \SI{94}{\gram} of foam occupies \SI{3.1}{\liter}. Crushing all of it at a plateau stress near \SI{100}{\kilo\pascal} through a densification strain of \num{0.9} absorbs about \SI{280}{\joule} \cite{gibson1997cellular}. Compressing every cell adiabatically from \SI{30}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} to solid polyethylene density is a generous upper bound and still reaches only about \SI{2.2}{\kilo\joule}. At a \SI{99}{\percent} water fraction the charge is \SI{1}{\kilo\gram} of energetic material carrying at least \SI{1}{\mega\joule}, so the foam takes at most about two parts in a thousand of it. Two further effects cut the loss below that bound. The strand is thin against the length of the pressure pulse, which stays several strand diameters long out to the neighboring charges \cite{cole1948underwater}, so the wave diffracts around it rather than stopping at it. A strand crushed to solid density also has an impedance near water's and transmits what reaches it, which ends the mismatch that started the collapse. What the foam does absorb goes into opening voids in the water, and those voids are the product rather than a loss.

The scaffold reaches the plate as debris in any case. Spread over the PuffSat's cross section, \SI{94}{\gram} of foam is about \SI{0.4}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} against roughly \SI{400}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} of water, and the foam is lighter and softer than the water it rides in, so it cannot set the impact load. A continuous strand also cannot hold the water together against the shock breaking it apart. Closed-cell polyethylene foam near this density carries a tensile strength of order \SI{200}{\kilo\pascal} \cite{gibson1997cellular}, so a \SI{1}{\milli\meter} strand parts at about \SI{0.16}{\newton}. Spread over the \SI{25}{\square\milli\meter} of water each strand serves, that is under \SI{10}{\kilo\pascal} working against a shock in the megapascal class, short of tethering a water fragment by two orders of magnitude. Notching the strand at intervals would force brittle fracture instead, since unnotched low-density polyethylene stays ductile down to about \SI{-94}{\celsius} while a shallow sharp notch raises that transition to near \SI{0}{\celsius} \cite{zewi1980ldpe}. That margin makes the notch unnecessary, and doing without one leaves the scaffold a plain extrusion with no cutting or forming step at all.

This is a low-energy use of the same broad material class as explosive emulsions used in terrestrial mining and construction \cite{emulsion_tutorial}. Unlike an industrial emulsion designed for bulk detonation, the PuffSat carries only enough energetic material to initiate breakup. Sensitizing or initiating components remain isolated during launch and transit and are brought into the active configuration late in flight. For design purposes, we assume that the energetic material is imported. Atomized water mass per kilogram of imported material is therefore the appropriate optimization metric.

Gas and surfactant can assist the final stages of breakup. After the ice has melted, carbon dioxide stored separately can be dissolved into the water shortly before interception. On rupture it comes out of solution, and the expanding bubbles stretch liquid sheets and ligaments through effervescent atomization \cite{sovani2001effervescent}. Carbon dioxide is preferred when the gas must be carried in solution because water dissolves about 50 times more of it than nitrogen at the same pressure near room temperature \cite{sander2023_henry}. Nitrogen remains a chemically inert fallback, but it is better introduced as a gas phase than used as a dissolved reservoir. Neither gas is expected to replace the primary energetic impulse.

Experiments show that a small surfactant addition can alter droplet coalescence behavior \cite{pan2016_surfactant_coalescence}. Tests must determine whether it shifts the PuffSat droplet-size distribution or limits recoalescence. Any benefit depends on dynamic surface tension during the short breakup event, rather than the equilibrium surface tension quoted for a settled mixture \cite{shavit1995_dynamic_surface_tension}. The surfactant must also survive freeze--melt segregation without attacking the skin or leaving unacceptable non-water residue. Freezing redistributes dissolved solutes and gases \cite{killawee1998_freezing}. Adding them after melting avoids assuming that their concentration remains uniform through the ice and after thawing.

Additional ice particles injected by the rocket ahead of the plume provide a final source of turbulent mixing and aerodynamic shear. The design combines this external breakup with distributed energetic impulses, effervescent expansion, and any measured surfactant benefit. Tests must determine how much each assist can reduce the energetic fraction while still preventing large water slugs from reaching the pusher plate.

Carbon dioxide is a low-cost assist only on carbon-rich icy bodies. Ceres may contain up to roughly 20 wt\% carbon at its surface \cite{marchi2019_ceres_carbon}, while Phoebe has trapped carbon dioxide ice \cite{phoebe}. The carbon-poor Moon does not offer the same low-cost supply.

\subsubsection{Liquid Oxygen PuffSats}\label{sec:lox_puffsat}
PuffSats filled with lunar oxygen (see \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}) must also atomize before reaching the pusher plate. To retain liquid oxygen (LOX) during orbital transit, these PuffSats require thicker skins engineered for elevated internal pressures, cryotolerance, and thermal insulation. To minimize boiling of the highly pressurized LOX, PuffSats use high-reflectivity coatings such as emerging ``solar white'' yttrium oxide composites \cite{nasa_solar_white_y2o3} (see \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}).  These coatings are backed by emissive layers that radiate residual heat into space, maintaining cryogenic temperatures without active cooling.

A detachable standoff sunshade is a second passive-cooling option, beside the conformal solar-white coating above. The James Webb Space Telescope flies one: five layers of aluminized Kapton, the two sun-facing layers also coated in doped silicon, cooling its cold side to about \SI{40}{\kelvin} with no active refrigeration. It holds that temperature at the Sun-Earth L2 point, just beyond Earth's night side at roughly \SI{1.01}{\astronomicalunit} from the Sun \cite{jwst_sunshield, gardner2023jwst_mission}. That is flight proof that a passive shade reaches deep cryogenic temperatures at Earth's distance. Webb's \SI{40}{\kelvin} cold side is below the required range for liquid oxygen. Oxygen freezes near \SI{54.4}{\kelvin} and has a normal boiling point near \SI{90.2}{\kelvin} at one atmosphere \cite{nist_webbook_oxygen}. A LOX shade can therefore run lighter, with fewer layers tuned to hold roughly \SIrange{60}{80}{\kelvin}.

The shade is jettisoned shortly before the PuffSat is atomized. A floppy multilayer cannot survive the collision, and it would scatter the gas plume. We do not keep it as reaction mass. A first-order estimate puts a \SIrange{1}{3}{\square\meter} shade at only \SIrange{0.2}{0.6}{\kilo\gram}, a few percent of a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat. Its aluminum and silicon metallization would also contaminate the non-combustible LOX pusher plate. That risk is worst at the cool \SI{3.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} lunar-return end, where the thin film may not fully vaporize on contact. The release timing is set by plume geometry, not by boiloff. Unshaded at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, the reflective PuffSat absorbs only \SIrange{10}{15}{\watt} and takes hours to warm by \SI{20}{\kelvin}, so a coast of seconds to minutes barely touches the LOX. The discarded Kapton has a high area-to-mass ratio and deorbits quickly, or it leaves on the dry-mass disposal path (see \autoref{item:discard_before_impact}).

Upon rupture, the thicker skin risks becoming hazardous shrapnel. While PuffSat skins are engineered to retain cryogenic LOX under elevated internal pressure, they need not be shock-resistant (though an outer protection against micron-scale micrometeorites is still required). Selecting materials prone to brittle fracture at low temperatures may enhance debris mitigation by ensuring the skin fragments on impact. The rocket releases a denser intercepting ice layer that shatters the brittle skin in a process analogous to how Whipple shielding breaks up space debris into less threatening dust \cite{whipple_shield}.

As with the icy PuffSats discussed in \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}, the liquid oxygen is atomized through a combination of micro-explosive detonation, aerodynamic shear, and shock interaction with the ice layer.  The pusher plate for liquid oxygen PuffSats must be lined with a non-combustible material. The foam scaffold of \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat} does not carry over either, because polyethylene is a fuel in liquid oxygen, and any nonmetal in a LOX system has to survive mechanical-impact ignition testing first \cite{nasa_oxygen_compatibility}. A LOX body needs an oxygen-compatible scaffold such as a fluoropolymer or a thin metal web, or it goes without one.

\subsubsection{Balloon PuffSats}
Inflated balloon PuffSats avoid the catastrophic risk of failed detonations.  When filled with gaseous water vapor or oxygen, balloon PuffSats can operate without advanced reflective cooling. Project Echo \cite{nasa_echo_ii_1966} demonstrated that large orbital balloons can be built from low-cost materials such as metalized mylar.

At low periapsis, maintaining balloon navigation control and structural integrity is difficult because of  atmospheric drag, debris impacts, and radiation pressure. Large balloon surface area also demands more polymer, increasing costs. If high performance polymer costs drop, balloon PuffSats could become a favorable option.

\subsubsection{Passive Louver Thermal Trim}\label{sec:louver_thermal_trim}

Every PuffSat variant needs some thermal trim, though the target differs: liquid water near \SIrange{273}{373}{\kelvin}, LOX under the shade of \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} near \SIrange{60}{80}{\kelvin}, a balloon's vapor fill warmer still. A movable louver adds fine control on top of a body's baseline reflective coating. Two passive, self-triggering actuator technologies are candidates. A bimetallic strip is the same spring that opened Pioneer 10 and 11's louvers with no power draw \cite{pioneer_anomaly_turyshev}, and that flew again decades later on Goddard's Dellingr CubeSat \cite{dellingr_louver}. A nitinol shape-memory wire can drive the flap instead, self-triggering the same way at a fraction of the mass. Such wire has been drawn down to single-digit micron diameters \cite{fwm_ultrafine_sma_yarn}, and SMA-driven mechanisms already fly at CubeSat scale, including NASA Glenn's ALBus deployment hinges \cite{nasa_glenn_sma_cubesat}. Either actuator senses the PuffSat's own temperature rather than the flap's sun-facing side, so a short pull cable separates actuator from flap instead of integrating them. Both the strip and the very thin wire keep actuator and flap light, the same low-mass logic behind the rest of the dry-mass budget (\autoref{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal}), so neither is a major shrapnel risk on its own. A spin-stabilized PuffSat can still reel the whole assembly clear of the plate before impact on its tension tether (\autoref{sec:spinning_tension_detail}), the same margin the dry-mass packages get. Thermal trim also stops during the final approach. Extended flaps add drag that perturbs the terminal trajectory at low altitude, so they retract or are shed early instead. Unshaded absorption stays near the \SIrange{10}{15}{\watt} already quoted for a reflective PuffSat (\autoref{sec:lox_puffsat}), which over the last ten minutes before impact raises a water body's temperature by a few tenths of a kelvin, negligible against the margin already banked.

\subsubsection{The Two-Shell Sphere: A Cheap Whipple Bumper That Also Balances Heat}\label{sec:spherical_water_thermal}

The louver of \autoref{sec:louver_thermal_trim} is the general fine-trim tool. A spherical PuffSat is the one variant that can drop even that, and it does so with two concentric shells that split the work. The inner shell is a polyethylene naphthalate (PEN) film that holds the water and any dissolved gas. The outer shell is a rigidized aluminized-Mylar balloon of the kind flown on Echo~II \cite{nasa_echo_ii_1966}, standing off about a centimeter. That outer film is an ultra-cheap Whipple bumper, a thin sacrificial sheet that shatters a micrometeorite before it reaches the wall behind it, and the same sheet carries the passive thermal control. One film of about \SI{20}{\gram\per\square\meter} shields the body, holds the shape, and sets the temperature, so the inner skin only has to be a barrier.

As a bumper the sheet works the small end of the flux. A grain below about \SI{90}{\micro\meter} is broken up by the film and sprays across the centimeter gap, so a diffuse cloud arrives at the inner skin rather than a solid grain. The standoff is about a hundred grain diameters, which spreads the debris over roughly a thousand times the grain's frontal area and dilutes the load the inner skin sees. The protection has a hard ceiling. A grain well above the fragmentation size punches a clean hole and passes through, and those impacts are PuffSat losses. Nesting two sheets at centimeter spacings buys more margin at trivial mass, each one re-shocking the debris cloud the sheet ahead of it made.

The outer sphere sets the temperature the same way for every PuffSat that carries one. A \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} water body forms a sphere of radius \SI{0.29}{\meter}, and a sphere presents the same projected area \(\pi r^2\) to the Sun at every attitude while radiating from its full surface \(4\pi r^2\). Its equilibrium temperature is therefore independent of orientation,
\[
T_\mathrm{eq}=\left[\frac{\alpha_s}{\varepsilon_\mathrm{IR}}\cdot\frac{S}{4\sigma}\right]^{1/4},
\]
where \(\alpha_s\) is the solar absorptance, \(\varepsilon_\mathrm{IR}\) the infrared emittance, \(S=\SI{1361}{\watt\per\square\meter}\) the solar flux at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, and \(\sigma\) the Stefan--Boltzmann constant. A flat plate or cylinder changes its projected area with Sun angle and swings in temperature. The ball does not, which is the orientation dependence the louver would otherwise have to correct.

Only the ratio \(\alpha_s/\varepsilon_\mathrm{IR}\) sets the temperature, and both terms are surface properties patterned from one laminate on the bumper. Where the aluminum faces out, the patch runs hot and barely radiates, near \(\alpha_s/\varepsilon_\mathrm{IR}\approx2.5\). Where the polymer faces out over an aluminum backing, a layout called a second-surface mirror, the patch radiates well, near \(\alpha_s/\varepsilon_\mathrm{IR}\approx0.2\) \cite{gilmore2002spacecraft}. Effective properties are area-weighted, so the aluminum-out fraction acts as the thermostat, on the order of \SI{90}{\percent} to sit near room temperature. This is the aluminum-polymer-aluminum laminate Echo~II already flew \cite{nasa_echo_ii_1966}, patterned as a checkerboard of high- and low-emittance patches the way standard passive balance already does \cite{gilmore2002spacecraft}. Putting the pattern on the outer shell also shields the water, which now sees neither the Sun nor cold space, only the bumper's inner face across the gap. The water floats in a cavity whose walls sit near the target, a milder place than an exposed skin. Because radiant exchange scales as \(T^4\) and the bumper's sunlit side runs far hotter than its shaded side, the water settles near the fourth-power-weighted mean of that inner face, not its arithmetic mean, so the aluminum-out fraction is tuned to the weighted mean rather than to an isothermal sphere.

The bumper pays for this with a steep gradient of its own. A thin film stores little heat and cannot carry it sideways, so each patch sits at its local balance. The sub-solar cap is the hottest, of order \SI{150}{\celsius} for an aluminum-dominated surface, above Mylar's \SI{78}{\celsius} glass transition, the temperature at which the film softens, but well under its \SI{250}{\celsius} melt. A rigidized balloon holds its shape from the yielded laminate, not from gas pressure, so a soft sunlit cap is tolerable, as it was on Echo. That is the case for the split. The layer that must not soften or leak, the inner PEN, sits in the cool cavity far below its \SI{120}{\celsius} glass transition, while the cheap Mylar takes the hot cap where only shape matters.

The passive sphere suits any body that tolerates a wide band near room temperature. Explosive and icy PuffSats both qualify. An icy body is meant to melt to liquid water during the coast, which lands it inside the \SIrange{273}{373}{\kelvin} liquid range; its surface runs a little more absorptive to supply the heat of melting and still needs no moving part. What decides the matter is the width of the tolerated band. Water's is wide and easy to hold. LOX must keep a tight window near \SIrange{60}{80}{\kelvin} (\autoref{sec:lox_puffsat}) that no fixed pattern can trim against eclipse and the drift of the sub-solar point, so LOX keeps the louver and a sunshade. The large thermal mass of a water body helps further. \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} of water stores about \SI{4.2e5}{\joule\per\kelvin}, so a \SI{35}{\minute} eclipse radiating near \SI{38}{\watt} cools it by only about \SI{0.2}{\kelvin}, and the \ang{15} shift of the sub-solar latitude over a \SI{15}{\day} coast is absorbed by the fixed pattern.

Spin keeps the water uniform inside the cavity. The assembly holds its spin axis perpendicular to the Sun-line, so the equator faces the Sun and each point on the outer bumper cycles through light and shade once per rotation, spreading the input around the shell. The same spin stirs the liquid. Centrifugal acceleration is \(\omega^2 s\), with \(s\) the distance from the axis, largest at the equator and zero on the axis, so warmer water floats toward the axis while denser cold water is thrown to the rim. Three numbers decide whether that churn runs and how fast. The Rayleigh number weighs buoyant driving against the damping that would smother it, so it answers whether convection starts at all, and for a modest spin it reaches order \(10^8\). Set against the onset near \num{1700} that a still fluid needs, the margin looks enormous. That reading overstates it, because the water rotates fast enough to organize its own flow. Rotation deflects every moving parcel sideways, the Coriolis force, and the Ekman number weighs viscosity against that deflection. At \SI{1}{\radian\per\second} it is near \num{7e-6}, so deflection wins and convection sets in as columns aligned with the spin axis rather than as free cells \cite{greenspan1968rotating}. Onset for those columns lies orders of magnitude above \num{1700}, leaving a margin of tens. The Rossby number, inertia against the same deflection, is near \num{0.02} and holds circulation well below the free-fall speed buoyancy alone would reach. Overturn is minutes rather than the tens of seconds a non-rotating reading gives, which is still short against a \SI{15}{\day} coast. Nothing here raises the threshold the way a buoyancy field parallel to the rotation axis would \cite{chandrasekhar1961hydrodynamic}, because \(\omega^2 s\) points away from that axis. These are scaling estimates, not a solved flow. The one exception is the spin poles, where the centrifugal drive vanishes and mixing is slowest, and where axis-aligned columns carry little flow along the axis either. Those are also the coldest points of the bumper, so they carry aluminum-out low-emittance caps that shed almost nothing, and the water beneath them stays warm on conduction from the bulk.

That geometry is specific to a body centered on the spin axis. A bag carried off-axis on a ring (\autoref{sec:tethered_ring}) sits far enough out that \(s\) varies by under a tenth across it, so its centrifugal field is nearly uniform and points outward. That field is fixed in the bag's own frame only while the bag co-rotates with the ring, which is what the spoke bridles of \autoref{sec:tethered_ring} are for. Its coldest points, the caps facing along the spin axis, then sit broadside to that field rather than where it vanishes. A sideways temperature difference sets fluid moving at any strength, with no onset value to clear. Rotation still slows that flow on the same Rossby-number grounds as above, so the mixing time runs to tens of minutes rather than seconds. The drive is meanwhile about 12 times the centered body's, because \(\omega^2 R\) at \SI{2}{\meter} beats \(\omega^2 s\) anywhere inside a \SI{0.29}{\meter} sphere. The ring bags therefore need no polar caps, and only the hub bag on the axis keeps them. The internal vapor space assists, carrying latent heat from warm patches to cold ones as a passive heat pipe.

The sphere is also the strongest shape against internal pressure, carrying a uniform membrane stress \(\sigma_\mathrm{hoop}=Pr/2t\) with no stress concentration \cite{young2011roark}. At \SI{290}{\kelvin} the saturation pressure of water is only \SI{1.9}{\kilo\pascal} \cite{nist_webbook_water}, which in a \SI{12}{\micro\meter} film gives \SI{23}{\mega\pascal}, about a tenth of the film's tensile strength. Vapor pressure is not a structural concern at this temperature. Any carbon dioxide dissolved for effervescent breakup (\autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}) sets the real design pressure, and the skin is sized to that rather than to the water vapor.

The two shells want different films. The inner barrier is aluminized PEN, a polyethylene naphthalate film. Its water-vapor barrier is three to four times that of PET and its modulus is higher, at a glass transition near \SI{120}{\celsius} \cite{macdonald2004engineered}. Barrier and strength are what the inner skin needs, since it holds water vapor and dissolved gas without outgassing, and it does that in the cool cavity. The outer bumper is aluminized Mylar, a polyethylene terephthalate (PET) film. It is the cheapest candidate and survives the \SI{15}{\day} ultraviolet dose, as Echo's years in orbit showed \cite{nasa_echo_ii_1966}, and its low \SI{78}{\celsius} glass transition does not matter on a rigidized shell that only holds its shape. Kapton would add a \SI{400}{\celsius} rating and the best radiation resistance \cite{sroog1991polyimides}, capability neither shell uses, at the highest film cost. Both films are mass-trivial, about \SI{20}{\gram} over \SI{1.05}{\square\meter} at \SI{12}{\micro\meter}, so the choice is film price per area against the PuffSat cost target, not mass.

An ISRU PuffSat built in the outer solar system may coast for years rather than the baseline fifteen days, so its exposed polymer can be stabilized against ultraviolet. Only the second-surface-mirror patches expose film to sunlight, and they must stay poor absorbers to keep radiating, so the stabilizer has to block ultraviolet without darkening the visible. A white titanium-dioxide screen does this and keeps the patch a good radiator, and pigmenting polyester film with it is routine, the same white PET used in photovoltaic backsheets. One caution earns the grade choice. Bare titanium dioxide is photocatalytic, making radicals under ultraviolet that attack the polymer around it, the mechanism behind chalking paint. The durable outdoor grades defeat this with a silica or alumina shell on each particle that quenches those radicals at the surface, so coated rutile protects the film instead of eating it. Carbon black is the cheaper broadband absorber but is black across the spectrum, fit only for a surface meant to run hot. Far from the Sun the solar ultraviolet flux is small, so on a deep cruise it is energetic-particle radiation, which no additive fixes, that sets the film's life.

A frozen ISRU PuffSat wants a compliant inner liner rather than a stiff barrier film. Water expands about a tenth on freezing and shrinks back on melting, which a stiff film resists and a stretchy one follows without tearing or going slack. Stretch and vapor-tightness pull against each other in any single polymer, since the chain mobility that lets a film stretch also lets vapor diffuse through it, so the two shells split the jobs. The rigid outer bumper holds the shape and carries the pressure, and the liner only takes up the freeze-thaw volume. Thermoplastic polyurethane fits that liner. It is the bladder material of inflatable spacecraft and pressure suits, tough and elastic, and its polyether grades resist the hydrolysis, the slow cleaving of the chain by water, that a polyester grade would suffer against a water load for years. A thin oxide or metallized layer on the liner recovers vapor-tightness where the cold coast alone does not.

\subsection{Comparison To Pellet Beam Propulsion Prior Art}\label{sec:pellet_prior_art}
In 2001, Gerald Nordley proposed an externally pulsed propulsion system in which self-steering hypervelocity particles transfer momentum to a spacecraft \cite{nordley2001interstellar}. This concept developed earlier work by Clifford Singer \cite{singer1979interstellar}, who envisioned unguided macroscopic projectiles launched by a long electromagnetic mass driver. A 1994 patent by Robert Willis sits between them \cite{willis1994momentum}. Willis proposed supplying a crewed Mars ship with ``smart'' guided projectiles thrown by a lunar mass driver. His ship tracks each incoming projectile, radios course corrections to it, catches it in an onboard electromagnetic launcher, and throws it back astern for a second push. The catch is electromagnetic rather than a plate impact, and the closing velocity comes from a mass driver rather than orbital mechanics. His target-side tracking and command loop still anticipates the guidance architecture of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}. Nordley and Crowl survey this family of mass-beam propulsion concepts \cite{nordley2015massbeam}.

Both designs require an external particle-launch system, but only Singer clearly specifies a mass driver. Nordley discusses accelerator concepts as well as photon-pressure alternatives. These systems would need to accelerate sizable objects to very high speeds, posing formidable engineering challenges. To address this, Jordan Kare introduced the SailBeam concept \cite{kare2001sailbeam}, which replaced the mass driver with solar or laser driven miniature sails. Kare further proposed vaporizing the sails upon impact and using a magnetic pusher plate to convert the resulting plasma into thrust.   Artur Davoyan received NASA funding to improve the efficiency of Kare's ideas by accelerating pellets using laser ablation rather than radiation pressure alone \cite{davoyan2023pelletbeam}.

A second branch of this family lays the reaction mass out in advance instead of beaming it. Whitmire and Jackson proposed seeding a ramjet's flight path with fuel from a space-based launcher \cite{whitmire_jackson1977laser_ramjet}, and Matloff sketched a runway of fuel pellets laid down by tanker craft over decades \cite{matloff1979runway}. Kare's ``fusion runway,'' presented at 1990s workshops but never formally published, added impact ignition. A ship already moving much faster than \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} drops its own pellet onto each runway pellet it passes, and the collision alone ignites fusion \cite{kare1997buzzbomb}. Greason revisited the concept in 2020, arguing that chip-scale spacecraft now make smart runway pellets attainable \cite{greason2020runway}. The runway branch is the nearest ancestor of our approach. It draws closing velocity from relative motion with pre-placed mass rather than from an accelerator, the same trade PuffSats make with orbital mechanics. Runways spend that velocity igniting fusion for interstellar flight, while PuffSat collisions stay non-nuclear and near-Earth (we return to impact-triggered fusion in \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}).

These pellet stream propulsion ideas often prioritized interstellar speeds over economic feasibility.  Each also rests on an accelerator that was never built. Singer and Willis assume mass drivers, Nordley requires externally accelerated particles, Kare and Davoyan assume large laser arrays, and a fusion runway serves only a ship already at ignition speed. That missing accelerator, not the impact physics, is where this family has stalled since the 1970s. Our PuffSat pulsed propulsion system replaces the accelerator with low cost orbital maneuvers, so the closing velocity is paid for by the same conventional rockets that lift the PuffSats. Two prerequisites remain, cheap bulk launch and precise terminal guidance, and both have flight heritage. Reusable heavy-lift rockets supply the first \cite{starship}, and \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions} reviews the second.  Unlike pellet beam proposals, PuffSat pulsed propulsion provides very heavy lift to propel large craft into orbit.  Those proposals also leave unexamined the near-term power sources needed to lift multi-ton objects into orbit. Our PuffSats can weigh kilograms, and are composed mostly of materials that gasify to biocompatible chemicals like water vapor, nitrogen, oxygen, and carbon dioxide.   This means they generate less pollution for a given $\Delta V$, which makes them practical for very large-scale near-Earth applications.

Although our PuffSats contain electronics, the majority of their mass consists of inexpensive explosives or ices. This composition enables comparable propulsion at lower cost than systems dominated by electronics or solar sail material. Water ice has been detected on the Moon, Ceres, and Phoebe \cite{colaprete2010lcross,prettyman2017dawn_ceres_water,phoebe}, making it a candidate PuffSat feedstock at several locations.  Larger PuffSats can afford heavier electronics than Nordley's snowflake-sized guided pellets.  Beamed-plasma proposals like MagBeam push on a spacecraft's magnetic field with plasma streamed from a stationary platform \cite{winglee_magbeam}. Landis analyzed the related case of a neutral particle beam pushing a magnetic sail for interstellar flight \cite{landis2004particlebeam}. He estimated roughly two million kilometers of thermal beam spread per light-year in one example, much less than laser diffraction, and concluded that particle beams could support interstellar probes. Guided masses like PuffSats replace passive beam propagation with active terminal guidance. While PuffSats could, in principle, interact with magnetic pusher plates, their largely gaseous composition (after gas generation) may make direct kinetic collision a simpler and more dependable mechanism for momentum transfer.  However, magnetic technologies would still be useful in the shock-absorber behind the pusher plate (see \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_damping}) or in magnetic nozzles (see \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}).  If a dense flow of small pellets hits a rocket, the resulting plumes may interfere with each other.   PuffSats have larger pulses spaced far enough apart that plume interference is negligible.  Work on pellet streams continues. In the newest variant, wind--pellet shear sailing, the craft overtakes slower pellets and pushes them backward against the interstellar medium, drawing power from the relative wind \cite{greason2022windpellet}.  PuffSats are not concerned with interstellar missions in the near term.

\subsection{Mass Fraction Of Rocket To PuffSat Mass}\label{sec:mass_fraction}
For PuffSat pulsed propulsion to be a viable option, the ratio of PuffSat gas mass to target rocket mass must be low for relevant rocket velocity changes. We develop a closed-form approximation for this ratio (\autoref{eq:PuffSat_ratio} in \autoref{sec:PuffSat_ratio_approximation}). Since real collisions are not perfectly elastic, the approximation includes an energy-loss ``fudge factor'' $f$. When all the gas strikes the plate, $f$ ranges from $0.5$ for a perfectly inelastic impact, where the gas sticks, up to $1$ for a perfectly elastic one, where it rebounds and pushes the plate about twice as hard. If some of the gas misses the plate, $f$ drops below $0.5$.

Project Orion established that opacity and radiation transport matter in pulsed-propulsion pusher interactions \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}. Kramers' law provides a first-pass opacity scaling with density and temperature in its applicable ionized regime \cite{hansen_kawaler_trimble_stellar_interiors,kippenhahn_weigert_weiss_stellar,pols2011stellar}. An opaque plasma reabsorbs its own thermal radiation instead of letting it escape into the plate as heat, so less kinetic energy is lost and the bounce stays closer to elastic. Whether PuffSat impacts reach that regime depends on composition, partial ionization, radiation transport, and wall coupling, and the preliminary single-code hydrodynamic analysis cited below models those losses directly.

Two modeled design choices can raise $f$. A replaceable ablative layer is intended to protect the plate; in the current model it gives only a small rebound improvement, driven more by injected mass than by opacity shielding. A shallow concave plate redirects more of the rebound momentum along the axis. Preliminary single-code hydrodynamic analysis finds that the best survivable $f$, the highest rebound factor the plate reaches without failing, ranges from about $0.77$ to $0.82$ across impact speeds of \SIrange{3.2}{16}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. A pessimistic case that freezes the gas chemistry, so no energy is recovered as the plasma recombines, falls lower, and an independent hydrocode cross-check remains outstanding. The mission scenarios remain viable at more conservative values. A third lever acts on the arriving gas rather than on the plate, since the same simulation finds the best coupling when the gas reaches the plate as a flat disk. Splitting one PuffSat into a tethered ring of smaller bags aims at that shape with less expansion, and so with less spill past the rim, at the cost of a configuration the sweep has not yet covered (see \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}).

Relevant mass ratios and mission scenarios are summarized in \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios}, where several rows preview missions developed in later sections. The table shows PuffSat pulsed propulsion can lift tens of tonnes to orbit. For instance, if a reusable rocket like SpaceX Starship \cite{starship} lifts \SI{25}{\tonne} of PuffSats into a trans-lunar orbit, they can propel a  \SI{32}{\tonne} target craft into low Earth orbit. This exceeds both the Space Shuttle's maximum payload capacity \cite{nasa_wings_in_orbit} and the zero-fuel mass of a smaller regional jet like the Embraer E170 \cite{embraer_e170}.

\section{Engineering Feasibility of the Near-Term PuffSat Architecture}\label{sec:engineering}



\subsection{PuffSat Formation Challenges Compared To Current Missions}\label{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}
Precise formation flying is rapidly advancing. The European Space Agency's (ESA) Proba-3 mission recently demonstrated millimeter-scale formation flying, creating artificial solar eclipses with a precise two-satellite formation \cite{llorente2013proba3,zhukov2025proba3aspiics}. The Stanford Space Rendezvous Laboratory's VISORS mission is designed to demonstrate centimeter-precision formation flying with two 6U CubeSats for \SI{10}{\second} durations at a \SI{600}{\kilo\meter} altitude \cite{guffanti2023autonomous,visors_formation_flying}.

Two lessons from these missions carry over to PuffSats, and one hard problem is left untouched. Proba-3 shows that two spacecraft can hold a tight relative geometry, while VISORS extends similar sensing and control methods to a two-CubeSat design validated through simulation. These are relevant precedents, but neither mission demonstrates a many-satellite formation. Both matter here because the PuffSats must arrive bunched in a small cluster near the plate. What these missions do not demonstrate is the interception. Proba-3 and VISORS hold a near-fixed geometry at relative speeds of centimeters per second, while each PuffSat closes on the target near \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Landing one body on another at that closing speed is a hit-to-kill problem, driving an interceptor onto its target rather than parking beside it, and it sits closer to kinetic missile defense (see \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}) than to station-keeping. The formation reasoning here inherits the first two lessons.  The hit-to-kill problem is solved separately, by the terminal guidance and off-board tracking of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}.

Our hypervelocity PuffSats employ formation-flying algorithms for precise positioning, but with a greater margin for error than VISORS or Proba-3.
