Because the package arrives barely above escape, only that tail, chiefly near-surface spall released with little shock, can clear lunar escape at all, so the fraction that leaves the Moon stays small. Repeated impacts do not build a lasting debris ring around the Moon. In the ideal two-body case, ejecta thrown from the surface follows an orbit that passes back through its own launch point, so a fragment either falls back or leaves the Moon outright rather than settling into a bound orbit. Real perturbations soften that rule. The Moon's lumpy gravity, collisions inside a dense ejecta plume, and the tides of Earth and the Sun can each change a fragment's velocity after launch and divert a small fraction of debris into temporary lunar orbits. Those orbits are themselves unstable, so the same perturbations soon return most of that debris to the surface or fling it away, and little accumulates. The steady meteoroid-fed dust cloud that LADEE mapped is grains in mid-hop, replenished and reabsorbed, not an accumulating population \cite{horanyi2015dustcloud}. The cost is aim. A \SI{1737}{\kilo\meter}-radius target moving at \SI{1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} demands tighter timing than Earth's atmosphere, but that is a guidance burden for the microthrusters and navigation the package already carries, not an extra $\Delta V$.

The picture above, where little ejecta escapes and nothing accumulates, holds at modest cadence. Scaled to the billions of disposals a year a mature PuffSat fleet implies, even the small per-impact ejecta adds up, and the far-falling fraction becomes a standing hazard to lunar settlements and their traffic. We would therefore start by letting packages strike bare regolith as above, and add ejecta control only as the rate climbs toward that regime. At that point we aim every package at one of a few fixed disposal sites rather than scattering hits across the surface. A single site is impractical because the Moon rotates, so the surface point that lines up for a low-$\Delta V$ approach changes over its month-long cycle. A handful of sites spread in longitude keeps one near the favorable geometry at any given time. Concentration turns a diffuse, drifting hazard into a few fixed keep-out zones that lunar traffic can route around. It also lets each site absorb its own ejecta in a trap. Ejecta leaves the impact at every launch angle, and two parts of it threaten lunar traffic. The low-angle debris is the fraction most able to enter a bound lunar orbit, and a rim catches it, stopping anything launched below $\arctan(H/R)$, for rim height $H$ and mouth radius $R$, before it clears the mouth. The near-vertical debris defeats a rim. Sub-escape fragments near \SI{1.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} clear the mouth, follow a high ballistic arc that keeps them aloft for tens of minutes, and can land a thousand kilometers from the site. That hazard is transient rather than a standing cloud, but it falls unpredictably far, so no fixed keep-out zone contains it. Catching it takes a trap that roofs the impact rather than merely ringing it.

There are two candidate ways to get that roof, and the choice is enclosed against open, not built against natural. Loose regolith relaxes to an angle of repose near \SI{35}{\degree} \cite{mckay1991lunar}, so a hole simply dug into it settles into a wide open bowl that still passes the near-vertical plume. A purpose-built trap could instead bond or sinter the regolith to form steep walls and a roof with a small entry aperture. A lunar lava tube, an underground void left by drained basalt, offers the same roofed geometry without building the main cavity, though its walls hold a volcanic record worth surveying before it is filled. Observations establish the existence of lunar pits and candidate intact tubes \cite{wagner2014pits,kaku2017lavatubes}. They do not establish that a tube, or a sintered-regolith enclosure, can survive repeated impacts.

Either enclosure places the impact beneath a roof, so most direct ejecta trajectories meet the floor, walls, or ceiling instead of open sky, and the small aperture subtends only a narrow cone from the impact point. The geometry cuts direct escape but does not close it. Ejecta striking the enclosure throws off secondary fragments, spall, dust, and vapor, and part of that finds the aperture. Repeated impacts erode the interior, work the roof toward fatigue or fracture, and fill the cavity with delivered metal and fallen debris. Two measures at the package can slow that erosion and cut the secondaries that reach the aperture. Impact energy scales with the square of arrival speed, so a modest terminal burn buys a large reduction. Shedding the \SI{2.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} arrival to \SI{1.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} costs about \SI{1.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of $\Delta V$ yet drops the deposited energy to a quarter, and a lower-energy hit throws slower secondaries that are less able to clear the aperture. A burn that misfires is contained, because the package still lands inside the trap rather than on open regolith. A cheaper variant fragments the package into a spray just before it strikes, using a small dispersal charge of the kind the fleet already flies for the tether cut. That spreads the energy over a wider, shallower footprint and lowers the peak shock that drives cratering and spall. The burn removes energy from the cavity as exhaust, while the dispersal only spreads it, so under either measure the delivered metal still arrives and the cavity still fills. A viable site therefore needs an impact and thermal model, then testing, before anyone claims a capture efficiency or a service life. That analysis has to cover missed approaches that strike near the aperture, and the site will likely need a sacrificial impact bed, roof and wall standoff, material removal, and periodic resurfacing. The removal rate is undemanding. Even billions of disposals a year deposit only thousands of tonnes a day fleet-wide, a few hundred per site, which is a modest continuous-excavation load by terrestrial mining standards. The harder part is doing it in low gravity and around the roof: a blade dozer loses traction at one-sixth $g$, so clearing likely uses a bucket-drum excavator of the IPEx class already proposed for regolith mining (see \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}), and the enclosure needs an access route separate from the impact aperture. Until then the enclosure is a candidate for ejecta control, not a demonstrated solution. At high cadence the deposited energy could also supply useful heat, but any such power system waits on the same thermal and structural analysis.

A final route keeps the dry mass instead of destroying it. Each package is refined metal already lifted out of a gravity well, and the lunar trap above ends by burying that metal and then digging it back out. Parking the packages in cislunar space leaves them where the material can be used. This is a salvage route rather than a disposal route, and the difference matters at fleet scale. Billions of disposals a year shed thousands of tonnes a day, past what any depot could absorb. The Lagrange option therefore takes the fraction whose materials are wanted, and the Moon takes the rest.

The cost is a second burn. Lunar impact needs only the retrograde burn that drops perigee to lunar distance, because there the arrival speed is the point. Parking has to null that arrival speed instead. A package falling from the \SI{900000}{\kilo\meter} turnaround to the Earth-Moon L1 point at \SI{326000}{\kilo\meter} arrives near \SI{1.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, while the point itself moves at \SI{0.87}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Insertion then costs roughly \SI{0.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on top of a \SI{0.46}{\kilo\meter\per\second} perigee burn. That is about twice the \SI{0.43}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the impact route, though still under the \SI{0.86}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of reentry. Those are coplanar two-body figures that ignore the Moon's own gravity. A lunar gravity assist would cut the insertion burn, as would a weak-stability-boundary approach, which coasts in along the edge of the Moon's gravitational reach where capture is nearly free. Both cost months of transit and tighter targeting.

Stability picks the destination, and it rules out the nearest points. L1 and L2 are saddle points, stable along one axis and unstable along another, so an inert ball left at either drifts away within days unless something holds it in place. Earth-Moon L4 and L5 are the stable pair, because the Earth-Moon mass ratio of \num{0.0121} falls below the \num{0.0385} threshold for linear stability at those two points \cite{vallado_astrodynamics}. They also sit at the Moon's own \SI{384400}{\kilo\meter}, so arrival costs the same perigee burn as an impact plus about \SI{0.18}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to insert. Solar perturbation still erodes that stability over long spans, so a site survey has to precede any accumulation. Sun-Earth L4 and L5 are stable as well, but they lie \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} from Earth, nowhere near the cislunar industry that would draw on the metal. Reaching them also means drifting \SI{60}{\degree} along Earth's orbit, which trades time against $\Delta V$: about \SI{165}{\meter\per\second} for a ten-year drift, or \SI{33}{\meter\per\second} for fifty.

Consolidating the arriving packages into one body is harder than it sounds. Cold welding in vacuum is real, but it is weak under the static contact a gentle rendezvous produces. The ESA tests discussed in \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates} measured under \SI{0.1}{\newton} of adhesion between metal pairs held statically. That rose to \SIrange{9.5}{19}{\newton} only under fretting, the small repeated sliding that breaks surface oxides in vacuum \cite{merstallinger_cold_welding}. Sub-newton adhesion will keep parts stuck once they touch. It will not build a block that holds together under handling. A depot has to supply the joining itself, by bagging the pile or sintering it with concentrated sunlight. Until it does, the parked mass is a loose cluster, a single object to track but not yet usable stock.

The Jupiter cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} needs none of this machinery, because its PuffSats arrive on heliocentric orbits and their dry mass stays there, spread through a volume roughly fourteen orders of magnitude larger than the shell satellites occupy. Even at billions of discarded packages a year, that leaves a large spacecraft below one expected collision per billion years. If that is unacceptable, each package already crosses Jupiter's orbit twice per revolution, so a few meters per second applied at perihelion walks it into the planet over a few centuries.

Since PuffSats form large formations, care must also be taken to avoid existing satellites. Short, seconds-long gaps in the formation could be intentionally introduced to avoid collisions.

\subsection{PuffSat Deployment Details For Low Earth Orbit Scenario}\label{sec:leo_orbit_details}
Let's consider the complexities of launching target rockets into low Earth orbit with propulsion from PuffSats deployed in highly eccentric Earth orbits. A purely low, circular orbit is not an option for them. Atmospheric drag at the roughly \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude would decay such an orbit within days. Each PuffSat instead rides a highly eccentric ellipse that only dips briefly through the dense low-altitude band, with a \SI{50}{\kilo\meter} perigee that disposes of any miss (see \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}).

These PuffSats will not follow strictly identical Keplerian orbits. As the target rocket accelerates, the optimal PuffSat interception point moves along with it, so that successive PuffSats have slightly different orbital elements.
For low-mass PuffSats, solar radiation pressure will distort their orbits. To simplify orbit planning and maintain consistent effects across the fleet, it's ideal for each PuffSat to remain outside the shadow of the others. This may necessitate slight variations in orbital parameters, such as inclination, for nearby PuffSats. The microthrusters on each PuffSat make corrections for unmodeled perturbations. We still ensure each PuffSat achieves the correct interception position with the target rocket. 

The eccentric orbit carries each PuffSat through the Van Allen radiation belts \cite{li2019vanallen} on its inbound leg, so the electronics face only a single pass of dose, not a repeated orbit. The PuffSat's own propellant mass shields the avionics buried inside it. The outer belt is dominated by energetic electrons, which modest material stops. The inner belt holds high-energy protons that are harder to shield, but one fast transit keeps the accumulated dose low enough to accept. Where more margin is needed, triple-modular redundancy, running three copies of the electronics and taking a majority vote, tolerates occasional radiation upsets with commercial-grade parts rather than fully radiation-hardened ones. SpaceX's Dragon flies this way and rode out the loss of one of its three flight computers to a radiation hit \cite{aviationweek2012dragon}.

Deploying PuffSats near apoapsis minimizes the $\Delta V$ required for both inclination changes and optimal satellite spacing. This reduction in required thrust suggests that mechanical launchers with moderate size, power, and peak acceleration could deploy PuffSats very precisely with sufficient velocity changes. The launch mechanism could use rotating wheels or computer-controlled rail catapults, with recoil forces countered by the rocket's reaction control systems. PuffSats can be launched in series, further reducing launch mechanism operational demands.

\subsection{Lightweight Pusher Plate Design Beyond Orion}\label{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}
Modern materials allow pusher plates far lighter than those Project Orion contemplated. Although individual PuffSat pulses may reach peak shock pressures comparable to Orion's nuclear impacts, their total impulse is orders of magnitude lower. This lower total impulse permits thinner structural elements, just as rifle barrels withstand high pressures with far less mass than \SI{155}{\milli\meter} artillery barrels \cite{cip_308win, ohara1999_155mm_breech}. Unlike Orion, PuffSat plates don't need to shield crew from nuclear radiation. The absence of bomb radiation also extends longevity for pusher plate materials like complex aramid polymers.

Kevlar liners, unavailable during Orion's development, offer lightweight spall protection against impact fragmentation \cite{aramid_ballistic_review}. Titanium alloys, used in artillery that operates under repeated firing shocks such as the M777 Howitzer \cite{titanium_m777}, are viable structural plate substrates. Fracture- and corrosion-resistant silicon nitride liners, fabricated as in-situ-rifled gun barrel liners for high-temperature erosion service \cite{si3n4_gun_liner}, could serve as a protective interface in PuffSat pushers. Silicon nitride liners mitigate chemical corrosion across different PuffSat gas chemistries, including pure, superheated oxygen (see \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat}). Temperature-resistant superalloys like Inconel 718 \cite{inconel_718_strong} can supplement or replace titanium where heat or cost justify the additional weight penalty.

An alternative architecture draws inspiration from Project Medusa \cite{solem_medusa}, a conceptual successor to Orion. Medusa replaced Orion's rigid plates with flexible, parachute-like tensile sails. Scaled-down, high-tensile fabric sheets could absorb PuffSat impulses with reduced mass and simplified deployment above the atmosphere. Since PuffSats approach from the rear, these flexible pushers would be mounted behind the rocket on shock-absorbing struts. The struts could be thermally coated, reinforced carbon fiber with titanium or superalloy joints. Carbon fiber offers high strength-to-weight and thermal stability. The metal alloy joints prevent fracture at peak shock points.

A sail and a rigid plate sit at two ends of one tradeoff. The sail absorbs each pulse over a long stroke with a light membrane, while a rigid plate takes the same pulse over a short stroke and carries more mass. Either can be shaped concave on its gas-facing side to wrap the divergent puff and collimate the rebound forward, the same curvature that raises the effective $f$ (see \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}).

Mounting the sail behind the rocket carries a structural cost. Real Medusa flew its sail ahead of the payload with the blast in the gap between them, so every connecting member was in tension and could be a long, light cable \cite{solem_medusa}. Our sail rides behind the rocket, where the gas must overtake the craft and strike the sail's rear face. So those members are in compression instead, and compression members buckle. The Euler buckling load falls as the square of the member's length, which makes a long compression strut a far harder problem than a long tether. The penalty is paid down, not removed. A compression boom stays buildable if it is braced like a sailboat mast by guy wires in tension, and telescoped so its peak load falls at its shortest length.

Keeping the sail behind is a deliberate fail-safe. Adding forward momentum with external reaction mass forces each PuffSat to approach from the rear and overtake the craft. A front-mounted tension sail, the true-Medusa arrangement, would route every hypervelocity PuffSat past the crew cabin. A rear sail shields the crew instead. If its silhouette is at least as large as the craft, any PuffSat that misses the sail also clears the craft, and overshoots or duds fly off into empty space behind. The compression and buckling penalty is the price of keeping people out of the firing line. Uncrewed cargo can instead use a front-mounted tension sail, with its light cables and no buckling, accepting that a rare strike costs a vehicle rather than lives. A front sail split symmetrically to the sides narrows that gap. Built paired or annular so the thrust stays on axis, it routes PuffSats up the flanks and keeps even a crewed cabin off the centerline. That layout is only partially fail-safe, since a mis-aimed solid can still clip a flank rather than flying off behind, and it costs harder off-axis aiming.

In contrast to Medusa's massive nuclear parachutes, PuffSat-compatible sheets can be thicker yet remain flexible, tailored to smaller impulse magnitudes. A practical implementation weaves the sail itself from continuous ceramic-fiber cloth. Suitable yarns include 3M Nextel 720 alumina--mullite \cite{nextel_720_fiber} and Hi-Nicalon Type S silicon carbide. Both retain useful tensile strength and remain drapeable above \SI{1200}{\celsius} \cite{nextel_720_hot_tensile, hi_nicalon_sic}.

The woven fabric would also need an environmental barrier coating. Existing EBC systems identify candidate material families \cite{ebc_review}, but coating thickness and a deposition process compatible with flexible woven cloth require development. Silicon carbide or silicon nitride layers \cite{silicon_ceramic_oxidation} form a self-healing silica scale in oxygen-rich plasma. Hexagonal boron nitride is the most compliant interphase available for ceramic composites \cite{uhtcmc_review}. However, it volatilizes as B\textsubscript{2}O\textsubscript{3} above $\sim$\SI{1200}{\celsius} in oxidizing atmospheres \cite{bn_oxidation}, so it is reserved for inert-gas PuffSat chemistries. A compliant high-temperature silicone overlay \cite{silicon_coatings} can serve as a secondary sealant on cooler regions of the sail. That limit belongs to the sail, whose hot face must hold membrane tension while incandescent. A rigid plate carries its load in the structure behind its facing, so silicone is admissible on a plate's hot face even where it is barred from a sail's.

The coated cloth is only the outer ply. The whole sail can be built as a stitched laminate that splits the thermal and structural work between materials, much as NASA's Hypersonic Inflatable Aerodynamic Decelerator builds its flexible heat shield \cite{hiad_flexible_tps}. That design lays a high-temperature reinforcing fabric of Nextel or Nicalon yarn over one or more layers of porous insulation, such as Pyrogel or Saffil felt, backed by a thin inner gas barrier, with the plies stitched together rather than rigidly bonded. A PuffSat sail can adopt the same stack. The coated ceramic cloth is the plasma-facing ply. A ceramic felt batting sits behind it. The innermost ply is a polymer tensile net that carries the integrated impulse load back to the struts and adds the fold endurance and shock toughness that brittle ceramic yarn lacks. Vectran, a liquid-crystal polymer fiber, suits this role. It holds tension without creep, so the sail stays taut across many pulses, and it tolerates higher temperatures than polyethylene \cite{vectran_brochure}. Stitching the plies, rather than bonding them face to face, also avoids delamination from the large mismatch in thermal expansion between ceramic and polymer. A brief, widely spaced pulse cannot drive heat through the felt before radiation sheds it, so the back ply runs far cooler than the incandescent face. The polymer sets the temperature ceiling, and the candidates form a ladder from Dyneema through Vectran and aramid to coated PBO, which holds usefully high and decomposes only near \SI{650}{\celsius} \cite{zylon_technical_info} (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). The felt must hold the back ply below the chosen fiber's limit across the whole pulse train. The sail's radiative equilibrium temperature, fixed by pulse energy, areal density, emissivity, and pulse spacing, decides how far up that ladder the net must climb. Each step toward PBO trades away shock toughness, so the net climbs no higher than the heat forces.

This layered sail differs from a reentry heat shield in the way that drives the material choice. Starship seals the gaps between its silica tiles with a wrapped felt gasket, the ``crunch wrap,'' replacing the loose gap fillers carried over from the Shuttle \cite{starship_crunchwrap}. Those tiles, and the bare stainless steel of the leeward side, only have to insulate and resist oxidation. The steel hull behind them stays cool and carries every structural load, so the tiles themselves bear almost no tension \cite{starship}. A Medusa sail has no cool rigid hull in the hot zone. Its plasma-facing ply must hold membrane tension while incandescent, carrying each pulse across the short unsupported spans between stitch lines and into the net below. That is why the hot face needs Nextel 720 or Hi-Nicalon Type S, yarns that keep tensile strength above \SI{1200}{\celsius} \cite{nextel_720_hot_tensile, hi_nicalon_sic}, not a silica tile chosen only to insulate.

The sail's organic parts must survive the orbital environment as well as the plasma. The carbon-fiber struts and the polymer net both face atomic oxygen and ultraviolet in low orbit \cite{ao_protection_banks}, so both carry the same metallized or silica barrier described in \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}. That one low-outgassing layer reflects the ultraviolet and seals out the atomic oxygen, and behind it the vacuum keeps the fiber dry.

The metallized net carries the self-healing overlay that \autoref{sec:fiber_selection} describes, since micrometeoroid and thermal-cycling defects would otherwise let atomic oxygen undercut the fiber. The sail's ceramic plies need no overlay. They already reglaze themselves with a silica scale in oxygen-rich plasma, and their oxide and silicon-carbide chemistries resist atomic oxygen and ultraviolet without outgassing. The carbon-fiber struts still need a space-qualified resin so the matrix does not shed volatiles in vacuum.

Project Orion proposed that a sacrificial ablative oil, resprayed onto the pusher's skin between every pulse, would fully protect it from plasma vaporization \cite{projorion, ga5009_vol3}. However, PuffSat gases like pure oxygen, carbon dioxide and water contain oxygen and will decompose at impact temperatures. This oxygen would aggressively attack flammable phenolic ablators like PICA \cite{pica_ablator_milos2010}. Adding flame retardants and materials like boron carbide and hafnium carbide increases the oxygen resistance of phenolic resins like PICA \cite{phenolic_oxidation_hfc_b4c}.

HITEC-type nitrate/nitrite salts absorb thermal energy and decompose when heated \cite{hitec_solar_salt}. Their decomposition releases oxidizing species \cite{nitrate_salt_decomposition}. Tests must compare their mass-loss rate, impulse coupling, ejecta, corrosion, and adhesion against PICA-based formulations on a deforming ceramic sail. Modified PICA is the reference and solar salt the alternative.

For the rigid metal-plate option \cite{projorion}, protected spray bars carry a distributed hydraulic pressure-atomizing, or airless, manifold instead of inkjet printheads.
Pressure-swirl nozzles break the liquid into droplets using its own pressure drop, eliminating an atomizing-gas supply \cite{lefebvre2017_atomization}.
Adjacent nozzles overlap their footprints and meter carbon-loaded silicone liquids, organic oils, or ionic liquids by zone.
An electrode charges each pressure-generated spray \cite{marchant1985_hydraulic_charge}.
The grounded conductive face then draws the droplets inward, applying the electrostatic deposition mechanism used to raise transfer efficiency and reduce overspray \cite{law2001_electrostatic_spray}.
The charging field switches off before impact.
Reduced ambient pressure can drive a liquid jet into flash-boiling breakup, so the nozzle, liquid vapor pressure, and feed temperature are selected together in vacuum tests \cite{alghamdi2019_flash_boiling}.
Once released, each droplet follows a ballistic path.
The controller compensates for plate motion from the shock absorbers and reaction control thrusters.
Orion's own film sets the scale to work down from.
It was a carbonaceous oil about six thousandths of an inch thick, \SI{150}{\micro\meter}, resprayed over the whole \SI{26}{\meter} plate within the 0.80 to \SI{1.5}{\second} between pulses \cite{ga5009_vol3}.
That thickness was matched to a plasma impinging at \SIrange{100}{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{projorion}.
PuffSat gas arrives near \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.
Scaling the velocity term alone, that speed difference lowers the kinetic energy per unit impinging mass by a factor of 80--330 and maps Orion's film to \SIrange{0.45}{1.8}{\micro\meter}.
The thickness itself is within modern spray-deposition capability.
Ultrasonic spray coating has produced polymer films averaging \SI{0.3}{\micro\meter} and \SI{0.6}{\micro\meter}, including a patterned \SI{10}{\centi\meter} by \SI{10}{\centi\meter} device, with thickness set through ink concentration \cite{liu2017_ultrasonic_spray_film}.
The PuffSat manifold uses pressure-fed ballistic droplets instead, but its \SIrange{1}{2}{\micro\meter} target sits above demonstrated spray thicknesses.
Here that figure is the nominal areal-equivalent wet thickness, the delivered liquid volume divided by plate area.
Coverage sets the lower limit.
Overlapping footprints and surface wetting must leave no optical holes between plate asperities.
We set that limit by opacity rather than ablated mass.
The film has to block the pulse's radiation before it reaches the structural plate, and the base liquid cannot do that job.
A \SI{100}{\micro\meter} slab of clear silicone transmits above 95\% from 350 to \SI{800}{\nano\meter} \cite{liu2015_pdms_transparency}, so a film a hundred times thinner is effectively a window.
The absorber has to be carbon dispersed in the liquid, which is why Orion's film was a graphite-based grease rather than plain oil \cite{projorion}.
Fresh light-absorbing carbon has a mass absorption cross section near \SI{7.5}{\square\meter\per\gram} at \SI{550}{\nano\meter}, and for particles small against the wavelength that figure rises as $1/\lambda$ \cite{bond2006_lac_absorption}.
At a fifth carbon by mass, a \SIrange{1}{2}{\micro\meter} film carries \SIrange{0.2}{0.4}{\gram\per\square\meter} of carbon, an optical depth of \numrange{1.5}{3} at \SI{550}{\nano\meter} and \numrange{4}{8} at \SI{200}{\nano\meter}.
Carbon black spherules run \SIrange{20}{50}{\nano\meter} \cite{bond2006_lac_absorption}, small enough to disperse in a film that thin.
We therefore take a nominal \SIrange{1}{2}{\micro\meter} coat, which is \SIrange{25}{50}{\milli\liter} of near-unit-density liquid and weighs \SIrange{25}{50}{\gram} across a \SI{25}{\square\meter} plate, or up to \SI{75}{\gram} for a denser ionic liquid.
Resprayed every pulse at a few hertz, that is about \SI{0.1}{\kilo\gram\per\second}, or \SIrange{25}{50}{\kilo\gram} over a thousand pulses.
Orion's \SI{150}{\micro\meter} on the same plate would cost \SI{3.8}{\kilo\gram} per coat and \SI{3.8}{\tonne} over that thousand pulses, more than the plate itself.
The liquid film is consumed or swept away during the pulse while protecting the structural metal plate beneath it. Some carbonaceous residue may remain. The design credits it with no protection and applies a fresh sprayed film before the next pulse.

That sprayed film is replenished in flight, between pulses or between bursts. A thicker between-flight option for a rigid pusher is a replaceable facing of factory-cured, millimeter-scale, fiber-reinforced silicone sheet. Laboratory work has molded liquid-silicone sheets onto high-silica glass fabric, where the fabric reinforces the compliant, damping rubber \cite{chen2018_silicone_fiber_sheet}. Under ablation the silicone decomposes to a fragile silica char, and the fabric is what holds that char in place. Oxygen-rich tests on silicone-rubber insulation support the mechanism. Raising the oxygen content thickened the glassy layer on the surface, which buffered particle erosion and slowed recession, though excess oxygen still widened the ablation pit at its edge \cite{shi2019_silicone_oxygen_ablation}. Those tests measured a peak linear ablation rate of \SI{0.933}{\milli\meter\per\second} against a continuous oxygen-kerosene torch carrying entrained particles. A PuffSat pulse is a far shorter and far hotter load, so that rate does not predict PuffSat recession. At the measured torch rate, a \SI{2}{\milli\meter} facing represents about two seconds of cumulative exposure. One millisecond of torch-equivalent exposure per pulse would map that thickness to about \num{2000} pulses. The PuffSat-equivalent dwell and recession rate have not been measured, so \num{2000} is a comparison scale rather than a lifetime floor. The facing mass is near \SI{2.6}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} against a plate whose survival floor is already tens of kilograms per square meter (see \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_mass_stroke}). Small sheets could be bonded or mechanically retained on the plate and exchanged between flights. Factory control of cure and thickness could make this faster and cheaper than casting the same layer on the vehicle. Tests must measure the hot dwell per pulse, seam and bond fatigue, recession, char retention, and fragment shedding through a full PuffSat pulse train.

The other between-flight option is a tiled steel facing. The spray remains the pulse-by-pulse thermal layer, while the steel keeps particle damage in a replaceable part. Over the projected area of a \SI{10}{\micro\meter} spherical ice grain, a \SIrange{1}{2}{\micro\meter} near-unit-density liquid film carries only one-sixth to one-third of the grain's mass. That comparison does not give a ballistic limit. The required steel thickness depends on grain size, cumulative crater overlap, rear-face spall, and the number of pulses between inspections. A \SI{1}{\milli\meter} full-area steel facing on the \SI{25}{\square\meter} plate would weigh about \SI{196}{\kilo\gram}. That is about 6\% of the \SI{3.2}{\tonne} plate baseline in \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_mass_stroke}, and three times the \SI{65}{\kilo\gram} silicone facing above. A \SI{0.5}{\milli\meter} steel facing would weigh about \SI{98}{\kilo\gram}. Individual tiles could be inspected and replaced between flights, so undamaged area would not be discarded.

The tile back and the structural plate form the load-bearing contact pair. The carbon-loaded spray lies on the tile's exposed face and does not separate them. Clean metal at that interface can cold-weld in vacuum, and a metal perimeter retainer carries the same risk at the tile edges. Load runs through distributed pads, discrete contact patches rather than one continuous seat. They confine the bare-metal contact and can be clamped hard enough to suppress fretting, the small repeated sliding that breaks surface oxides in vacuum. ESA tests measured adhesion rising from less than \SI{0.1}{\newton} in static contact to \SI{9.5}{\newton} under fretting for one titanium-alloy and stainless-steel pair. Other metal pairs reached approximately \SIrange{11}{19}{\newton} under fretting \cite{merstallinger_cold_welding}.

Which alloy the plate is made of matters more here than the tile does. Adhesion under fretting fell with nickel content across the steels ESA tested. It ran from Inconel 718 at 52\% nickel down to the low-nickel bearing steels AISI 52100 and AISI 440C, the lowest of any metal pair in the survey. Ti-6Al-4V against itself gave the highest at about \SI{19}{\newton}, more than ten times the bearing steels \cite{merstallinger_cold_welding}. Above we offered titanium alloys and Inconel 718 as plate substrates on strength and temperature grounds. That ranking inverts at this joint, which is a reason to prefer steel. The grades ESA measured are hard bearing steels rather than large structural plate, so the argument binds tightest at the load pads and retainer inserts, where those grades can be used directly.

A titanium plate carries two further penalties that a steel one does not. First, titanium and steel form brittle Fe--Cr--Ti intermetallic phases when they melt together. Attempts to fusion-weld commercially pure titanium to type 304 stainless steel cracked on the steel side, and gas tungsten arc welding separated the workpieces as soon as they cooled \cite{pasang2018_ti_ss_intermetallics}. Melt injected through a punctured tile could fuse a spot that will not release cleanly. Second, NASA's galvanic table for seawater places titanium at anodic index 45 and plain carbon steel at 100, a gap of \SI{0.55}{\volt} against a \SI{0.25}{\volt} cap on any galvanic couple \cite{nasa_std_6012a}. Vacuum removes the electrolyte in flight, but the couple still has to survive humid ground handling between flights.

The same ESA tests found no adhesion between a Vespel SP-3 polyimide--molybdenum-disulfide composite and stainless steel \cite{merstallinger_cold_welding}. A replaceable interleaf of that material is therefore the baseline at the backing and retainer pads if the measured backside temperature stays within its service limit. If the pads run hotter, the fallback is a hard, low-nickel steel insert with a vacuum-compatible diamond-like-carbon coating. Under fretting that coating cut adhesion between AISI 52100 and a nickel-bearing stainless steel from \SI{2499}{\milli\newton} to \SI{856}{\milli\newton} without eliminating it \cite{merstallinger_cold_welding}. Under the gentler impact contact the same coating held for more than \num{37000} cycles with no adhesion measured \cite{merstallinger_coatings}. Jacking tabs, threaded lugs that drive a bolt against the plate to push a stuck part free, supply removal force if a pad still adheres. Qualification uses the actual tile, interleaf, and backing materials at the design clamp pressure, temperature cycle, shock spectrum, and accumulated fretting motion. It also measures interleaf creep. Coating thickness is set by wear-through and delamination in that test rather than by residual-stress cracking alone.

As opposed to Orion, our propulsion system includes reaction control thrusters (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}). These can be positioned forward on the target vehicle, increasing standoff distance and minimizing thermal or directional interference with the pusher plate. 

\subsubsection{Shock Absorption and Damping}\label{sec:pusher_plate_damping}
Shock absorber technology has advanced since Orion. Magnetorheological (MR) fluids offer dynamic vibration damping, reducing the risk of carbon-fiber strut fracture under repeated loading \cite{mr_fluids}. To further reduce mass and thermal buildup, magnetic braking systems can complement mechanical springs and hydraulics. Rare-earth magnets \cite{rare_earth_braking} or high-field REBCO superconducting tapes \cite{rebco_tapes} enable compact magnetic dampers that earlier magnet materials were too weak to support. REBCO tapes are, however, still an emerging technology and require cryogenic refrigeration and superconductor quench management. Because PuffSat impulses are scalable, we can deliberately reduce acceleration per pulse, further relaxing shock absorber requirements.

\subsubsection{Plate Mass, Stroke, and Pulse Rate}\label{sec:pusher_plate_mass_stroke}
Unlike Orion, the PuffSat plate has no critical mass. Orion's plate was forced large by the kiloton floor on nuclear yield, because no bomb can be made arbitrarily small \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}. PuffSat pulse energy scales down smoothly, so the plate can shrink with the pulse. Let $\nu$ be the pulse rate, $M$ the craft mass, $a$ its mean acceleration, and $m$ the plate mass. Each pulse supplies impulse $J=Ma/\nu$, producing a plate velocity step $J/m$. Traversing stroke $s$ during the interval $1/\nu$ gives $s\sim J/(m\nu)$, so $ms\sim Ma/\nu^2$. Faster, smaller pulses therefore buy either a lighter plate at the same stroke or a shorter stroke at the same mass, never both at once. At a pulse rate of a few hertz, a stroke of a few meters holds the plate near a tenth of the craft mass, a few tonnes on the \SI{32}{\tonne} vehicle of our low-orbit case. We assume a survival floor of order tens of kilograms per square meter. That is far below Orion's regime, and below what the buffer already demands. So here the dynamics, not survival, set the plate near a tenth of the craft.

A higher pulse rate brings a cost of its own. It drives the ripple, the leftover swing in the craft's acceleration between pulses, up into the \SIrange{4}{8}{\hertz} band where the body is most sensitive to vertical vibration \cite{iso2631}. There a single-stage absorber would let the craft swing by several g on every pulse. A two-stage absorber, carried over from Orion, fixes this \cite{projorion}. A stiff, short-stroke first stage catches the per-pulse blow, and a soft second stage tuned well below the pulse rate isolates the crew. The first stage absorbs the impulsive blow, so the second stage can run lightly damped, where its transmissibility falls as roughly the square of the frequency ratio. Tuned that far below the pulse rate, it cuts the acceleration ripple by more than an order of magnitude and leaves a nearly steady push.

\subsubsection{Cancelling the Downward Pulse Component}\label{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick}
Catching PuffSats on the descending leg also adds a downward push that the plate's orientation can cancel. Because PuffSats are intercepted while still falling toward their deliberately low \SI{50}{\kilo\meter} disposal perigee (see \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}), the momentum each one delivers is not purely forward. It carries a downward component of about $\sin\gamma$ of the impulse, where $\gamma$ is the PuffSat's flight-path angle below the craft's velocity. At the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude this orbit gives $\gamma \approx \SI{8.5}{\degree}$, so $\sin\gamma$ is about 15\%. That 15\% is a floor. The downward part of each pulse holds near \SI{1.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, while the overtaking velocity that drives the forward thrust shrinks as the craft speeds up, so the downward fraction climbs through the boost and approaches roughly one-half as the craft nears orbital speed (\autoref{sec:down_angle_geometry}). The component has the same sign on every pulse, so it accumulates and bends the trajectory Earthward unless the plate cancels it.

The cancellation is largely geometric, and cheap rather than free. What sets the net force on the craft is the plate's orientation relative to the thrust axis (the craft's forward direction of flight), not its orientation relative to the line back to the PuffSat. In the elastic limit, a plate held normal to the thrust axis reverses the forward velocity while leaving the downward tangential component unchanged, so the craft receives no downward impulse. A plate aimed square at the incoming PuffSat would instead double the downward kick. A plate sized to catch the gas limits sideways escape by geometry, not opacity. The preliminary simulation treats geometric capture, what fraction of the gas the plate intercepts, separately from thermophysical restitution, how elastically that gas rebounds. Its shallow-concave geometry sweep gives $\eta_{\mathrm{capture}}$ values of roughly $0.86$--$0.99$ across the modeled shapes \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The study does not directly model the oblique downward-cancellation geometry, so the vector argument here remains preliminary. At the working $f \approx 0.8$ the bounce is partly inelastic, leaving a residual downward force. Because the downward fraction climbs toward one-half late in the boost, this residual is largest near orbital speed. The active trim below, or the reaction-control thrusters, removes it at a small cost in forward thrust.

Neutralizing the kick this way is usually enough. If a steady upward trim $F_{\mathrm{up}}$ is wanted on top of it, the plate is tilted slightly further off the thrust axis so the reflected gas is thrown a little downward. The craft then feels an upward reaction set by the full rebounded momentum rather than a small spill fraction. Such a trim, applied at the rear plate a distance $d$ behind the center of mass, would pitch the craft. Placing the impact point a height $h = d\,(F_{\mathrm{up}}/F_{\mathrm{fwd}})$ below the centerline makes the forward thrust's couple cancel that pitch. The resultant then passes through the center of mass, and the trim costs nothing in attitude. This shaped plate inherits its momentum coupling from Orion's pusher plate \cite{projorion} and its concave, expansion-wrapping geometry from Medusa's sail \cite{solem_medusa}. The curvature that turns the rebound forward to raise $f$ (see \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}) is the same curvature that lets the rebound carry the downward momentum out, so the down-push fix and the high-$f$ design are one plate-shape decision with two payoffs.

\section{I Love ISRU.  Cheaper PuffSat Pulsed Propulsion \textit{Without} Giant Reusable Rockets}\label{sec:isru}
For PuffSat pulsed propulsion of intercity passengers (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) to achieve genuine economic competitiveness, the cost of launching propulsion PuffSats must be exceptionally low.  The rocket plane must be reliable enough to guarantee on-time takeoffs and avoid missing its initial PuffSat rendezvous.  Rocket acceleration and passenger cabin vibrations must be limited for infants and other susceptible passengers (see \autoref{sec:pregnant_women}), further increasing costs and $\Delta V$.  Even highly reusable rockets may prove too expensive, limiting this technology primarily to the high-end private aviation market. While luxury aviation customers typically prioritize flexibility, PuffSat propelled flights require advance scheduling. Global airline passenger revenue was about \$744 billion in 2024, compared with \$9.3 billion in commercial launch revenue \cite{sia_satellite_industry_2025,iata_air_transport_2024}. The addressable market for scheduled premium PuffSat routes would be only a fraction of airline revenue, but these totals show the scale of passenger transport relative to satellite launch.

Still, it would be disappointing if we can't make suborbital travel mainstream.   If we can launch our PuffSats without giant rockets, we can reduce cost.   After all, ``the best part is no part,'' \cite{best_part_no_part} and a reusable rocket is one heck of a part.

\subsection{Lunar Volatiles For PuffSat Pulsed Propulsion}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Water Drawing From Moon.png}
    \caption{Lunar-launched PuffSats replace Starship-launched PuffSats \cite{earth_image,moon_image}}
    \label{fig:lunar_launched_PuffSats}
\end{figure}
Instead of using Starship, lunar volatiles extracted from the moon's permanently shadowed regions could fill our propulsion PuffSats. As shown in \autoref{fig:lunar_launched_PuffSats}, these PuffSats could be sent into a trans-lunar injection orbit that intersects our terrestrial suborbital rocket plane. Although PuffSat skins and electronics would likely still be sourced from Earth, volatiles constitute the majority of the PuffSats' mass. One approach to launching these PuffSats into orbit involves using rockets powered by lunar-derived propellants. The space community is enthusiastic about water electrolysis to produce and store cryogenic fuel for lunar rockets \cite{nasa_water}. However, this process is energy-intensive and requires complex cryogenic infrastructure, particularly for hydrogen fuel. A reusable lunar rocket designed for repeated landings would require approximately \SI{6}{\km\per\second} of $\Delta V$ \cite{arney_lunar_architecture_2013}, implying propellant mass fractions near 75\% for hydrogen or 80\% for methane, assuming vacuum specific impulses of about \SI{450}{\second} and \SI{360}{\second} respectively \cite{sutton_rocket_propulsion}. While these figures are an improvement over Earth-launched rockets, the high propellant mass fractions remain a serious obstacle.   

\subsection{Lunar Rockets Without Lunar Rocket Fuel} \label{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}
Instead of launching our lunar volatiles towards Earth with conventional rocket fuel, we launch off the moon using the same PuffSat pulsed propulsion we've been discussing for terrestrial launches.   A rocket launched with PuffSat pulsed propulsion from the lunar surface into a trans-lunar injection orbit can burn its engines at periapsis around Earth.  Due to the Oberth effect, a small burn near Earth returns the rocket to the moon with a large velocity change.  As the rocket approaches the moon, it deploys its payload of PuffSats previously filled with lunar volatiles.  These returning PuffSats can push a new, more massive rocket filled with fresh volatiles off the moon.   We can then repeat this cycle for exponential growth in lunar PuffSat launch mass capacity.   We've reduced combustible fuel needs to one small periapsis burn, fueled by a charge carried from Earth, so no rocket fuel must be manufactured on the Moon.


\begin{table}[htpb!]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
        \hline
        Before Collision & After Collision \\\hline
        \includegraphics[width=0.45\textwidth]{images/Pulsed Propulsion Chamber Before Impact.png} &
        \includegraphics[width=0.45\textwidth]{images/_Pulsed Propulsion Chamber After Impact.png} \\ \hline
         
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{A pulsed propulsion chamber.   PuffSats enter, collide and produce thrust.   The valve, likely a fast rotating door synchronized with PuffSat arrivals, is optional because a small front opening will produce less thrust than a large rear opening.  If the explosion turns the gas into plasma, the valve could be magnetic rather than a mechanical door.}
    \label{tab:pulsed_combustion_illustration}
\end{table}

However, we ideally don't require any chemical rocket fuel at all.   Let's again use PuffSat propulsion to launch our PuffSat deployment rocket from the lunar surface into a trans-lunar orbit.   We also launch a second set of PuffSats from the lunar surface into a retrograde trans-lunar orbit.   Using the centimeter precision navigation techniques we've discussed, we sequentially collide these retrograde PuffSats with more massive prograde PuffSats that the main rocket is carrying.  As depicted in \autoref{tab:pulsed_combustion_illustration}, we trap the exploding gas from these collisions in a pulsed propulsion chamber and redirect this gas for thrust.   To use the Oberth effect and maximize gas collision velocity, we collide our PuffSats close to periapsis near Earth.

Suppose our colliding PuffSats each travel at $v_p=\SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$.  From \autoref{eq:max_m_rp}, the maximum theoretical combined effective exhaust velocity is $v_e = \frac{1}{2}v_p = \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$.  Suppose this pulsed explosion has real-world losses for an effective exhaust of $v_e = \SI{3}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$.  By \autoref{eq:ve_general} that assumption is a jet efficiency of $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.77$. This sits comfortably above the $0.5$ floor, where forward thrust vanishes for this mix (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}).   If the main rocket accelerates at its Earth periapsis with a burn of \SI{1.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it will reach the moon at \SI{7.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.   Off the lunar surface, a payload needs about \SI{3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to enter a prograde transfer with low Earth periapsis and about \SI{3.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for the retrograde one \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.  The cycle launches both, in the one-quarter retrograde to three-quarters prograde mix that \autoref{sec:dv_effective} shows maximizes chamber momentum.   If the rocket deploys propulsion PuffSats on lunar approach, they can push another rocket off the moon with a mass greater than the incoming PuffSats. Specifically, the incoming propulsion PuffSats have enough momentum to start an exponential growth cycle where each loop around the moon launches about 1.455 times the starting mass.   If we do this once per lunar month (27.3 days), we have increased our initial launch mass capacity by a factor of 1 million in about 2.8 years \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsection{Lunar Oxygen Has Mass And That Is All It Needs}\label{sec:lunar_mining}
Lunar volatiles like water ice are ideal for filling PuffSats, but they are scarce.   However, oxygen is the most abundant element in lunar regolith, roughly 45\% by mass \cite{mckay1991lunar}, locked up in its minerals.   Using solar energy and mining rovers derived from platforms like NASA's IPEx (ISRU Pilot Excavator) \cite{ipex_pilot_excavator}, we can produce oxygen.  On the volatile-depleted sunlit lunar surface, finding something to burn oxygen with for rocket propulsion is hard.   But PuffSat pulsed propulsion does not require oxygen combustion, just gas momentum.  In shadowed craters or artificial shade, lunar derived oxygen will liquefy and perhaps even solidify for storage.  

We use some of our lunar PuffSats for pulsed propulsion to lift terrestrial suborbital supply rockets into low Earth orbit.  The supplies include solar panels, pusher plates, PuffSat skins, electronics, and rovers. These supply rockets then use conventional engines for the trans-lunar leg so they can resupply our lunar surface operations.   When they reach the moon, we also use PuffSat pulsed propulsion to decelerate for lunar landing.  The deceleration propulsion PuffSats would previously have been launched from the moon's surface into an eccentric lunar orbit opposite to the incoming supplies' velocity.   We can optionally reduce terrestrial resupply requirements by embracing basic lunar manufacturing using metals like iron that are byproducts of lunar oxygen production.  These lunar metals could be shaped into pusher plate and pulsed propulsion chamber components.  

We use the rest of our lunar PuffSats to exponentially grow our PuffSat mass capacity in orbit using the Oberth cycle discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}.  Oxygen production is very energy-intensive, and abrasive lunar regolith will likely degrade machinery faster than it would on Earth. Apollo surface suits were more worn after eight hours of lunar activity than training suits were after a hundred \cite{gaier2005lunardust}.  Regolith-resistant machines may still enable propulsion at costs that make hypersonic travel scalable.

\subsection{Jurassic Dark: Lunar Cold Trap Paleontology and PuffSat Mining Ethics}\label{sec:jurassic_dark}
In \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}, we explored using lunar volatiles from permanently shadowed craters for PuffSat pulsed propulsion. These cold craters could also preserve biomolecules and organic biomarkers from Earth impact ejecta over very long timescales \cite{dino_dna}. While speculative, the scientific value of lunar cold trap paleontology obligates us to check for frozen fossil biomarkers as we mine lunar ice for PuffSat propulsion. The ethical tension is direct. The same excavation that fills PuffSats destroys the layered record it samples, so a survey must precede mining rather than run alongside it.

We could analyze the volatiles we're extracting to see if they contain homochiral organic molecules. Near-total single-handedness is a strong biosignature, since nonbiological chemistry tends to yield racemic mixtures (equal amounts of both handedness) or only small imbalances between them \cite{glavin2020chiral}.   Studying samples that contain homochiral molecules could reveal much of Earth's biological history, including how life began. And, of course, the discovery of dinosaur DNA would surely delight \textit{Jurassic Park} \cite{jurassic_park} fans.

\section{Sorry, I Don't Need ISRU}\label{sec:sorry_isru}
\subsection{The Need For Speed} \label{sec:no_isru_rocket}\cite{topgun1986needforspeed}
If our propulsion PuffSats can reach high enough velocities, we could directly launch terrestrial payloads heavier than the PuffSats themselves.   This would eliminate the need for lunar ISRU. Repeatedly executing this launch cycle with ever larger (or more numerous) payloads would enable exponential growth in our launch capacity.

Consider a rocket launched from Earth carrying a pulsed propulsion payload.   We send $\frac{3}{4}$ of our payload on a prograde trajectory with a solar periapsis similar to the Parker Solar Probe and the remaining quarter on the opposing retrograde trajectory with the same Sun-grazing periapsis.  That split is the loss-free optimum of \autoref{sec:dv_effective}.  A lossy nozzle moves it toward more prograde mass, and because over-diluting costs far less than under-diluting, a flown architecture should err on that side (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}).  These trajectories require high $\Delta V$ but we can use Jupiter gravity assists to achieve them (see \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}).   These payloads release micro-thrust, precision-steered projectiles (see \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}) which then collide sequentially at periapsis in a pulsed propulsion chamber as described in \autoref{sec:dv_effective}. At these energies that chamber is a magnetic nozzle rather than a walled one, since no wall survives the pass (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}). In propulsion chambers that minimize energy losses, the resulting plasma can produce effective exhaust velocities of \SI{100}{\kilo\meter\per\second} or higher, depending on periapsis. Perhaps we accelerate from \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{250}{\kilo\meter\per\second} with Earth crossing speeds around \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.  At these Earth crossing velocities, incoming PuffSats could collide with PuffSats aboard a new terrestrial rocket in the rocket's propulsion chamber.  With the thrust from these collisions, our payload would then be sent on prograde and retrograde orbits with low solar periapsis, repeating the cycle.

If desired, we can optimize exhaust velocity further by lowering the periapsis in steps, so the payload falls farther toward the Sun between collisions.  This way, collisions after each periapsis reduction occur at progressively higher velocities for greater effective exhaust velocity.   A boosted projectile leaves periapsis on an escaping hyperbola and crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} only once, about \SI{125}{\degree} of heliocentric longitude from where Earth has moved to. The return must be phased to arrive at Earth rather than merely cross its orbit. Phasing costs a longer trajectory, a heavier boost, or a gravity assist, and it sets the shortest re-intercepting cycle near \SI{0.86}{\year} (\autoref{sec:earth_reintercept}, computed in \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}). Doubling the payload each cycle then reaches a factor of a million in about 17 years \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Diving instead to the 4 solar radii of the original 2005 Solar Probe design \cite{mccomas2005solar} raises periapsis speeds from about \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so effective exhaust velocity rises by more than half. We spend the stronger Oberth effect on economy rather than on a faster return. A periapsis boost of \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, about 30\% smaller than the \SI{50}{\kilo\meter\per\second} boost above, holds the same \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth-crossing speed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That speed already carries enough kinetic energy at Earth to throw the next payload onto its own low-periapsis orbit, and the deeper dive delivers it with fewer PuffSats per projectile (\autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal} sizes the shield for the deeper dive).

\subsection{Radiative Differences From Project Orion}\label{sec:radiative_differences}
Project Orion required shaped explosives to redirect plasma towards the pusher plate because the bomb's prompt energy was mostly emitted as X-rays that had to be converted into directed kinetic propellant \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}.  In contrast, our proposed system anticipates minimal radiative losses from collisions, even at speeds matching the Parker Solar Probe. Our reasoning for this low loss stems from
\begin{itemize}
    \item The colliding atoms in our system have a much lower atomic mass than fissile atoms. For a given total kinetic energy, the energy is distributed among a greater number of particles, giving lower average kinetic energy per particle and therefore lower plasma temperatures. Plasmas from lighter elements also have higher specific heat capacity, meaning they absorb more energy for a given temperature increase. 
    \item Fission fragments recoil at a few percent of the speed of light, roughly \SI{10000}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{krane1988nuclear}. An inefficient bomb whose expanding plasma carries about 1\% of that kinetic energy moves near one-tenth the fragment speed, roughly \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so our colliding atoms at a couple hundred \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} are about one-fifth as fast. A thermalized plasma's temperature scales as $m\langle v^2\rangle$, so this velocity ratio alone suggests peak temperatures at least 25 times lower.
    \item Folding the lower atomic mass into the same $m\langle v^2\rangle$ scaling puts temperatures perhaps 200 times lower in our collision than in a bomb. Radiative flux rises with the fourth power of temperature ($T^4$) \cite{incropera_heat_transfer}, so this points to a peak flux many orders of magnitude below that of the least efficient nuclear bombs, naively $200^4 \approx 10^9$. That figure is an order-of-magnitude plausibility bound, not a computed result.
    \item The colliding masses should be much smaller than a nuclear bomb, with a lower radiative surface area.   As the collision plasma expands adiabatically, a small plasma's volume grows proportionally faster than a large bomb plasma's for the same absolute expansion. The faster volume growth drops the temperature more quickly, cutting radiative output.
\end{itemize}

Lower temperatures generate longer-wavelength blackbody X-rays compared to those from bombs. This means materials with lower atomic numbers (lower Z) can absorb this radiation and keep the plasma opaque. While Project Orion favored expensive, difficult-to-work-with tungsten as the shaped-charge propellant slab \cite{balcomb1970nuclear, dyson2002project_orion}, these longer wavelengths should allow for the use of cheaper elements to achieve plasma opacity. Opacity keeps the plasma pushing on a physical surface. Two later sections turn this plausibility argument into a requirement. \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} leaves low radiative loss as a design target for a radiation-hydrodynamic calculation we have not run. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} sets the number that calculation has to return. Radiative escape has to stay below about a tenth of a percent for the magnetic nozzle to remain a passive structure.

\subsection{Solid Projectiles Rather Than Gas Generating PuffSats}\label{sec:solid_PuffSats}
Given the extreme velocities at periapsis, we switch from low-density PuffSat-generated gases to solid projectiles. Despite the harsh conditions, the corona's low perturbation environment (see \autoref{sec:periapsis_challenges}) enables high precision. Like Proba-3, we target millimeter-scale precision for head-on hits on small solids. At these speeds, impact vaporizes and ionizes the solids, so the interaction must be modeled as a radiating plasma \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. Project Orion shows that opacity and radiation transport matter in pusher interactions \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}, but its nuclear-pulse geometry does not establish the optical depth or momentum coupling here. Those quantities depend on composition, areal density, geometry, and nonequilibrium radiation transport. The iron dopant discussed below is intended to increase optical depth, but a dedicated radiation-hydrodynamic calculation must determine the required fraction. Until then, low radiative loss and near-elastic coupling are design targets rather than established results.

We'd make these solid projectiles primarily from cheap low atomic number (low Z) materials like the carbon materials and ceramics that dominated the Parker Solar Probe's thermal protection system \cite{parker_thermal_protection}.   A small amount of cheap higher Z materials like iron would be included to ensure opacity to the blackbody X-rays produced.  
All the projectile's mass contributes to propulsion, irrespective of its composition.  As shown in \autoref{fig:parker_cross_section}, the projectiles can contain electronics, heat shielding, and some liquid that passively or actively cools the skin through a centrifugally driven convection cycle.    When close to the Sun, they can operate on batteries or compact heat engines, like MEMS-scale Stirling engines \cite{mems_stirling_engine}. These alternatives are good options where intense near-Sun sunlight can degrade traditional photovoltaics.

One possible low-cost projectile architecture might use graphite as both a structural backbone and thermal conductor, channeling heat toward shadowed radiators.  If the graphite can be cheaply made into insulating carbon foams, these foams will also reduce heat flux towards electronics.  The foams don't need to be optimized for low density like the Parker Solar Probe since all the projectile mass contributes to energy on impact. A bonding layer joins a thin ceramic skin designed to reflect incident solar flux. Ceramic or ablative heat shields protect electronics and propulsion systems housed in permanently shadowed zones. Given the low raw material costs of graphite and ceramics, large-scale manufacturing could drive per-kilogram projectile costs down to just a few dollars.

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Parker Projectile Cross Section.png}
    \caption{This cross-section shows a projectile with a highly reflective, heat-tolerant skin that is cooled by a centrifugally driven fluid. Heat is radiated away through a pipe that extends into the projectile's shadow, or at least to areas that receive sunlight obliquely. Batteries and electronics provide power.}
    \label{fig:parker_cross_section}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Navigation Challenges Near Periapsis}\label{sec:periapsis_challenges}
Close solar periapsis poses several challenges. Sensors using visible light must filter solar illumination, and instruments must withstand heat and solar wind. Relativistic effects are predictable but must enter the trajectory calculations, and the high collision speeds demand extremely accurate navigation. The pulsed propulsion chamber may ablate slightly on each pulse, and the projectiles must be miniaturized to at most a few kilograms to contain the immense energy released at these velocities. Solar-luminosity variability is another navigation input. Average radiation pressure can be included in the trajectory model, while variations from sunspots and other magnetic phenomena must be forecast or estimated in flight. Neural networks have been studied for predicting total and spectral solar irradiance from photospheric images \cite{muralikrishna2022solar}.

Despite the extreme environment, solar approaches to within the outer corona experience relatively mild total perturbations. The outer corona is tenuous \cite{corona_density}, far below the residual-atmosphere densities that drag low- and very-low Earth orbit satellites \cite{leo_orbit_perturbation_simulation}. Combined with the predictable gravitational environment near solar periapsis, this leaves the unpredictable share of drag and gravitational variation smaller in the outer corona than in low-Earth orbit.
  
Accelerating rockets gently over minutes would require a large time gap between successive PuffSats. That gap is set by orbital phasing and stays fixed around the orbit. The PuffSats sit farthest apart at periapsis, where they move fastest, and bunch together at larger radii, where they move slower. These two effects cancel, so the arrival cadence at the rocket is the same everywhere. Because periapsis speeds are so high, even a large and hard-to-maintain spacing yields only a brief gap between arrivals. Left at that spacing, the Earth-crossing impacts would land in too short a window for the minutes-long push a crewed vehicle needs. That cadence is not the binding constraint, though. We spread the stream during the long return coast to Earth, not at periapsis. Deploying the PuffSats about \SI{0.5}{\astronomicalunit} out and giving each a small self-steering $\Delta V$ makes them arrive over a five-minute window, about $\pm 2.5$ minutes. The furthest unit needs only about \SI{45}{\meter\per\second}, its \SI{22500}{\kilo\meter} half-window divided by the \SI{5e5}{\second} coast at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, well within a microthruster's reach. The target's acceleration is then $a = \Delta V / T$, the delivered speed over the window length $T$. An \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb to low Earth orbit spread over the \SI{300}{\second} window is about 2.7 g, and an \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} eccentric insertion about 3.7 g. Widening the window lowers g directly. Passengers held to the 2 g cap in the benign chest-to-back $Gx$ direction of \autoref{sec:pregnant_women} need the window widened to about $\pm 3.5$ minutes, bought with slightly more $\Delta V$ or a deployment farther out. Because the stream supplies the velocity change from outside, the payload carries little or no propellant, and a reusable ground launcher can give it the low-speed initial nudge. Orbital speed itself still comes from the PuffSat impacts, not from that launcher.   

A gentler staged path stays available for the most delicate payloads. Rather than push astronauts directly, we can use two sets of propulsion PuffSats. First, our Earth-crossing PuffSats from the Sun push an unmanned payload into an eccentric orbit around Earth. Then that eccentric payload deploys PuffSats to push the high-value cargo, people or satellites, into its final orbit just as we discussed in \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}. The staging roughly doubles the propellant, a small penalty at the \$80 per kilogram materials cost of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal}.

\subsection{Initial Periapsis Orbits With Jupiter Gravity Assists} \label{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}
A final issue is placing the initial payload in a retrograde low periapsis solar orbit.  The best approach for this is likely using a gravity assist from Jupiter, which was the original plan for the Parker Solar Probe mission \cite{mccomas2005solar}. 

To operate in deep space without relying on large solar arrays, the spacecraft may draw power from fuel cells or batteries. However, electrochemical systems are difficult to optimize under extreme cold. To mitigate thermal constraints, exothermic fuels can provide localized heating.  Alternatively, power can be generated via miniaturized chemically driven heat engines such as MEMS-scale gas turbines \cite{mems_gas_turbine}. If these energy sources use explosive reactants or yield volatile reactant products, these compounds can later be repurposed as PuffSat propellants. They then contribute to momentum transfer on impact. Near Jupiter, the PuffSats' volatiles would freeze and shield the spacecraft from radiation. The internal power source and shielding infrastructure would permit a close Jupiter flyby that maximizes our gravitational slingshot.

These alternatives are not the only option. Large solar arrays have already powered craft at Jupiter. NASA's Juno spacecraft reached Jupiter on three \SI{9}{\meter} arrays and has operated there on about \SI{500}{\watt} since 2016, the first spacecraft to run on sunlight at Jupiter's distance \cite{bolton2017juno, juno_solar_record}. Concentrating optics, mirrors or lenses that gather sunlight from a wide aperture onto a small cell, can shrink that array or warm the electronics directly, much as we propose for the Ceres power depots in \autoref{sec:ceresly_good}.

Optionally we can push more mass into the initial orbit by using a Jupiter gravity assist to place propulsion PuffSats into a retrograde Hohmann trajectory back to Earth. We can add a burn at Jupiter to use its Oberth effect.   They would cross Earth at around \SI{69}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which could push a larger terrestrial payload onto  prograde and retrograde colliding trajectories at low solar periapsis.

\subsection{Jupiter-Only Exponential Launch Growth}\label{sec:jupiter_only_growth}
A simpler way to bootstrap an exponential launch cycle is to use the retrograde Hohmann trajectory from Jupiter described in \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial} but without close solar approaches.   PuffSats returning from Jupiter can push about 8 times their weight into a highly eccentric orbit around Earth.   A small conventional burn coupled with solar electric engines and gravity assists with the inner planets can return this payload to Jupiter and restart the cycle.     The growth is slow compared to other proposals but may provide sufficient launch capacity to supply the satellite market within a few decades.  A trajectory search in the companion calculations repository, scored on how quickly the launched mass doubles, found the best cycle is also the simplest. A direct burn from Earth with an unpowered Jupiter flyby (no burn at Jupiter) beats every gravity-assist chain through the inner planets. Its Earth-phased return closes at about \SI{60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and doubles the launched mass every 4.0 years once the small apoapsis burn that turns the retrograde push into a prograde departure is charged \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Catching the returning PuffSats head-on in a pulsed propulsion chamber instead, the prograde--retrograde trick of \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}, cuts that chemical charge to 6\% of the departing mass and shortens the doubling to about 3.0 years \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Growing faster than that asks the departing batch to split at Jupiter. One wave arrives early and pushes the next payload into its parking orbit, and a second arrives one parking orbit later to supply the projectiles that launch it. An earlier model treated the planets as moving on circles in one plane, and in that model the split had to be bought with a burn at Jupiter. Rescoring the chain on real ephemerides retires that charge. A real ephemeris lets the transfer choose when it meets Jupiter, a second steering knob the circular model does not have. Eight of the eleven flown cycles then buy their ten-day head start for under \SI{1}{\meter\per\second}, a chain mean of \SI{0.179}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The chain then doubles the launched mass every 1.74 years when the nozzle delivers 60\% of its ideal impulse, and every 1.45 years at 80\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That efficiency is the parameter everything else rides on, and \autoref{sec:space_mortgages} prices what it is worth.

We can also combine this Jupiter launch cycle with reusable rockets or the lunar ice based cycle discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}.  By increasing the number of initial PuffSat launches toward Jupiter, we can more rapidly scale up our launch throughput. Once throughput is up, Earth-based payloads can ride the returning PuffSats to orbit.

\subsubsection{With Space Mortgages, First-Time Buyers Win}\label{sec:space_mortgages}
Someone has to pay for the first few cycles, and here is a way to ask. An investor does not buy a launch slot. They buy kilograms of the fleet itself, and those kilograms stay in the loop and multiply. Call it a space mortgage. The operator borrows against the subscriptions to fund the first Starship loads of PuffSats toward Jupiter, and the growing fleet in transit is the collateral. A house bought with a mortgage is worth roughly what it was worth last year. A kilogram in this fleet is worth about half again what it was worth last year. A kilogram returning from Jupiter pushes about eight times its own weight into a high Earth orbit, and that is where the growth comes from. The hardware this funds is not exotic in scale. The outbound leg's pulse is small enough that its magnetic nozzle masses tens of tonnes rather than hundreds. The seed vehicle is therefore a ship a single Starship can loft (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}).

What sets the rate of return is how well that nozzle works. A PuffSat arrives head-on at about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and vaporizes a slug of cheap matter carried aboard the departing craft. A magnetic field then turns the fireball into a jet out the back. Write $e_2$ for the recovery, meaning the share of the ideal collimated impulse that the real device delivers. It absorbs the spread of the plume and the mass the field fails to grip. Two other efficiencies already appear in this paper, and $e_2$ is neither of them. The fudge factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction} says how elastically gas rebounds off a solid plate. The jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} scales the jet before the arriving projectile's momentum is debited. That is why it has a floor at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$, where forward thrust vanishes. Recovery scales what is left after that debit, so it has no such floor. Any $e_2$ above zero still pushes the craft forward.

One nozzle could in principle fly both legs of this cycle, rather than a pusher plate on the Earth boost and a nozzle on the Jupiter departure. \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle} prices that option and finds it the better one over most of the range. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} settles first what the slug that either device vaporizes should be made of.

\autoref{tab:space_mortgage_growth} scores the whole chain against $e_2$ and $f$ together. Each entry is the multiple by which the mass launched off Earth grows across the eleven cycles the chain actually flies, from November 2026 to April 2055. Both parameters matter and neither should be quoted without the other.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Growth of launched mass over the eleven flown cycles of the Jupiter-only chain, \SI{28.4}{\year}, by nozzle recovery $e_2$ and pusher-plate fudge factor $f$ \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Every cycle is charged for its own ground launch and must return a fifteenth of the mass lifted off the pad, a floor that binds only in the top two rows. The recovery is written $e_2$ because \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle} gives the growth push a recovery $e_1$ of its own; this table keeps a pusher plate on that leg. Entries below 1 are cycles that lose mass. Thirty years is the round number a mortgage is written for; the ephemeris supplies 28.4, and these are the figures it verified rather than a rate run past where the chain was flown.}
    \label{tab:space_mortgage_growth}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Recovery $e_2$} & \textbf{$f = 0.5$} & \textbf{$f = 0.6$} & \textbf{$f = 0.7$} & \textbf{$f = 0.8$} \\\hline
        0.25 & 0.011 & 0.022 & 0.039 & 0.059 \\\hline
        0.30 & 1.0 & 3.2 & 7.7 & 15 \\\hline
        0.40 & 40 & 161 & 506 & \num{1328} \\\hline
        0.50 & 363 & \num{1587} & \num{5371} & \num{15100} \\\hline
        0.60 & \num{1676} & \num{7827} & \num{28060} & \num{83070} \\\hline
        0.70 & \num{5182} & \num{25460} & \num{95480} & \num{2.94e5} \\\hline
        0.80 & \num{12350} & \num{63180} & \num{2.46e5} & \num{7.83e5} \\\hline
        0.90 & \num{24590} & \num{1.30e5} & \num{5.21e5} & \num{1.71e6} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Read the last column first. At $f = 0.8$ a nozzle recovering 40\% of the ideal impulse grows the fleet 28.8\% a year, and one recovering 90\% grows it 65.8\% a year. That is roughly twice the annual rate. Anyone who has compared a 3\% savings account against a 6\% one over thirty years knows the answer is not twice the money. Here it is \num{1284} times the mass, \num{1328} against \num{1.71e6}. Half a point of nozzle recovery is worth three orders of magnitude of delivered payload. No financing structure does that. Only the hardware does.

The top row is why this is a mortgage rather than a gift. At $e_2 = 0.25$ and $f = 0.5$ the loop cannot pay its own transport costs and returns \num{0.011}, about a cent on the dollar. Nothing is running backward there, since recovery has no reversal floor. It is a fleet spending more mass to reach Jupiter than it brings home. You can be underwater on a space mortgage, and the whole first row shows what that looks like.

None of this can be settled on a test range, which is the reason early cycles are worth buying. The energy is not the obstacle. Seventy kilograms moving at \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second} carries \SI{171}{\giga\joule}, about 41 tonnes of TNT, a large but ordinary test. Delivering that energy is the obstacle. Reaching the speed inside a launcher of length $L$ demands an acceleration of $v^2/2L$. Even a \SI{10}{\kilo\meter} barrel subjects the projectile to \num{25000}~g. A guided round carrying a slug does not survive that. The air is a second obstacle. At \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second} sea-level air presses on the nose at roughly \SI{6}{\giga\pascal}. The entire flight path therefore has to be evacuated. That means the Straw Way of \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}, infrastructure this cycle is supposed to pay for rather than presuppose. Shrinking the pulse to something a laboratory can make does not rescue the test either. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} shows that a smaller fireball cools and radiates faster, so a scaled-down shot returns a recovery that does not carry over to flight. The only instrument that measures the flight value of $e_2$ is a flight.
