Being early therefore beats being rich. The chain flies eleven cycles in 28.4 years, spaced 2.18 and 3.28 years apart, because that is how long Jupiter takes to return to a usable position \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Each cycle returns one measurement of $e_2$ and permits one redesign of the nozzle, so eleven redesigns is the entire budget. A competitor with ten times the capital still waits 2.18 years for their next data point. A rival starting five years late is two redesigns behind and stays two redesigns behind. That is the part of this business that money cannot buy.

One condition sits under every number in the table. The parked payload splits into the craft that departs and the slug that craft will vaporize, and both go back to Jupiter. A kilogram taken as delivered cargo is therefore a kilogram not reinvested, and the fleet grows at its own rate less whatever holders withdraw. The multiples above assume nobody withdraws. A working business compounds first and harvests later, so the valuable claim is the one with the most cycles still ahead of it. That is the sense in which first-time buyers win.

\subsubsection{Watering It Down, Literally}\label{sec:watering_it_down}
A magnetic nozzle does not run cold. Two things heat it. The first is the current the plume itself carries. A hot plasma pushes the magnetic field out of its own interior, and it does that by running a sheet of current around its edge. That current runs through a plasma of finite conductivity, so it dissipates, and the field slips through the plume rather than gripping it perfectly. The field leaks out about eight times more slowly than the plume expands, which is good enough to steer with and far from lossless. The pulsed field also drives currents in the coils themselves, and a superconducting coil has to have that heat pumped back out at cryogenic temperature. The second source is light. A plume opaque enough to trap its own radiation glows like a blackbody, and at \SI{15000}{\kelvin} a surface radiates \SI{2.87}{\giga\watt\per\square\meter}. Intercepting even a small share of that is a large thermal load on a small ship. Radiators sized for it would weigh more than the nozzle.

That last penalty is worse than it sounds, and it has a cheap fix. A superconductor carries current with no resistance right up until some small patch warms past its critical temperature. The patch turns resistive, the current heats it further, and the normal region spreads. Engineers call that runaway a quench, and it ends the pulse and possibly the coil. Heat is expensive to remove down there as well. Pumping a joule out at \SI{77}{\kelvin} against a \SI{300}{\kelvin} sink costs \SI{2.9}{\joule} at the Carnot limit and roughly twenty joules with a real cryocooler. The fix is to keep the induced current out of the superconductor entirely. A separate shell of ordinary copper, mounted between the plume and the coil, is a lower-impedance path for a rapidly changing field than the coil behind it, so the current flows in the copper instead. The copper has resistance and warms, but it warms at whatever temperature it is mounted at and radiates from there rather than from inside the cryostat. Pulsed fusion propulsion designs already work this way. Romanelli, Mignone and Cervone give each ring of their multi-coil chamber a superconducting coil that supplies the seed field and a separate conducting thrust coil that carries the current the expanding plasma induces \cite{romanelli2017pulsed_fusion_nozzle}. The superconductor sets the field up and the copper takes the hit.

A second layer of defense sits inside the conductor itself. REBCO tape is plated with copper as a stabilizer, a parallel path that carries the current and absorbs heat if a patch does go normal. It matters more in these tapes than in older superconductors, because a normal zone spreads slowly in REBCO and is correspondingly hard to catch by watching for a voltage. Marchevsky reports that raising the copper cross-section from \SI{0.16}{\square\milli\meter} to \SI{0.40}{\square\milli\meter} halved the hot-spot temperature in current-dump tests on insert coils \cite{marchevsky2021quench}. That copper carries no supercurrent, so more of it means less current per square millimeter of conductor and a weaker field for the same coil mass. The shell outside the coil costs nothing in current density, which is why it belongs first in the order of defenses and the stabilizer last.

The slug can absorb that heat instead, and this is the reason it is water. Write $k$ for the slug ratio, the kilograms of carried matter vaporized for every kilogram of arriving PuffSat. Taking water from \SI{300}{\kelvin} to steam at \SI{1000}{\kelvin} absorbs \SI{3.88}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, against \SI{1.16}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for liquid nitrogen and \SI{1.38}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for dry ice. Most of that is the phase change. Boiling a kilogram of water costs \SI{2.26}{\mega\joule} on its own, eleven times what boiling a kilogram of nitrogen costs, and that one number is most of why water wins. At $k = 4$ the full figure soaks up 1.5\% of the pulse energy, where nitrogen manages 0.45\%. Read those as a ceiling rather than as an operating point. \autoref{tab:bag_state} shows the flown bag stopping well short of them, and explains why. Nothing is thrown away in the process. Heat poured into the slug before the collision is heat the collision no longer has to supply, so the energy needed to turn the slug into a conducting plasma falls from \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} to \SI{81.3}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. The waste heat is not vented, it is spent on the propellant's own ignition. Water also sits still for the trip. A passive body runs near \SI{278}{\kelvin} at Earth and \SI{122}{\kelvin} at Jupiter, and ice is stable across that whole range. Nitrogen boils at \SI{77}{\kelvin} and would need active refrigeration at both ends of a cycle lasting two to three years. Carbon dioxide reaches about \SI{40}{\bar} of vapor pressure at Earth distance and would need a pressure vessel. This is the opposite of the choice made for the PuffSat itself, which is atomized into droplets rather than boiled (\autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}), and the reason for the difference is simply that the PuffSat has no waste heat to spend while the ship has heat it must get rid of.

Where the ship is changes the accounting, and the cycle's two burns sit at opposite ends of it. The Jupiter departure fires where a passive body runs \SI{122}{\kelvin}, so its slug is not cool water waiting to boil. It is ice a hundred and fifty kelvin below freezing, and nothing can be vented until it is melted. Warming that ice to \SI{273}{\kelvin} takes about \SI{0.26}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, melting it takes \SI{0.33}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and bringing the liquid up to room temperature takes another \SI{0.14}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. The total is \SI{0.73}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and the ship owes it every pulse whether or not the nozzle runs hot. The Earth boost fires at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, where the same slug sits near \SI{278}{\kelvin} as liquid water and owes almost none of that bill. One burn gets a free \SI{0.73}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of heat sink from the cold it was stored at. The other does not, and pays for the difference in bag film.

Water alone will not steer. A magnetic field grips a conductor, and steam at a few thousand kelvin is not one. The fix is an alkali seed, a trace of an easily ionized metal that supplies free electrons at temperatures where nothing else will \cite{kerrebrock1964nonequilibrium}. Open-cycle magnetohydrodynamic power plants seed their combustion gas the same way and for the same reason \cite{rosa1968mhd, messerle1995mhd}. We assume potassium carbonate at 1.77\% of the slug by mass, which delivers 1\% potassium. The carbonate is what makes it practical, and it is cheaper and easier to handle than potassium metal, which catches fire on contact with water. Getting 1\% potassium takes \SI{1.80}{\gram} of carbonate per \SI{100}{\milli\liter} of slug, against the \SI{110}{\gram} that will dissolve \cite{crc_handbook}, so the solution sits at about a sixtieth of saturation and nothing precipitates at any temperature the slug sees. Solubility is not what narrows the field. Potassium chloride would be cheaper and dissolve as readily, but it would put chlorine into the plume, and chlorine destroys ozone catalytically \cite{molina1974ozone}. This paper already declines to put lead in the atmosphere (\autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}), and chlorine is refused on the same grounds. Potassium sulfate is ruled out the same way rather than on solubility, since \SI{12}{\gram} per \SI{100}{\milli\liter} is still five times what the seed needs. Sulfur in the plume is the objection. Carrying the carbonate rather than the bare metal costs \SI{0.36}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of extra dissociation, which is 0.42\% of the ignition budget. Potassium is also the right alkali rather than merely an affordable one. Cesium ionizes more easily but weighs 3.4 times as much, so above about \SI{3800}{\kelvin} a kilogram of potassium supplies more electrons than a kilogram of cesium, having already ionized most of what it carries.

The seed buys something beyond conductivity, and it decides how much of the collision energy is usable. Breaking water into its atoms takes \SI{50.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, about 60\% of the whole ignition bill, and that energy sits in broken bonds rather than in motion. It comes back only if the atoms find each other again as the plume cools and expands. They do, and quickly. The reaction needs three bodies to meet, so its rate climbs steeply with density, and at \SI{1}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} the recombination time is a hundredth of a microsecond against an expansion lasting hundreds. The energy is a loan rather than a cost. What makes the loan collectable is that potassium keeps supplying electrons long after the water has re-formed.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Conductivity and chemistry of a seeded water plume as it cools, at 1\% potassium by mass. The magnetic Reynolds number $Rm$ measures how strongly the field grips the flow. The field holds the plume at all as long as $Rm$ stays above about 1, but the share of stored field energy that leaks into the plume each expansion is about $1/Rm$, and the slug can only absorb that heat while $Rm$ stays above roughly 25.}
    \label{tab:seed_window}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|l|}\hline
        \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{Potassium ionized} & \textbf{$Rm$} & \textbf{State of the water} \\\hline
        \SI{2000}{\kelvin} & 0.01\% & 0.1 & recombined, field lets go \\\hline
        \SI{3000}{\kelvin} & 1.1\% & 9.2 & recombined \\\hline
        \SI{4000}{\kelvin} & 11.0\% & 76.5 & recombined \\\hline
        \SI{5000}{\kelvin} & 38.0\% & 238 & mostly recombined \\\hline
        \SI{6000}{\kelvin} & 70.1\% & 400 & dissociating \\\hline
        \SI{15000}{\kelvin} & 99.9\% & 361 & fully dissociated \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\autoref{tab:seed_window} shows the window this opens, and without meaning to it also prices the field leak that \autoref{tab:bag_state} charges the slug for. Those are the same number, and the paper spent a while treating them as two. Magnetic field soaks into a conductor over the diffusion time $\tau_d = \mu_0 \sigma L^2$, and the plume expands across its own size in about $L/v$. Divide one by the other and the length appears once, leaving $\mu_0 \sigma v L$, which is the definition of $Rm$. So the share of the field's stored energy that leaks into the plume during one expansion is about $1/Rm$, and the third column of \autoref{tab:seed_window} is a leak schedule written backwards.

Read that way the window is narrower than the ionization alone suggests. At \SI{4000}{\kelvin}, $1/Rm$ is 1.3\%, which the slug absorbs without difficulty. At \SI{3000}{\kelvin} it is 10.9\%, and at the stored energies of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} that would ask the slug to take up about two and a half times its own mass as vapor. It cannot, and what fails at that point is not the bag but the confinement. The usable floor is therefore near \SI{3300}{\kelvin}. Potassium does condense out near \SI{2500}{\kelvin}, but the leak binds about eight hundred kelvin before condensation gets the chance. Nothing in the recombination argument is lost, since \autoref{tab:seed_window} already has the water recombined by \SI{4000}{\kelvin}, well above the new floor. More potassium will not move it either. For a trace alkali in a neutral gas Saha gives $n_e \propto \sqrt{n_K}$, so $Rm$ climbs only as the square root of the seed fraction, and lifting $Rm$ at \SI{3000}{\kelvin} to its \SI{4000}{\kelvin} value would take sixty-nine times the potassium, which is most of the slug. Sodium would work in the same role but holds the cold end about \SI{430}{\kelvin} less well, so matching 1\% potassium takes 2.5\% sodium at \SI{5000}{\kelvin} and 12.7\% at \SI{3000}{\kelvin}. One condition sits under all of it. Recombination freezes if the fireball is thinner than about \SI{0.01}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, and we have not computed the density a real pulse produces. That number belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation already owed in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}.

Venting the slug as steam into a bag before the collision buys one more thing, and it is the field strength. Spreading the same mass through more volume lowers its density and therefore its pressure, and the field only has to stand off pressure. It does not make the plume hotter. Temperature depends on energy per kilogram, which the bag does not change. The field needed falls as the bag radius to the power of three halves, so the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug a \SI{25}{\kilogram} PuffSat vaporizes at $k = 8.5$ wants \SI{21}{\tesla} at \SI{1.8}{\meter} and \SI{2.0}{\tesla} at \SI{8.7}{\meter}. The bag cannot simply be made enormous, because the same expansion that lowers the pressure raises the surface area, and the plume radiates from that surface. Loss grows as the radius squared. It also has to stay opaque, since a plume thin enough to see through radiates from its whole volume rather than only its skin.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Bag sizing for the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug of a \SI{25}{\kilogram} PuffSat at $k = 8.5$, with the plume at \SI{15000}{\kelvin}. This is a sweep of enclosed volume, quoted as the radius of the sphere holding it, so it is independent of the shape the volume is finally poured into; \autoref{tab:axial_bag} handles shape. The field falls with size and the radiative loss rises with it. The two field columns bracket the burn. The first is the coldest pulse the fleet flies, \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing, where the plume sits at \SI{14700}{\kelvin} and is only \SI{5}{\percent} ionized. The second is the hottest, \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, where energy conservation and Saha together give \SI{26200}{\kelvin} and \SI{57}{\percent}. The mist column is the coldest the mist can sit and still hold together as vapor at that density, from \autoref{tab:bag_state}, and it depends on the bag before the collision rather than on the plume after it. The loss column assumes the plume holds \SI{15000}{\kelvin} for the full expansion, so it is an upper bound.}
    \label{tab:bag_sizing}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Bag radius} & \textbf{Density} & \textbf{Field, coldest pulse} & \textbf{Field, hottest pulse} & \textbf{Energy radiated} & \textbf{Coldest mist} \\\hline
        \SI{1.8}{\meter} & \SI{8.72}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} & \SI{21.3}{\tesla} & \SI{35.4}{\tesla} & 0.1\% & \SI{372}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{3.5}{\meter} & \num{1.19} & \SI{7.9}{\tesla} & \SI{13.1}{\tesla} & 0.5\% & \SI{332}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{5.4}{\meter} & \num{0.32} & \SI{4.1}{\tesla} & \SI{6.8}{\tesla} & 1.2\% & \SI{306}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{8.7}{\meter} & \num{0.077} & \SI{2.0}{\tesla} & \SI{3.3}{\tesla} & 3.0\% & \SI{281}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{17.5}{\meter} & \num{0.0095} & \SI{0.70}{\tesla} & \SI{1.16}{\tesla} & 12\% & \SI{250}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{28}{\meter} & \num{0.0023} & \SI{0.35}{\tesla} & \SI{0.58}{\tesla} & 31\% & \SI{228}{\kelvin} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

At this slug mass the two bounds trade places. Radiative loss is the surface area divided by the energy inside, and area grows more slowly than volume, so the loss weakens as the cube root of slug mass. A \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug at \SI{8.7}{\meter} gives up 3\% where a \SI{5}{\kilogram} one at the same density gives up 11\%. Opacity binds instead. At the pessimistic absorption coefficient of \SI{0.5}{\square\meter\per\kilogram} the plume stops being optically thick past about \SI{7}{\meter}, and a plume that has gone thin radiates from its whole volume rather than from its skin. The usable band is therefore roughly three and a half to seven meters, needing three to eight tesla at the coldest pulse and five to thirteen at the hottest. A third limit caps it independently, and it is the one that ends up reshaping the bag. The arriving PuffSat has to sweep the whole slug, and once the bag is much wider than the incoming projectile the impactor punches a column through it and couples only to the mass in that column. Numbers make that worse than it sounds. Areal density, the mass per unit area lying along a path, is the quantity that decides whether one body stops another. A \SI{5.4}{\meter} sphere holding \SI{213}{\kilogram} runs at \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, so crossing it presents only \SI{3.5}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}, while a \SI{5}{\centi\meter} rod of ice massing \SI{25}{\kilogram} is \SI{3183}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}. The rod outweighs what it meets by a factor of nine hundred. Newtonian drag over the whole crossing is $\rho v A \ell$, about \SI{1550}{\kilogram\meter\per\second} against the \num{1.4e6} the rod carries. It surrenders a tenth of a percent of its momentum, leaves the far side intact, and has swept \SI{27}{\gram} of the \SI{213}{\kilogram}. That is a needle through fog. \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} is what to do about it.

One thing the bag does not buy is a lighter nozzle, and the reason is one line of gas law. The field has to stand off the plume, so the magnetic pressure $B^2/2\mu_0$ equals the plume's pressure $P$. The energy stored in a field of that strength filling a volume $V$ is $B^2V/2\mu_0$, which is the same product $PV$. The ideal gas law then finishes it, because $PV = nR_gT$, and neither the number of moles nor the temperature has any idea how big the bag is. Dissociated water is three particles per \SI{18}{\gram}, so \SI{213}{\kilogram} of slug is \SI{35500}{\mole}, and at \SI{15000}{\kelvin} that is \SI{4.43}{\giga\joule}. The stored field energy is that number at every row of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing}, fixed before the bag's size is chosen at all. On the mass rule of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} the coils weigh the same. What a bigger bag buys is a weaker peak field, and since the mechanical load on a coil grows as the square of the field, that is worth having on its own.

That \SI{4.43}{\giga\joule} assumes the plume is dissociated but not ionized, which is all \SI{15000}{\kelvin} buys. The collision delivers more than that. Dissipated energy per kilogram of merged blob is $\tfrac{1}{2}kw^2/(1+k)^2$, which at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and $k = 8.5$ is \SI{265}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, against the \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} that vaporizing, dissociating and heating to \SI{15000}{\kelvin} costs. The leftover \SI{180}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} does not mostly go into temperature. Stripping one electron from each of water's three atoms takes \SI{13.6}{\electronvolt} apiece, or \SI{219}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for the molecule, so ionization absorbs the excess the way boiling absorbs heat from a pot without warming it \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. Dividing \num{180} by \num{219} would suggest the plume ends up four fifths ionized, and that is wrong, because the gas does not get to choose its ionization fraction independently of its temperature. Saha's equation fixes the fraction once temperature and density are given, and it is stingy at these densities \cite{hansen_kawaler_trimble_stellar_interiors}. At \SI{15000}{\kelvin} and \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} it allows only \SI{5.9}{\percent}. The plume cannot pour \SI{180}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} into an ionization channel that narrow, so the temperature has to climb until the channel opens. Solving energy conservation and Saha together is a two-line root find, and at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it lands at \SI{26200}{\kelvin} and \SI{57}{\percent} ionized, splitting the budget as \SI{86}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of thermal energy against \SI{125}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of stripped electrons.

That state moves across the burn, which turns out to rescue the original assumption at the end that matters. The craft accelerates into the oncoming wave, so closing speed falls from \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on the slowest cycle, and the plume cools with it: \SI{22400}{\kelvin} at \SI{37}{\percent} ionized when the closing speed is \SI{65}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{19400}{\kelvin} at \SI{22}{\percent} at \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and \SI{14700}{\kelvin} at \SI{5.3}{\percent} at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The last of those is the coldest pulse the fleet has to fly, and at that point \SI{15000}{\kelvin} with negligible ionization is not an assumption at all. It is the answer.

What ionization changes is the particle count, because every atom that loses an electron becomes two particles, and pressure counts particles rather than mass. Water goes from three particles per molecule to $3(1+f)$. Multiplying that by the temperature ratio gives a pressure \num{2.75} times the assumed value at the hottest pulse and \num{1.03} times it at the coldest, so the field runs from \num{1.02} to \num{1.66} times \autoref{tab:bag_sizing}'s original column and the stored energy from \SIrange{4.6}{12.2}{\giga\joule}. The table therefore brackets the burn rather than quoting a point. Pinning it properly, including the second ionization of oxygen that this treatment ignores, needs the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation this section keeps owing.

The bag is not free either, and its price is a short piece of arithmetic worth doing in full. Take a spherical film of thickness $t$ holding pressure $P$ at radius $R$. Cut it in half. The pressure pushing the two halves apart acts over the circle $\pi R^2$, while the film resisting it has cross-section $2\pi R t$, so the stress is $\sigma = PR/2t$ and the thickness needed is $PR/2\sigma$. Multiply that by the surface area $4\pi R^2$ and by the film's density $\rho_f$ to get the film's mass, then use the ideal gas law to write $P$ in terms of the mass of vapor inside, which is $x$ times the slug. Every factor of $R$ cancels:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bag_film_mass}
\frac{m_{\mathrm{bag}}}{m_{\mathrm{slug}}} = F\,\frac{x\,\rho_f R_g T}{M \sigma} \approx 2.6\times10^{-4}\, F\, x\, T
\end{equation}
where $x$ is the fraction of the slug carried as vapor, $T$ is its temperature in kelvin, and $F$ is a pure number set by the bag's shape alone. The numerical form is for the polyethylene fiber chosen below, worked at half its quoted strength to allow for weave and seams. At the sphere's $F = 3/2$ the bag masses about four hundredths of a percent of the slug for every kelvin of steam it holds. The radius does not appear, which makes the film the second quantity in this section a bigger bag leaves alone, the stored field energy being the first.

The shape factor earns its own line because the design below stops using a sphere. A sphere gives $F = 3/2$. A cylinder of the same radius is worse, and the half-cut argument shows why. Slice a cylinder of length $L$ lengthwise and the pressure acts over the rectangle $2RL$, while only two strips of film of combined cross-section $2tL$ hold it together, so $\sigma = PR/t$. That is twice the sphere's stress at the same thickness, and it gives $F = 2$. A capsule, meaning a cylindrical barrel of length $L$ capped at both ends with hemispheres of radius $r$, sits between the two:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bag_shape_factor}
F = \frac{2L + 2r}{L + \tfrac{4}{3}r}
\end{equation}
which runs from $3/2$ when $L$ is zero to $2$ for a long tube. The entire penalty for abandoning the sphere is therefore bounded at $4/3$, and by an aspect ratio of two most of it is already paid. Standard shell formulas reach the same stresses by a longer route \cite{young2011roark}.

\autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} prices the \SI{1000}{\kelvin} steam of the heat-sink argument and finds it unaffordable. A bag of \SI{1000}{\kelvin} steam masses about 40 percent of the slug inside it, and the collision destroys it every pulse. Everything above the latent heat is sensible heat of hot vapor, and sensible heat is exactly what pressurizes the bag, so the heat-sink capacity and the film mass are the same knob turned twice. The bag has to run cold.

What the film is made of sets that coefficient, and the property that matters is specific strength, the tensile strength divided by the density. Toyobo's comparison of high-performance fibers puts high-strength polyethylene at \SI{3.5}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{0.97}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, para-aramid at \SI{2.8}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.45}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, PBO at \SI{5.8}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.54}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, and ordinary polyester at \SI{1.1}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.38}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter} \cite{toyobo_zylon}. Per kilogram carried, polyethylene comes within 4 percent of PBO, beats para-aramid by 1.9 times, and beats high-tenacity polyester by 4.5. Oriented films of the same polymers run lower still than their fibers, so a film-only bag is the expensive way to build this one.

Polyester is the obvious cheap answer and it loses anyway, for a reason that recurs whenever this paper picks a material. A \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug wants \SI{2.8}{\kilogram} of polyethylene fiber against \SI{12.5}{\kilogram} of polyester, and every one of those kilograms was lifted off Earth and is destroyed in the pulse. Delivering the ten extra kilograms costs about \$50{,}000 at the near-term fare of \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}. The feedstock difference between two commodity fibers is a hundred dollars or so. Those are two to three orders of magnitude apart, so no plausible price for either fiber decides this and the mass decides it instead. Read as slug lost per cycle rather than returned, 5.9 percent against 1.3 percent costs roughly 40 percent of the fleet over the chain's eleven cycles. Cheap by the kilogram is not cheap here. Polyethylene fiber is in any case a commodity rather than a specialty item, made at scale for rope, ballistic fabric and fishing line.

Polyethylene is normally disqualified for the one reason that stops applying here. It melts near \SI{423}{\kelvin} \cite{toyobo_zylon}, which is why it belongs nowhere warm, and a \SI{1000}{\kelvin} bag would have destroyed it before the collision got the chance. The mist runs at \SI{306}{\kelvin} on the Jupiter departure and \SI{328}{\kelvin} on the warmer Earth boost, so the same bag design keeps \SI{95}{\kelvin} of margin in the harder of the two cases. Its second standard objection is creep under sustained load, and this bag is loaded for seconds and then vaporized. Sanborn and colleagues measured Dyneema SK76 single fibers from \SI{e-3}{\per\second} to \SI{e3}{\per\second} and found failure strength rising with strain rate to a plateau near \SI{1}{\per\second} \cite{sanborn2014dyneema}, so loading this bag fast is the favorable case rather than the risky one.

Plume chemistry settles the choice. Polyethylene is carbon and hydrogen and nothing else, so a vaporized bag puts no halogen, no sulfur and no metal into the exhaust. That matches decisions the paper has already made. Chlorine is refused for the seed above, lead for the primaries in \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}, and unhalogenated polyethylene is already the pick for the airlock pipe of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} and for the bulk of the pusher-plate slab in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}. A metallized barrier film would break that rule for nothing, because the bag holds only \SI{4.9}{\kilo\pascal} for the seconds between venting and impact, and a plain polyethylene liner leaks slowly enough at that pressure. Weave and barrier can then be the same polymer, welded rather than glued, which is also the practical answer to polyethylene's poor adhesive bonding.

Cold is easier here than the number \SI{373}{\kelvin} suggests, because \SI{373}{\kelvin} is not a property of water. It is the temperature at which water's vapor pressure reaches one atmosphere, and there is no atmosphere. What sets the floor instead is the bag's own density. Vapor condenses back to fog once its pressure passes the saturation pressure at its temperature, so the more room a given mass is given, the colder it can stay a gas. The last column of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} follows that curve down. A bigger bag is a colder bag. Containment runs the other way, and the section should not pretend otherwise. On Earth a bag of steam sitting at ambient pressure carries no load at all, because the air outside pushes back just as hard. In vacuum the film carries the whole difference.

How much of the slug turns to vapor is not a choice. The ship has no radiator, so every joule of waste heat has to go into the water, and once the ice has melted the water answers by boiling. Divide what is left after melting by the latent heat and the vapor fraction falls out. The saturation curve then fixes the temperature, the temperature fixes the pressure, and \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} fixes the film. \autoref{tab:bag_state} runs that chain for the \SI{5.4}{\meter} row.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The bag's state is derived, not chosen. Everything below the double rule is an output. Per kilogram of slug, for the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug in a \SI{5.4}{\meter} bag. The waste heat is held the same across both columns so that only the storage temperature varies. The field-leak entry is the least settled number here and is discussed below.}
    \label{tab:bag_state}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Per \si{\kilogram} of slug} & \textbf{Jupiter, \SI{122}{\kelvin}} & \textbf{Earth, \SI{278}{\kelvin}} \\\hline
        Blackbody light intercepted (1.2\% radiated, a tenth of the sky) & \SI{0.10}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.10}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        Field leaking through the plume (4.4\% of \SI{20.8}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}) & \SI{0.89}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.89}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        \textbf{Waste heat to be absorbed} & \textbf{\SI{0.99}{\mega\joule}} & \textbf{\SI{0.99}{\mega\joule}} \\\hline
        Warming and melting the slug up to liquid & \SI{-0.73}{\mega\joule} & \SI{-0.21}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        Left over to boil water & \SI{0.26}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.78}{\mega\joule} \\\hline\hline
        Vapor fraction $x$ & \num{0.11} & \num{0.33} \\\hline
        Temperature, from the saturation curve & \SI{306}{\kelvin} & \SI{328}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        Pressure & \SI{4.9}{\kilo\pascal} & \SI{16}{\kilo\pascal} \\\hline
        Bag film, from \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} & 1.3\%, \SI{2.8}{\kilogram} & 4.2\%, \SI{8.9}{\kilogram} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The two columns differ only in the temperature the slug was stored at, and the Earth-side bag comes out three times heavier for it. Cold storage is doing real work. Ice at \SI{122}{\kelvin} absorbs \SI{0.73}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} on its way to liquid, which is heat that never reaches the boiling step and so never turns into pressure. The fix for the warm end is machinery this paper already flies. A detachable multilayer sunshade of the kind described in \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} takes a shaded body at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} well below freezing, so a slug parked behind one before the Earth boost arrives at the burn frozen and recovers the same \SI{0.73}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. It is not fast. Rejecting \SI{156}{\mega\joule} from a \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug through a couple of square meters at \SI{150}{\kelvin} takes about a month, which suits mass that is already parked between delivery and use and does not suit mass on a short turnaround. Shading the slug is therefore a scheduling choice rather than a hardware one, and the two columns bracket what it is worth.

The cold that helps the slug is a hazard everywhere else on the ship, which has to hold two temperatures at once. Electronics stop working well before \SI{122}{\kelvin}. The coils want to be colder than that, not warmer. Both sit on the same vehicle, and the answer is to zone it. Multilayer insulation, a stack of thin reflective sheets separated by vacuum, blocks radiative heat flow well enough that a warm bus and a cold magnet can share a structure. What the warm side then needs is power.

Sunlight at Jupiter is weak rather than absent. Flux falls as the inverse square of distance from the Sun, so at \SI{5.2}{\astronomicalunit} it is about a twenty-seventh of Earth's \SI{1361}{\watt\per\square\meter}, near \SI{50}{\watt\per\square\meter}. That is enough, and it has been flown rather than argued. Juno reached Jupiter on solar power with no radioisotope generator aboard, using three nine-meter arrays carrying \num{18698} cells that make about \SI{14}{\kilo\watt} at Earth and about \SI{500}{\watt} at Jupiter \cite{bolton2017juno, jpl_juno_solar_record}. Galileo, the only earlier Jupiter orbiter, carried radioisotope generators instead. Two more solar-powered Jupiter missions, JUICE and Europa Clipper, are in cruise, so the approach is being repeated rather than left as a one-off.

Concentrators buy the same power from less array. A lens or a mirror gathers sunlight over a wide aperture and delivers it onto a small cell, so the expensive part scales with the concentrated area rather than the collecting area. Deep Space 1 flew line-focus silicone Fresnel lenses at about eight times concentration and ran both the spacecraft and its ion engine on them for the mission's thirty-eight months \cite{stella2000scarlet}. Mirrors are the other option, and the non-imaging kind is worth preferring here. A concentrator that forms no image accepts light across a wider range of incidence angles than one focusing a sharp image, so it tolerates looser Sun-pointing. A ship whose attitude is disturbed by pulses has reason to want that.

Power at Jupiter also buys cryogenics more cheaply than the same power would at Earth, and the reason is the radiator on the warm end. A refrigerator's minimum work depends on the gap it pumps heat across, as the ratio of that gap to the cold temperature. Lifting a joule from the \SI{77}{\kelvin} of liquid nitrogen to a \SI{300}{\kelvin} radiator costs \SI{2.9}{\joule} at the Carnot limit. At Jupiter a shaded radiator settles near \SI{150}{\kelvin} instead, and the same joule costs \SI{0.95}{\joule}, three times less. The distance that makes the electronics need a heater is what makes the magnet affordable to keep cold, and multilayer insulation is what lets one ship have both.

Two features of that table deserve more than a row each. The first is that light turns out to be the small term. The blackbody figure is the eye-catching one, but at this bag size the plume gives up only 1.2\% of its energy as light, while the field leaks through it at 4.4\%. The nozzle's heat problem is resistive rather than radiative by about nine to one, which is worth knowing because the two are fixed by different things. Leakage falls as the square of the bag radius through the magnetic diffusion time, so the same growth that makes the bag colder also makes it tighter magnetically. The leak fraction is the least settled number in the table and rests on a conductivity we have estimated rather than measured, so treat the nine-to-one split as an ordering rather than a ratio. Ionization makes it less settled rather than more, and the reason is that the leak is an integral over an expansion that passes through two very different regimes. The leak is field energy diffusing into the plasma, and it is the product of a fraction and the stored energy the field holds. The stored energy is now pinned, and it rises. Designing the magnet for the hottest pulse puts it at \SI{12.2}{\giga\joule} rather than \SI{4.43}{\giga\joule}. The fraction is not pinned, and it moves the other way early in the expansion. While the fireball is at \SI{26000}{\kelvin} and more than half ionized, water supplies about \num{1.7} electrons per molecule against the \num{0.005} that fully ionized \SI{1}{\percent} potassium contributes, so the plume is some four hundred times more conductive than the seeded estimate and barely leaks at all. The seed only becomes the conductor later, once the water has recombined and the plume has fallen into the \SIrange{3000}{6000}{\kelvin} window of \autoref{tab:seed_window}, which is also where the magnetic Reynolds number drops toward unity and most of the leaking must happen.

Which regime dominates the integral decides whether the flown \SI{0.89}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} is three times too large or three times too small, and we cannot settle it here. What we can do is say what the integral is. Since the leaked share is $1/Rm$, the flown 4.4\% corresponds to $Rm \approx 23$, which sits between the \SI{3000}{\kelvin} and \SI{4000}{\kelvin} rows of \autoref{tab:seed_window}. The whole question is therefore how long the plume lingers below \SI{4000}{\kelvin} while the field is still working, which is a residence time rather than a field calculation. The consequence is worth stating plainly, because it is the one place in this section where the answer would change the design rather than adjust it. Hold the fraction at \SI{4.4}{\percent} and scale only the stored energy, and the waste heat reaches \SI{2.62}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, five sixths of the slug flashes to vapor, the mist runs at \SI{352}{\kelvin} rather than \SI{306}{\kelvin}, and the bag masses \SI{31}{\kilogram} instead of \SI{3.7}{\kilogram}. Polyethylene still survives that, with \SI{71}{\kelvin} of margin, but a bag costing \SI{15}{\percent} of the slug every pulse is a different vehicle from one costing \SI{1.7}{\percent}. \autoref{tab:bag_state} is quoted at the seeded-conductivity figure because that is the state the plume spends most of its expansion in. The radiation-hydrodynamic calculation has to check it against that upper bound before any of the film masses here are treated as settled.

The second is that a two-phase mist regulates itself, which no single-phase coolant can. Liquid and vapor sitting together are locked to the saturation curve, so extra heat has nowhere to go except into boiling more water. The temperature does not move. If the nozzle runs hotter than designed, the bag answers with a larger vapor fraction at the same \SI{306}{\kelvin} and only the pressure creeps up. A tank of gas would simply get hotter and push harder on a film sized for something cooler.

The mist settles one more thing, and it is the seed. Potassium carbonate does not evaporate. It has no vapor pressure worth the name below the roughly \SI{1170}{\kelvin} at which it decomposes, so boiling the slug dry would strand the seed in the tank and send clean steam to the collision. Venting a mist leaves two thirds to nine tenths of the slug as liquid droplets, and each droplet carries its dissolved carbonate along. The concentration rises by a tenth to a half, which changes nothing. The claim that the seed needs no feed system of its own therefore survives, but the reason it survives is the mist rather than the solubility.

The same argument may carry to the near-Sun cycle, on one side of it. The prograde three quarters of the split in \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial} carry their reaction mass aboard a shielded rocket, so water is admissible there and brings the same replacement for radiators to a chamber running far hotter than this one. The retrograde quarter cannot use it. Those projectiles are released to fly their own Sun-grazing orbit with no shield of their own, which is why \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} builds them from carbon and ceramic instead. Water would be gone long before periapsis. We have not priced a near-Sun slug and coolant budget, so this is an observation rather than a result.

\subsubsection{A Needle Through Fog}\label{sec:needle_through_fog}
There are two ways to make a projectile couple to a slug it currently ignores. Widen the projectile until it covers the bag, or narrow the bag until it matches the projectile. The first is the obvious one and it loses, for a reason that has nothing to do with mass.

A head-on nozzle is a chamber with a hole in the end the projectile came through. Roughly half the collision's thermal energy starts out moving back toward that hole (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}), and the field's job is to turn that half around. Anything left open leaks it. Spreading \SI{25}{\kilogram} of impactor across a \SI{3}{\meter} bore means an opening as wide as the chamber, which is not a mirror at all. Keeping the impactor compact means an opening of about \SI{0.15}{\meter} into a bore of \SI{28}{\square\meter}, which is \SI{0.25}{\percent} open. \autoref{tab:two_leg_growth} prices what that is worth. Moving $e_2$ from 0.6 to 0.8 multiplies the fleet by fourteen over eleven cycles. No amount of bag film or slug water competes with that, so the projectile stays compact and the bag has to change shape.

Narrowing the bag is cheaper than it looks, because of the invariance found above. The standoff volume is set by $PV = nR_gT$ and does not care about shape, so pouring the same \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} into a long tube instead of a ball gives a bore of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bore_from_length}
r = \sqrt{\frac{V}{\pi \ell}}
\end{equation}
for a column of length $\ell$. Bore falls as the inverse square root of length. What it costs is conductor. A solenoid of length $\ell$ making field $B$ needs $NI = B\ell/\mu_0$ ampere-turns, and each turn is $2\pi r$ of tape, so the tape mass runs as $B r \ell$, the field times the area of the magnetic wall being built \cite{rebco_tapes}. Substituting \autoref{eq:bore_from_length} makes that $B\sqrt{V\ell/\pi}$, which grows as the square root of length. \textbf{Bore and conductor therefore trade inversely, one for one. Halving the bore doubles the tape.} Structure mass does not enter, because the virial floor of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} tracks contained energy, and contained energy is the $nR_gT$ that shape leaves alone.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Pouring the same \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} of standoff volume into columns of different length. Bore falls as $\ell^{-1/2}$ and conductor rises as $\ell^{1/2}$, both normalized to the \SI{5.4}{\meter} sphere. $F$ is the shape factor of \autoref{eq:bag_shape_factor} and the film column follows \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} at the flown vapor state.}
    \label{tab:axial_bag}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Column length} & \textbf{Bore radius} & \textbf{Conductor} & \textbf{$F$} & \textbf{Film} \\\hline
        \SI{10.8}{\meter} (sphere) & \SI{5.40}{\meter} & 1.00 & 1.50 & \SI{2.8}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{16}{\meter} & \SI{3.62}{\meter} & 0.99 & 1.82 & \SI{3.4}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{23}{\meter} & \SI{3.02}{\meter} & 1.19 & 1.90 & \SI{3.6}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{32}{\meter} & \SI{2.56}{\meter} & 1.41 & 1.94 & \SI{3.6}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{50}{\meter} & \SI{2.05}{\meter} & 1.76 & 1.97 & \SI{3.7}{\kilogram} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Nothing in \autoref{tab:axial_bag} picks a row, so something outside the physics has to. We let the launch envelope decide. A \SI{5.4}{\meter} bore means coils about \SI{11}{\meter} across, which has to be assembled in orbit. A \SI{3}{\meter} bore means about \SI{7}{\meter} across, which fits an \SI{8}{\meter} fairing and flies up built. Length is the cheap dimension for a rocket to carry and diameter is the expensive one, and \autoref{sec:space_mortgages} already sells the seed vehicle as a ship one Starship can loft. That fixes the column at \SI{23}{\meter} with a \SI{3.02}{\meter} bore, for a fifth more conductor and \SI{0.8}{\kilogram} more film.

The film is the part worth being explicit about, because it is the number a reader expects to be large. It is not. The shape factor moves the bag from \SI{2.8}{\kilogram} to \SI{3.6}{\kilogram}, and the plug described next moves it to \SI{3.7}{\kilogram}, which is \SI{1.7}{\percent} of the slug. That is under a third of what the polyester bag rejected earlier would have cost, and it buys a bore small enough to launch. The slug itself does not grow at all. The ratio $k$ comes from the ignition window and the launch budget, not from the bag's shape.

A narrow bag alone still does not solve the needle problem, because a rod crossing \SI{23}{\meter} of \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} mist meets \SI{7.4}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} and is still nine hundred times denser than that. What couples the two is a solid plug of ice at the end of the column where the projectile enters. Now the projectile hits something of its own density, and the governing rule is the hydrodynamic penetration law: a projectile striking a target of comparable density erodes over a depth of about its own length times $\sqrt{\rho_p/\rho_t}$ \cite{birkhoff1948lined}. Ice on ice makes that square root equal to one, so the plug has to be at least as thick as the projectile is long. A \SI{25}{\kilogram} ice rod \SI{0.1}{\meter} in radius is \SI{0.87}{\meter} long, so a plug of the same width and half the mass is only \SI{0.43}{\meter} thick and lets a residual jet through into the mist. We size the plug at \SI{37.5}{\kilogram}, half again the projectile's mass, and let the extra go into width, since the crater runs wider than the rod.

The plug is close to free twice over. It adds nothing to the film, because \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} counts only the vapor fraction $x$ and condensed water exerts no pressure on the bag. And it works as a heat sink on the way to being a target. The plug has to stay condensed, not frozen, so it can absorb the full \SI{0.73}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of warming and melting from \SI{122}{\kelvin} without becoming vapor. Over \SI{37.5}{\kilogram} that is \SI{27}{\mega\joule} of the pulse's \SI{211}{\mega\joule} waste-heat bill removed before the boiling step ever sees it. The vapor mass lands at \SI{24.5}{\kilogram} against \SI{23.4}{\kilogram} without the plug, and the saturation curve moves the mist from \SI{306}{\kelvin} to \SI{307}{\kelvin}. Polyethylene keeps \SI{116}{\kelvin} of margin below its melting point.

Ice is also the right material for the projectile, and the argument is chemistry rather than cost. Making the projectile out of the same substance as the slug leaves the merged blob a single material, so \autoref{tab:seed_window} and the \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} ignition budget describe all \SI{238}{\kilogram} instead of \SI{213}{\kilogram} plus a contaminant. Polyethylene is the obvious alternative and it breaks the recombination loan that \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} depends on. \SI{25}{\kilogram} of polyethylene carries \SI{21.4}{\kilogram} of carbon, which is \SI{1.78}{\kilo\mole}, and carbon scavenges oxygen into carbon monoxide. The C--O bond is the strongest in diatomic chemistry at \SI{11.1}{\electronvolt} \cite{crc_handbook}, so that oxygen does not come back on a hundred-microsecond expansion. Fifteen percent of the slug's oxygen never re-forms water, and since the loan is \SI{50.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} out of an \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} budget, that costs about nine percent of the ignition energy for nothing. Ice's own weakness is the cruise rather than the impact. A bare ice body near \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} does not sit at the \SI{278}{\kelvin} a rock would. It cools until the latent heat carried off by sublimation, plus what it radiates, balances the sunlight it absorbs. Setting \SI{340}{\watt\per\square\meter} of averaged absorbed flux against $\sigma T^4$ plus $L$ times the sublimation rate puts that balance near \SI{194}{\kelvin}, where ice's vapor pressure is \SI{0.064}{\pascal} \cite{marti1993ice} and the free-evaporation rate is \SI{7.4}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} per day. A \SI{25}{\kilogram} rod carries about \SI{55}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}. It would be gone in a week. Behind the detachable multilayer sunshade already flown in \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} the projectile sits near \SI{150}{\kelvin}, where the same arithmetic gives \SI{0.58}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} across a two-year cruise, about \SI{1.4}{\percent} of the rod. Not free, but affordable. Guidance hardware and structure still want a polymer, so the projectile is a hybrid and the question is the ratio rather than the choice.

One consequence of the plug reaches past the bag and into the magnet. Concentrating the collision at one end means the fireball sweeps the mist as a snowplow rather than merging with all of it at once, and a snowplow's pressure falls as it goes, because it is spreading a fixed amount of dissipated energy through a growing volume. Working \autoref{eq:bore_from_length}'s geometry against the swept mass gives a field requirement near \SI{20}{\tesla} a meter in, \SI{12}{\tesla} at \SI{3}{\meter}, \SI{9}{\tesla} at \SI{6}{\meter}, and \SI{5}{\tesla} at the exit. That is not a complication, it is the shape a nozzle is supposed to have, with a strong field at the chamber and a weak one at the throat, which the spherical bag never produced. The high end sits at the working point commercial high-temperature superconducting magnets are already being built to, about \SI{20}{\tesla} at \SI{20}{\kelvin} \cite{creely2020sparc}. The field must be graded in space and static in time. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} keeps induced current out of the superconductor on purpose, so a coil field that deliberately changes during a pulse would break the quench design the same section argues for.

\subsubsection{Two Nozzles Beat a Nozzle and a Plate}\label{sec:two_leg_nozzle}
The cycle collides twice, and the two collisions face opposite ways. On the Jupiter departure the PuffSat arrives head-on, so its momentum points backward. A plate there would brake the craft, and vaporizing a slug is the only way to get forward thrust rather than an improvement on one. The Earth boost is an overtake. The returning PuffSat catches the craft from behind, its momentum already points where the craft is going, and a plate need only turn it around. Write $w$ for the closing speed, meaning how fast the two approach each other, and $k$ for the slug ratio introduced in \autoref{sec:watering_it_down}. An ideal nozzle in an overtake delivers $1+\sqrt{1+k}$ times the arriving momentum, against $\sqrt{1+k}-1$ head-on. Set $k$ to zero in the overtake and the first expression gives exactly 2, a perfect elastic bounce, which is what a plate does. That coincidence is arithmetic and not physics. A magnetic field steers a conductor, and a slug that vanishes dissipates nothing in the merge, so there is no plasma to steer. A plate is a genuinely different device rather than a nozzle run empty.

What stops $k$ from rising without limit is more interesting than a simple ceiling. Only the dissipated share of the collision heats the blob, and that share is $k/(1+k)$ spread over $1+k$ kilograms, so the energy per kilogram peaks at $k=1$ and falls away on both sides. Too much slug spreads a fixed energy too thin. Too little dissipates almost nothing and leaves the plume cold. The admissible slug ratios are therefore a closed interval rather than everything below a maximum, and $k=0$ sits outside it, which is the second reason the plate is not the small-slug limit of a nozzle. The interval has to hold at the coldest moment of the burn, not the hottest. The push accelerates the craft from rest to \SI{10.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the craft climbs toward the oncoming wave and the closing speed falls as the burn proceeds. The last pulse is the coldest one. One nozzle loading flies all eleven cycles, so the fleet must take the intersection over every cycle, and the slowest cycle decides.

The cadence is what makes some cycles slow. A round trip squeezed into two Earth-Jupiter synodic periods needs a more energetic transfer, which costs more to depart on and comes home faster. Three synodic periods relax the schedule at both ends. Seven of the eleven flown cycles take the shorter route and four take the longer, and the two groups do not overlap in speed. The two-synodic returns close between \SIrange{61.83}{65.13}{\kilo\meter\per\second} after departure burns of \SIrange{6.84}{7.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The three-synodic returns close between \SIrange{56.53}{57.43}{\kilo\meter\per\second} after burns of only \SIrange{5.32}{5.54}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The slowest of them starts its push at \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and ends it at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, where the plume will light only for $k$ between 0.098 and 10.21. The best two-synodic cycle would allow 15.32 on its own. The cheap-to-leave cycles are the ones that cost the fleet a third of its slug ratio.

Plasma temperature turns out not to be what binds. The slug has to be launched from Earth, and both legs draw on the same launched kilograms. Charging two thirds of liftoff as the launcher's own propellant and a quarter of the remainder as its dry mass leaves a quarter of liftoff arriving at the intercept point, and we require the loop to return at least a fifteenth of liftoff to be worth flying against a reusable rocket. Across the sixty-four combinations swept, that budget is what stops $k$ in fifty-six of them, while the ignition window binds in one. A temperature of \SI{15000}{\kelvin} is a low bar for a \SI{46}{\kilo\meter\per\second} impact. This charge lands on the pusher plate too, which had never been asked to pay it, and it moves the top two rows of \autoref{tab:space_mortgage_growth} without touching anything below them.

    \label{tab:two_leg_growth}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline

\autoref{tab:equivalent_plate} settles the comparison by converting every nozzle into the plate it would be worth. Read down any column and the pattern is the same, so the answer turns on $e_1$ alone and barely on $e_2$. Read the diagonal and the nozzle wins: at every recovery above about 0.3 it is worth a strictly better plate than that same number. Compounded over the chain the margin is large, because these are annual rates and the section's own arithmetic applies. A nozzle and a plate both at 0.6 return \num{6.63e4} against \num{7827}, a factor of 8.5 in delivered mass, and both at 0.8 return \num{2.83e7} against \num{7.83e5}, a factor of 36. The win is not universal. At 0.25 the launch budget crushes $k$ onto the bottom of the ignition window and the nozzle loses outright, returning \num{0.000133} against the plate's \num{0.000309}. The advantage switches on near 0.3 and compounds from there.

Whether that is the fair comparison rests on a speed, and for a while we compared against the wrong one. Against the $f=0.8$ plate used everywhere else here, the nozzle needs $e_1$ near 0.6 at a good departure leg and near 0.7 at a mediocre one, which is a real hurdle. The figure $f=0.8$ came from a hydrodynamic sweep across \SIrange{3.2}{16}{\kilo\meter\per\second} (\autoref{sec:mass_fraction}), while the closing speeds on this leg run from \SIrange{45}{65}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, three to four times faster. Nothing rebounds elastically at \SI{46}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the plate appeared to have been credited with a number from a regime it never encounters.

The plate in \autoref{tab:equivalent_plate} indeed never encounters it, because that plate carries no slug, and the tempting fix is to give it one. Work the momentum and the fix evaporates. Closing speed is not impact speed, and conservation of momentum separates them. One kilogram arriving at closing speed $w$ merges with $k$ kilograms sitting at rest, and the merged blob carries all of the incoming momentum spread over $1+k$ times the mass, so it drifts at $w/(1+k)$. At $w = \SI{56}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ and $k = 8.5$ that is \SI{5.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, comfortably inside the sweep that measured $f$ rather than four times above it. But the plate is pushed by what it turns around, and what it turns around is $(1+k)$ kilograms moving at $w/(1+k)$. The two factors cancel exactly. A slugged plate delivers $2f\,mw$, which is what an unslugged plate delivers, because the slug was sitting still and brought no momentum with it. Carrying \SI{8.5}{\kilogram} of water for every kilogram of projectile buys the plate a gentler impact and not one newton-second of thrust.

That cancellation is the cleanest statement of why the two devices differ, and it is worth more than the comparison it settles. A plate reverses bulk momentum and can do nothing else, so the slug is dead weight to it. A nozzle is pushed by the collision's dissipated energy $\tfrac{1}{2}\mu w^2$, where the reduced mass $\mu = mk/(1+k)$ climbs with every kilogram of slug added. The slug is the nozzle's food and the plate cannot eat it. This section's opening expression says the same thing in one line, since setting $k$ to zero in the overtake gives exactly $2$, which is the plate. \autoref{tab:equivalent_plate} is therefore the fair comparison after all, and the objection above stands as written. The plate really is being asked to survive \SI{46}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and it has no way to buy its way out.

What the arithmetic does change is a claim the nozzle should not be allowed to make. The nozzle's own reflecting surface sees the merged blob, not the projectile, so it is not facing \SI{46}{\kilo\meter\per\second} either. It faces \SI{5.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of drift with a thermal spread on top of it, since the blob is hot and its particles move relative to its center of mass. Dividing the dissipated energy by the blob's mass gives \SI{150}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} at the slowest cycle's opening speed, and a gas converting all of that to directed motion expands at $\sqrt{2u} = \SI{17}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$, so material reaches the field at roughly \SIrange{11}{23}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. That is the regime a plate is tested in. The nozzle's advantage here is that it collects the thermal three quarters rather than that it survives a speed the plate cannot, and a field touches nothing and cannot ablate at all. Two further effects pull in opposite directions and neither is modeled. In the nozzle's favor, the wrong-way drift it must reverse carries only 8.9\% of the blob's energy at the fleet ceiling, so the overtake costs it little. Against it, the overtake puts the impactor entrance and the exhaust exit at the same end of the ship, so each arriving PuffSat flies up the previous shot's plume. That is the largest thing missing from this model, and the head-on leg has no equivalent, since there the impactor enters the front and the exhaust leaves the back. Both tables also assume the dissociation energy comes back during expansion, which \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} argues for and which fails if the fireball is thinner than \SI{0.01}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}. The slug is water by choice rather than by optimization. The impulse law has no dependence on molar mass, so left to itself the model would pick whatever ionizes cheapest per kilogram and ignore what the nozzle and the cycle actually need.


One thing the two-nozzle answer owes is a picture of the hardware, because the two legs ask for opposite shapes. Thrust points prograde on both legs, so the exhaust leaves retrograde on both legs, so the throat faces the same way twice. On the departure the projectile enters the far end through a \SI{0.15}{\meter} aperture and the plume leaves through the throat, making a tube open at both ends. On the growth push the projectile enters through the throat itself, and whatever comes back out leaves the same way, making a cup closed at the ship end. The first device expands a flow already going the right way. The second reverses one going the wrong way. Those are different jobs for the same field.

They are not, though, different field profiles. Both want a strong field at the ship end and a weak one at the throat, because on both legs the collision products end up at the ship end and the exhaust has to leave from the other. That is a useful coincidence, and it means the ship carries one bore with one gradient rather than two magnets pointing opposite ways.

The reversal on the growth push is worth checking against the usual intuition, because the usual intuition does not apply. A magnetic mirror confines particles by the loss cone, meaning that a particle spiralling along a field line is turned back unless its velocity happens to point too closely along the line, and the escaping fraction follows from the ratio of the strong field to the weak one \cite{chen2016plasma}. That picture assumes particles orbit without colliding. Ours collide constantly. At \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} the dissociated plume holds \SI{3.2e25}{\per\cubic\meter} particles, and the molecules alone are \SI{1.1e25}{\per\cubic\meter}, about \SI{40}{\percent} of sea-level air. A neutral cross-section of \SI{e-19}{\square\meter} gives a mean free path near \SI{0.2}{\micro\meter} against a \SI{3}{\meter} bore, so the Knudsen number, the ratio of mean free path to the size of the container, is about \num{7e-8}. Ionization only shortens the path further, since Coulomb collisions reach much further than hard-sphere ones. This is a fluid, not a collection of orbiting particles, and the loss cone refills faster than it can empty. Mirror ratio is the wrong variable.

For a fluid the field is a wall, and the condition is pressure. The wall holds if $B^2/2\mu_0$ exceeds what the plume pushes with, which is its static pressure plus its ram pressure $\rho v^2$, the extra push a moving fluid delivers by being stopped. Their ratio is $Mv^2/(\gamma-1)E$, and it decides where the plug can sit. Put the plug at the ship end, where the projectile would reach it cleanly, and the merged \SI{62.5}{\kilogram} arrives at \SI{22.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} with all \SI{23.5}{\giga\joule} of its dissipated energy intact. The ratio is 6.7, the wall sees \SI{1.26}{\giga\pascal}, and it would take \SI{56}{\tesla}. Put the plug at the throat end instead and the fireball snowplows the whole \SI{23}{\meter} of mist before it meets the wall, arriving as \SI{238}{\kilogram} at \SI{5.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} spread through \SI{660}{\cubic\meter}. The ratio falls to 1.17, the wall sees \SI{23}{\mega\pascal}, and \SI{7.6}{\tesla} is enough. The plug therefore sits at the projectile's entrance on both legs, which is the far end on the departure and the throat end on the growth push.

What does leak is not the loss cone but Rayleigh--Taylor instability, the growth of fingers when a dense fluid is supported against acceleration by a light one \cite{chandrasekhar1961hydrodynamic}. Decelerating a plume with a field is exactly that case, since the field has no mass at all. The arithmetic is unusually clean. A ripple of wavelength $\lambda$ grows at $\gamma = \sqrt{kg}$ with $k = 2\pi/\lambda$, the stopping time is $v/a$, and writing the deceleration as $a = v^2/2d$ over a stopping distance $d$ makes the speed cancel:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rt_efolds}
\gamma t = \sqrt{2kd} = \sqrt{\frac{4\pi d}{\lambda}}
\end{equation}
A \SI{30}{\centi\meter} ripple grows \num{11} e-folds over a \SI{3}{\meter} stop and \num{4.6} over a \SI{0.5}{\meter} one. Stopping the plume gently is worse than stopping it hard, which is the reverse of the mechanical intuition. The established fix is minimum-B geometry, a field arranged to grow in every direction away from the plasma, which stabilizes these modes at the cost of giving up axial symmetry \cite{chen2016plasma}. Whether that cost is worth paying here waits on the same radiation-hydrodynamic calculation as everything else in this section.

Carrying both topologies does not mean carrying two magnets. The field has to be static during a pulse, because \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} keeps induced current out of the superconductor on purpose and a coil that deliberately swings its own field would defeat that. Static during a pulse is not static forever. The two burns of a cycle are separated by an interplanetary transfer, so recharging a persistent magnet over a day of cruise costs nothing measured against a pulse. Build one bore with independently fed coils, run the graded profile on the departure, and for the growth push put the current at the ship end and leave the rest near zero. That is a single-ended wall in the same hardware, with no moving parts.

Physically reshaping a magnet between legs is the option that sounds attractive and loses, and it loses on the structure rather than on the conductor. The virial floor of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} runs from \SIrange{0.4}{1.2}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and the upper end is not a material property. It is bought by winding the structure under pre-compression, so that the assembly starts out squeezed and has more room to stretch before the tape reaches its strain limit. Pre-stress is created at assembly and destroyed by taking the coil apart. A re-formable magnet therefore comes back at the plain-build \SI{0.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, tripling the dominant mass term. Even granting that both legs need equally heavy magnets, two fixed ones cost $2M$ against a reshapable one's $3M$, before any allowance for joints, mechanisms, and qualifying a magnet that comes apart. Superconducting tape is bendable while it is being wound and cannot stretch past about \SI{0.4}{\percent} afterwards, which is the same limit that sets the floor in the first place \cite{rebco_tapes}.

\subsubsection{Inner Planet Assist Alternatives}
PuffSats can employ gravity assists from the inner planets as alternatives to the Jupiter-only cycle. By using electric propulsion and deep-space maneuvers, these spacecraft can redirect their trajectories back to Earth at high velocities. Compared to Jupiter, inner-planet encounters occur more frequently and require less $\Delta V$ to reach from Earth.   

Flybys of the inner planets yield much lower Earth-relative velocities, typically between \SI{10}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and \SI{20}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.  This is less than the \SIrange{56.5}{65.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} return velocity from Jupiter. These lower velocities limit the payload multiplication achievable per cycle. However, the faster turnaround time of inner planet maneuvers can offset this limitation. Total payload growth rates end up comparable to those of Jovian reverse Hohmann transfers. 

Inner solar system trajectories can also use solar electric propulsion, which allows gradual
$\Delta V$ accumulation over long arcs in strong sunlight. These low-thrust arcs can be paired with high-thrust burns near perihelion or during flybys, which increase orbital eccentricity and aphelion, enhancing Earth-relative velocities for PuffSat pulsed propulsion.
 
Combining inner-planet and Jupiter assists raises throughput by giving more favorable launch windows.

\subsubsection{Electric and Solar Sails}\label{sec:esails}
An electric sail deflects solar wind protons with long, positively charged tethers, producing propellantless thrust \cite{janhunen2004esail}. Its thrust falls off with solar distance as $r^{-7/6}$, more slowly than the $r^{-2}$ of photon pressure, and its thrust vector can tilt roughly \SI{30}{\degree} from the anti-Sun line \cite{janhunen_sandroos2007_esail}. Reference designs target a characteristic acceleration, the thrust per unit spacecraft mass at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, near \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} \cite{mengali2008esail}.

We simulated a dive-and-boost profile under this thrust model \cite{katz_esail_sim_2026}. The sail departs Earth's orbit at zero hyperbolic excess, sheds angular momentum until its perihelion drops to \SI{0.5}{\astronomicalunit}, coasts down, and then thrusts along its velocity vector back out to \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. At \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} it returns after 300 days and crosses Earth's orbit at \SI{19}{\kilo\meter\per\second} relative to Earth. Diving to \SI{0.35}{\astronomicalunit} yields \SI{23}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in 1.4 years. Reaching \SI{49}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in 8 months requires \SI{5}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}, far beyond current designs. Low thrust cannot harvest the Oberth effect. An impulsive burn at perihelion converts the well depth into orbital energy, while a gentle push adds only what it can deposit during the brief perihelion pass, so the dive helps mainly by moving the sail to where its thrust is stronger.

The simulation surfaced two constraints on flying such dives. At \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} the thrust is a sixth of solar gravity at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, so thrusting continuously against the direction of motion spirals the orbit down while keeping it nearly circular, and the sail comes home with almost no Earth-relative speed. The sail must instead brake only along an arc near aphelion, which pins the aphelion at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} while the perihelion drops. A planar flip to a retrograde orbit also fails, since the path passes through zero angular momentum, a solar impact trajectory. Retrograde injection therefore stays with the Jupiter assist of \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}.

These speeds sit in the same \SIrange{10}{20}{\kilo\meter\per\second} class as the inner-planet flybys above, with no launch windows and no propellant, and the sail can dive again on a roughly yearly cadence. Two limits temper the appeal. The thrust inherits the solar wind's variability \cite{janhunen_sandroos2007_esail}, so sails deliver coarse trajectories, and terminal precision stays with ballistic coasts plus the fine-control thrusters of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}. The quoted acceleration also assumes a bare sail. A sail hauling several times its own mass in PuffSats slows to \SIrange{0.2}{0.3}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} and round trips of 2 to 3 years, so electric sails fit best as swarms of small, self-delivering momentum carriers rather than freighters.

Solar sails could fly the same profile on photon pressure \cite{mcinnes1999solar_sailing}, but their mass budget is harsher. Sunlight at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} exerts at most \SI{9.1}{\micro\newton\per\square\meter} on a perfect reflector, closer to \SI{8}{\micro\newton\per\square\meter} for a real aluminized film, so a sail system must stay under about \SI{8}{\gram\per\square\meter} of areal density to reach \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} with nothing attached. Pushing a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat at that acceleration takes \SIrange{5000}{8400}{\square\meter} of sail, a square 70 to 90 meters on a side massing \SIrange{15}{42}{\kilo\gram} \cite{katz_esail_sim_2026}, and that assumes \SIrange{3}{5}{\gram\per\square\meter}. LightSail 2 flew \SI{32}{\square\meter} at \SI{5}{\kilo\gram} total, about \SI{160}{\gram\per\square\meter} \cite{spencer2021lightsail2}, and IKAROS flew \SI{196}{\square\meter} at \SI{315}{\kilo\gram} \cite{tsuda2011ikaros}. The flight record is also thin and mixed. Cosmos 1 was lost to a launch failure \cite{cosmos1_2005_loss}, and NEA Scout deployed from Artemis I but was never contacted \cite{neascout2022lost}. The two electric sail tether experiments to reach orbit, ESTCube-1 and Aalto-1, both failed to deploy their tethers \cite{slavinskis2015estcube1, praks2021aalto1}, so no electric sail has yet produced measured thrust in space. Even so, a single sail returning a kilogram-scale impactor at \SI{19}{\kilo\meter\per\second} would rehearse PuffSat momentum exchange end to end at small cost, which earns the concept a place in early flight tests.

\subsubsection{Apoapsis-Raise Return With Onboard Burns}\label{sec:apoapsis_raise_loop}
The gentlest member of this family needs no third body at all. Instead of borrowing speed from a planet or the solar wind, the craft raises its aphelion with onboard burns and lets the fall back to Earth supply the closing speed. A craft leaving Earth at escape speed spends \SI{1.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of methalox at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} to lift its aphelion to \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit}, where an argon solar-electric burn sheds \SI{4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of tangential speed. Sunlight at \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit} is about a fifth of its \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} strength, so lightweight mirrors concentrating roughly five suns onto the cells, the same optics proposed for the Ceres power depots (\autoref{sec:ceresly_good}), restore roughly \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} intensity at the cell. The power plant is the least mature part of this loop. Shedding \SI{4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} over 90 days takes \SI{0.51}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}. At an argon exhaust speed of \SI{21}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, the \SI{2200}{\second} specific impulse implied by the mass budget below, that needs about \SI{9}{\watt} of electrical power for every kilogram of departing craft, or \SI{14}{\watt\per\kilo\gram} if the burn is squeezed into 60 days. NASA's Psyche carries \SI{21}{\kilo\watt} of array at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} on \SI{2747}{\kilo\gram}, about \SI{7.6}{\watt\per\kilo\gram}, and fires one xenon thruster at a time for \SI{240}{\milli\newton}, an acceleration of \SI{0.09}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} \cite{psyche_press_kit}. This loop asks for about a fifth more power per kilogram than Psyche carries, delivered where sunlight is a fifth as strong, with the concentrator counted in the mass. Perovskites suit such an array, a thin-film photovoltaic reaching \SI{44}{\watt\per\gram} at \SI{20.1}{\percent} efficiency in flexible form \cite{hailegnaw2024flexible}, though that is a cell-level figure and a deployed array stays limited by its structure rather than its cells. Concentration does cost one advantage of the distance. Five suns at \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit} put roughly \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} intensity back on the cell, so it runs warmer than an unconcentrated array, and perovskite ageing is thermally activated \cite{mavlonov2024thermal}. Trading the mirrors for five times the area would buy a colder, slower-ageing cell at the cost of the structure to hold it. Either design requires qualification under vacuum and AM0, the unfiltered solar spectrum above the atmosphere, conditions that degrade tested perovskite architectures \cite{costa2025perovskite}. A finite-thrust check confirms that a 60--90 day burn centered on apoapsis reproduces the ideal impulse to under 1\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The craft then falls back and meets Earth \SI{1.69}{\year} after departure, closing at about \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, just over twice the local escape speed at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude. The aphelion is the free knob that closes the phasing, the same trick the resonant solar dive of \autoref{sec:earth_reintercept} uses.

Unlike the flybys and sails above, these burns are carried propellant. They total \SI{5.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and cost 41\% of the departing mass. The collision then pushes 2.6 times the arriving mass to escape speed (\autoref{tab:mass_scenarios}), so the fleet multiplies by 1.55 each cycle and reaches a millionfold in about 54 years, three times the 17 of the phased solar-dive loop (\autoref{sec:earth_reintercept}). What the slow loop buys is nearness. It needs no solar dive, no gravity assist, no boost node off Earth, and no engine beyond flown types, so it is a candidate first flight of the re-intercept cycle while the network is young. The engines are flown hardware; the lightweight concentrated array that drives them is not. It also leans hardest of any row in \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios} on the fudge factor. Multiplying the 0.59 dry fraction into \autoref{eq:PuffSat_ratio} gives growth of $1.93f$ per cycle, so the cascade stops growing below $f \approx 0.52$. The trajectory numbers stand on their own; the doubling economics inherit the uncertainty in $f$ at a collision energy roughly $1/40$ of the regime the fudge factor was framed around (\autoref{sec:PuffSat_ratio_approximation}).

\subsubsection{Inner Planets Combined With Lunar ISRU}
An object launched from the Moon towards Earth can reach Venus or Mars with modest additional maneuvers.  If its rockets fire at a \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} perigee, only \SIrange{300}{600}{\meter\per\second} $\Delta V$ beyond lunar escape is needed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Gravity assists, coupled with small burns or solar-electric propulsion, could then return these payloads to the Moon on high-speed flybys.

If these payloads are PuffSats filled with lunar volatiles, they could launch several times their own mass from the Moon. This option requires mastering lunar ISRU and will likely need some initial fuel supplies from Earth. However, it offers faster launch capacity growth than PuffSat cycles that use suborbital rockets launched from Earth. This approach also avoids the need to master the prograde/retrograde chamber collisions discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}.

Near-Earth asteroids and comets are other possible sources of volatiles for PuffSats, but they have serious disadvantages. Mining in low gravity with limited available energy is difficult.  The logistics of resupply and navigating materials into Earth flyby trajectories are also challenging. Their recoverable volatile inventories depend on size, composition, accessibility, and mining method. The Moon's proximity and available oxygen make it the baseline initial target for PuffSat ISRU.

\subsection{The Minimum Nozzle Sets The Minimum Rocket}\label{sec:minimum_nozzle}
Everything above assumes the collision plasma can be turned into forward thrust, and near the Sun that means a nozzle. This is the main limitation of the direct-launch architecture. A nozzle sized to redirect a given per-pulse energy does not get lighter just because the rocket carrying it is small, so its mass sets a floor on how large the smallest worthwhile rocket has to be. Three ways lead past that floor. The rocket can be large enough that the nozzle is a small fraction of its mass. The impactors can be light enough that the nozzle needed to catch them is small to begin with. Or the collision can be slow enough that there is little energy to contain.

That opening assumption has a size, and \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} gives it a name. The jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ is the fraction of the ideal one-directional exhaust momentum that a real nozzle delivers. What the nozzle receives is not a uniform stream. Once the two masses merge, a quarter of the pulse energy remains as bulk motion of the fireball and three quarters becomes internal energy, random in the fireball's own rest frame. The bulk quarter already travels retrograde and needs no field, but it carries exactly the momentum the incoming projectile brought in, so by itself it cancels to zero thrust.
