The nozzle produces every newton-second by turning the thermal three quarters into one-sided flux. The head-on geometry helps here, for the reason given below. The expansion is a pancake with a preferred axis rather than a sphere, so the field trims a divergence instead of reversing one. What the geometry cannot avoid is that roughly half of that thermal energy starts out moving toward the ship. The field has to mirror that half, which requires the magnetic pressure at the throat to stand off the fireball's own pressure. Plasma that leaks forward is charged twice, once as exhaust that never left and once as heat on the structure. We have not computed the field strength this needs, and it belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}.

The system has a pass-through floor. Forward thrust reaches zero at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$ and reverses below it. The floor is $0.5$ at the three-to-one mix of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. A pusher plate has no such cliff, since a plate at any restitution still passes some momentum forward. That asymmetry is the strongest argument for a heavily diluted mix wherever the added propellant is cheap. The twenty-four-to-one dilution of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} puts the floor at $0.2$ and takes most of its margin there.

Ahedo and Merino's two-dimensional nozzle model supplies one published anchor for $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$. It reports a plume efficiency between $0.63$ and $0.83$ across a sweep of divergence rate and ion magnetization \cite{ahedo2010magnetic}. Their plume efficiency is the axial share of the downstream jet's energy. Taking its square root gives a divergence-only ceiling of $0.79$ to $0.91$ on $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$. Axial velocity spread and other losses can only lower the full parameter. The pessimistic anchor of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} needs about $0.89$, near the top of this model's range. The model assumes a collisionless, electron-magnetized, current-free plasma at low $\beta$, the ratio of plasma pressure to magnetic pressure. A dense fireball whose pressure exceeds the field lies in a different regime. These figures therefore anchor the question rather than settle it. They also identify $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ as the first nozzle parameter to compute.

At the highest near-Sun collision energies, a magnetic nozzle, a shaped magnetic field that redirects the ionized plasma with no wall in contact, may beat a solid absorber that has to survive each impact \cite{ahedo2010magnetic}. The Mini-Mag Orion study sized a nozzle of this kind for pulsed nuclear propulsion, an array of superconducting coils massing about \SI{200}{\tonne} that handles \SI{340}{\giga\joule} per pulse at an effective exhaust velocity near \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{ewig2003minimag, lenard2007minimag}. That per-pulse energy is close to what a \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} retrograde impactor delivers on a 4 solar radii dive (see \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), and the coil mass scales roughly with the energy the field must contain. Two features of our architecture cut that contained energy below the Mini-Mag baseline. Our plasma comes from an aimed head-on collision rather than a roughly isotropic fission burst, so it leaves along a preferred axis and the field only trims its divergence instead of reversing an expanding sphere. A hybrid nozzle can go further and let a sacrificial low-Z absorber take the bulk axial momentum, with the field standing only the hottest core off that surface. Even trimmed, a nozzle at this scale earns its mass only on a large vehicle. The prograde rocket it pushes must carry hundreds of tonnes of payload, better still more than a thousand, so the nozzle amortizes against the delivered mass rather than dominating it. Magnetic redirection at these energies is therefore large-scale infrastructure with matching payoff, not the engine of a small craft.

A second route to a lighter nozzle shrinks the impactor rather than the coils. Periapsis speed is fixed by the orbit and not by the projectile, so the per-pulse energy $\tfrac{1}{2}mv^2$ falls in direct proportion to impactor mass, and the coil mass falls with it. Trimming a \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} projectile to \SI{100}{\gram} is a factor of 25 in energy. That would carry a \SI{200}{\tonne} nozzle into the single-digit tonnes, above the cooling-system and minimum-coil-size floors that eventually halt the scaling. Gram-scale spacecraft are already flight-proven \cite{tavares2019kicksat2}. Cornell's Sprite flew a radio, processor, solar cells, and sensors on a \SI{3.5}{\centi\meter} board massing about \SI{4}{\gram} \cite{manchester2013kicksat}, and gram-scale wafer craft carrying cameras and two-way optical links are a studied design target \cite{lubin2016roadmap}. A \SI{100}{\gram} sunward projectile is then dominated by its heat shield, not its electronics, which is why the 4 solar radii thermal design of \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal} already tucks gram-scale guidance hardware into the shadow of its cone. These masses are optimistic and assume roughly another decade of miniaturization, but they extrapolate flown hardware rather than inventing it. If they hold, the nozzle no longer needs a thousand-tonne vehicle to amortize against. That is the larger of the two feasibility gains, and the one that opens the architecture to far smaller ships.

The second route carries a physics caveat, not just the hardware floors above. A lighter pulse is not simply a scaled-down heavy one. The plasma from a \SI{100}{\gram} hit is smaller and cools faster. More radiation can escape before the nozzle turns the remaining energy into thrust, lowering the jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}. The opacity estimate behind that assumption was sized for the denser, slower low-orbit collision. Applying it to a gram-scale near-Sun pulse is an extrapolation for the independent impact-simulation cross-check among our next steps. Until that check is done, the practical impactor is better read as a band, plausibly \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram}, than as one mass. Radiative loss sets its lower edge.

A third route sidesteps both the coil mass and that caveat, because it is lighter by speed rather than by impactor mass. The Jupiter-only cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} closes at about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Only the reduced mass of a collision is dissipated, and for a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat merging with the \SI{213}{\kilo\gram} slug of \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} that is $\mu = (25)(213)/238 = \SI{22.4}{\kilo\gram}$. The pulse then carries $\tfrac{1}{2}\mu w^2 = \SI{62.9}{\giga\joule}$, against \SI{477}{\giga\joule} for the near-Sun case, a factor of \num{7.6}. Two rules then disagree about what magnet that needs, and the disagreement is instructive rather than merely inconvenient. Scaling the Mini-Mag coil mass linearly with pulse energy, the rule used above, gives \SI{37}{\tonne}. Sizing the magnet by what it actually has to hold gives less. The field only stands off the plume's pressure, and pressure comes from translation alone, so the energy locked in dissociation and ionization is contained by nothing. That is most of the pulse. The stored field energy is the $nR_gT$ of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down}, \SI{4.4}{\giga\joule} at the coldest pulse of the burn and \SI{12.2}{\giga\joule} at the hottest, and the virial floor of the paragraphs above turns that into \SIrange{3.7}{11}{\tonne} and \SIrange{10}{30}{\tonne} respectively.

We take the virial figures, because the linear rule charges the magnet for energy that never pushes on it, and we quote the whole band rather than an end of it, because the burn genuinely runs across it. Call it tens of tonnes. That is a tenth to a third of a \SI{100}{\tonne} craft, so the amortization floor falls from a thousand-tonne ship to one a single Starship can loft, but the nozzle is a serious fraction of that ship rather than a rounding error on it. Neither figure is a design. The two-term mass model of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}, virial structure plus conductor, is what has to settle it, and it has not been run at this pulse. The radiative caveat of the previous paragraph works in this case's favor instead of against it, since a slower collision makes a cooler fireball that radiates less. The plasma is still well ionized. Spread over carbon atoms, \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} delivers about \SI{350}{\electronvolt} per atom. That is some thirty times carbon's \SI{11.3}{\electronvolt} first ionization energy. The weakly ionized \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} gas of a low-orbit insertion delivers only about \SI{4}{\electronvolt} and falls short of it. A magnetic nozzle is therefore legitimate on the outbound leg, and cheap enough that the growth cycle can afford one early.

A different answer drops the nozzle entirely and reverts to Orion's flat plate. The ship would release a slab of cheap propellant, mostly polyethylene with a thin iron backing, into the projectile's path behind the craft. The incoming projectile overflies the ship, so this option is for uncrewed vehicles only, the same firing-line logic as the front-mounted sail of \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}. On impact the backing acts as a tamper, an inert layer whose inertia biases the fireball's expansion away from itself and toward the plate. That is the collimating work Orion's case and channel filler performed when converting bomb x-rays into directed propellant \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}; here the kinetic energy arrives already directed along the collision axis, so the backing only sharpens an asymmetry the head-on geometry provides for free. Momentum conservation says the bias grows with the backing's share of the slab's mass \cite{gurney1943fragments} and with a concave backing shape, and no hydrocode has yet quantified the split, so we flag a possibility rather than a design. The plasma reaches the plate in the same velocity class as Orion's own propellant hit its plate, so the ablation budget starts from Orion's, managed with the coatings already described. A plate redirects the fireball without containing it, escaping the coil-mass scaling above, and pays for that by catching only the forward wing of the expansion.

Whichever option is chosen, the near-Sun thermal problem belongs to the pulse and not to the Sun. Sunlight at 4 solar radii carries \SI{3.9}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter} (\autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), so a nozzle and structure a few tens of meters across present a few hundred square meters and take in on the order of a gigawatt, and that is the bound where every surface absorbs everything. The impacts run far above it. A \SI{1000}{\tonne} ship taking the \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost at \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} effective exhaust velocity spends about \SI{255}{\tonne} of collision mass by \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass}, a quarter of it arriving retrograde at \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. That is roughly \SI{1e16}{\joule} of collision energy. Delivered over a periapsis burn of order \SI{1000}{\second} it averages about \SI{10}{\tera\watt}, and stretching the burn across the whole deep passage still leaves terawatts. Sunlight is then three to four orders of magnitude down, and a nozzle that misdirects even a tenth of a percent of its pulse energy into hardware swamps it. The small projectile's thermal design is a solar problem, worked in \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}. The ship's is not.

The magnetic nozzle does not help with the part that matters here. A field steers charged particles but is transparent to photons. Radiation supplies no thrust and lands on whatever hardware is in view. Two fractions set the load. They are the radiated share of pulse energy and the share of the sky filled by coils and structure. Their product multiplies the \SI{10}{\tera\watt} above. A few thousand square meters of structure at \SI{1500}{\kelvin} sheds about a gigawatt while the burn runs. Any excess has to enter a heat sink. The product therefore has to sit near \num{e-4} for the nozzle to stay a passive structure. With coils filling of order a tenth of the sky, radiative escape must remain below about a tenth of a percent. The propulsive budget is looser, though not by as much as the square-root rule alone suggests. Gas speed $v_g$ goes as the square root of retained energy, so losing 5\% of the pulse to radiation costs about 2.5\% of $v_g$. Effective exhaust velocity is what remains after debiting the incoming retrograde momentum (\autoref{eq:ve_general}). The same loss costs about 5\% of $v_e$ at the three-to-one mix and more near the pass-through floor. That penalty is secondary to the thermal limit, which binds roughly two orders of magnitude earlier. The radiation-hydrodynamic calculation of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} has to meet that thermal limit.

Two knobs act on that product, and both point toward the large end of this architecture. Absorbed power falls in proportion to the window the boost is spread over. Stretching a \SI{1000}{\second} burn to \SI{10000}{\second} spreads it across roughly 4 to 6 solar radii, where collision speed has dropped about 18\%, so a tenfold thermal gain costs on the order of 10\% of averaged exhaust velocity. That is a good trade for a vehicle that is heat-limited rather than fuel-limited. Impactor mass is the second knob. A kilogram-class projectile makes a larger and denser fireball than the \SI{100}{\gram} case above, and holds its opacity longer while the momentum transfers. The heavy end of the \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram} band is therefore the thermally comfortable one, which suits the large ships a nozzle at this scale already needs.

That is a hard problem, and it is the easy version of it. Adding fusion to the pulse (\autoref{sec:epstein_drives}) changes the kind of load, not just the size. Deuterium--tritium puts 80\% of its \SI{17.6}{\mega\electronvolt} into a \SI{14.1}{\mega\electronvolt} neutron \cite{orth2003vista}. A magnetic field does not steer neutrons, and the low-Z opacity that stops our collision's soft radiation (\autoref{sec:radiative_differences}) does not stop them either. Their mean free path in lithium is \SI{0.15}{\meter}, so the VISTA fusion vehicle stacked about a meter of lithium behind a meter of lithium hydride to keep its superconducting coils cold \cite{orth2003vista}. That shield covers under 4\% of the solid angle at the target and still masses about \SI{505}{\tonne} against a \SI{216}{\tonne} coil, with about \SI{7}{\giga\watt} to reject through its own radiators \cite{orth2003vista}. Target x-rays add a second charge. They stop in the shield's inner face rather than heating its bulk, and Orth found the resulting ablation to be one of the strongest constraints on the magnet's size and so on the whole vehicle \cite{orth2003vista}. A kinetic pulse pays neither bill. Its waste heat lands on surfaces that can be low-Z and sacrificial, and none of it arrives as neutrons. Fusion multiplies pulse energy, but even a modest multiplier can cost more shield and radiator mass than the added thrust returns, unless the fuel is the aneutronic mixture of \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}.

\subsection{Moneyball Meets Methalox}\label{sec:methalox_rebuttal}\cite{moneyball_film}
This is the most speculative economics in the paper. The chain below multiplies uncertain projectile costs against a nozzle efficiency we have not confirmed. That efficiency is the jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}. It is not the pusher-plate restitution factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}. The independent impact simulation among our next steps has yet to check its near-Sun value. The chain is worked at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$ throughout, and the paragraph after it prices the shortfall. Read it as an order-of-magnitude rebuttal, not a cost model. So we anchor on the worst case rather than the best. Suppose a retrograde projectile costs \$3200 per kilogram once delivered onto its 4 solar radii trajectory. That price is dominated by the projectile itself, which must survive the dive and steer to millimeter accuracy. It also assumes an early generation before repeated cycles drive projectile costs down. If the architecture beats chemical launch even there, it beats it.

\autoref{sec:fare_comparison} left the near-term fare stuck near \$5000 and named the reason. The expense is lifting hundreds of tonnes of PuffSats from Earth, not the propulsion. A skeptic sharpens this into one question. How can a heat-tolerant, precisely-steered projectile ever undersell a tank of methalox? The answer is that the costly projectile is only a small, fast seed of the reaction mass, not the bulk of it. Each collision hands its momentum to far more cheap mass, so its price per delivered kilogram falls by the dilution ratio, step after step down the cascade.

Start with the \$3200 projectile. At periapsis it collides head-on with cheap prograde propellant carried three parts to its one (\autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}). The merged plasma leaves at an effective exhaust velocity near \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. This is the loss-free value $v_p/2$ at the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis speed of a 4 solar radii dive. Diluting one costly part into four drops that reaction mass to about \$800 per kilogram. A \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost then spends the reaction mass to throw cheap payload across Earth's orbit at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The retrograde quarter arrives externally, so \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass} gives a total reaction-mass ratio of about $0.255$. About a quarter kilogram of reaction mass therefore launches each kilogram delivered, putting the crossing mass near \$200 per kilogram.

At Earth the same dilution repeats. The fast mass merges its kinetic energy into about twenty-four times its own mass of cheap onboard water or plastic. Spreading the energy over twenty-five parts caps the gas speed at $v_g = \sqrt{150^2/25} = \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. That is not the useful figure. This collision is head-on like the one at periapsis. The arriving mass therefore debits \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} spread across twenty-five parts, or \SI{6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. By \autoref{eq:ve_general}, the effective exhaust velocity is $30\,\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} - 6$. Its loss-free ceiling is \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, not $v_g$. We assume the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} collision produces plasma sufficiently ionized for the magnetic nozzle of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}. Unlike the weakly ionized \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} gas of a low-orbit insertion, this collision uses a magnetic nozzle rather than a material plate. Diluting the \$200 crossing mass across those twenty-five parts leaves reaction mass at about \$8 per kilogram. At \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exhaust against an \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb, \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass} gives a total reaction-mass ratio near $0.393$. Each kilogram of reaction mass therefore delivers about \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} to orbit. Spreading its \$8 cost over the mass it lifts leaves about \$3.20 per kilogram in low Earth orbit.

Even at the pessimistic anchor, that is roughly \$3.20 per kilogram to orbit. The methalox for a Starship ascent runs about \$10 per kilogram. Its roughly \$900{,}000 propellant load \cite{spacex_starship_cost} over the \SI{100}{\tonne} it lifts to low Earth orbit \cite{gunter_starship} comes to \$9 per kilogram, which we round up to \$10. We use the conservative \SI{100}{\tonne} payload carried through the rest of the paper, rather than the \SI{150}{\tonne} that \cite{gunter_starship} also lists. At the PuffSat rate a \SI{300}{\kilo\gram} passenger allocation is near \$1000 of fuel one way, against roughly \$3000 of methalox. That allowance triples the \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} of \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}, because fuel at \$3.20 per kilogram no longer rations cabin mass. Two honest corrections leave the conclusion standing. Launching the return-trajectory vehicles as well roughly doubles the bill to about \$6.40 per kilogram, still under methalox. We assume the larger prograde ship's magnetic nozzle, heat shield, and basic structure survive the pass and that PuffSats in Earth orbit decelerate and recover it, allowing its build cost to spread over many trips.

A third correction does not leave it standing unconditionally. Both collisions above run at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$. Their effective exhaust velocities fall steeply with this parameter because each carries a fixed incoming-momentum debit. At $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.9$, the periapsis step drops from \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{124}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The Earth step drops from \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{21}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The doubled bill then reaches about \$9.60 per kilogram, close to methalox. Break-even occurs near $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.89$. At $0.8$ the bill is near \$16 per kilogram, and this version of the rebuttal fails. The \$3200 anchor therefore carries a requirement rather than an estimate. No calculation in this paper supplies the required jet efficiency. The value also sits near the top of the $0.79$ to $0.91$ divergence-only ceiling from the cited nozzle model (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}). The scale case of the next paragraph is far less exposed because its fleet cost is roughly 290 times lower. It holds down to about $0.7$. At the three-to-one periapsis mix, forward thrust reaches zero at $0.5$ and reverses below it (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}).

The cost of the guided units on the 4 solar radii retrograde trajectory drops sharply as manufacturing scale and projectile mass grow. At aviation scale, a projectile is mostly carbon foam and a thin ceramic skin costing a few tens of dollars per kilogram. A small high-Z cap provides opacity, and gram-scale electronics provide guidance. Together these put unit cost near \$80 per kilogram. Unlike the Parker heat shield, the skin needs to provide only minimal structural support to a much smaller craft, so it can be made thinner and cheaper. At the scales used for global energy production (\autoref{sec:world_set_free}), larger retrograde projectiles are packed with cheap bulk material behind the heat shield (\autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}). A large power-generation ship blends the two costs. Its magnetic nozzle earns its mass only on a vehicle carrying hundreds of tonnes or more (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}), so most of its retrograde mass can be cheap bulk. Packing about ninety percent bulk with ten percent guided units puts the guided units at \$8 per kilogram of fleet mass. The bulk is not free. At the few dollars per kilogram that \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} projects for graphite and ceramic at scale, it adds about \$3, for a fleet cost near \$11 per kilogram. That \$11 per kilogram of retrograde mass then dilutes about sixteenfold on the way to Earth. Mixing three-to-one with cheap prograde propellant drops it fourfold. The reaction-mass ratio of $0.255$ spreads that mixture over nearly four times its mass of payload, dropping the cost again to about \$0.70 per kilogram. At \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} each kilogram carries $\tfrac{1}{2}v^2 \approx \SI{3.1}{\mega\watt\hour}$, so that \$0.70 per kilogram delivers raw energy at about \$0.22 per megawatt-hour. This stays below one dollar per megawatt-hour even after an inefficient conversion to electricity (\autoref{sec:strawway_economics}).

Even priced at the worst-case projectile, then, fuel to orbit competes with chemical launch. At scale the generation cost sits well under a dollar per megawatt-hour, below any current wholesale electricity source, once the fixed costs of the Straw Way, ocean heat sink, and power-generation equipment are set aside.

\section{One Hiroshima Per Second. The World Set Free}\label{sec:world_set_free}
In \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}, we propelled PuffSats to \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} using solar gravity and pulsed propulsion. A \SI{60}{\tera\watt} pulsed heat source, which is equivalent to one Hiroshima bomb per second \cite{hiroshima}, requires harnessing the kinetic energy of just \SI{5.3}{\tonne\per\second} of propulsion mass at these speeds. For context, humanity's total primary-energy consumption is approximately \SI{20}{\tera\watt} \cite{owid-energy-production-consumption}. A single one of these pulsed heat sources would produce roughly three times that, enough thermal power to cover the energy needs of all human civilization with room to spare.  This concept echoes H.G. Wells's 1913 novel \textit{The World Set Free}, where he imagined atomic bombs as a ``blazing continual explosion'' \cite{wells1914world}.  

Using kinetic projectiles to generate terrestrial power only makes sense if it achieves massive economies of scale that outperform the linear costs and land use efficiency of conventional renewables.  Let's explore how to build such an ambitious megastructure, and then explain why it's economically attractive in \autoref{sec:strawway_economics}.     

\subsection{Straw Ways To Heaven}\label{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}
The most common proposal for utilizing space-based power on Earth involves wireless power transfer from orbiting power plants to terrestrial rectenna arrays \cite{malaviya2022comprehensive}.  Unfortunately, commercial-scale power beaming remains orders of magnitude beyond today's demonstrations and would need ground rectennas several kilometers across \cite{space_beaming_power}. The required transmission infrastructure, both in space and on Earth, would be colossal.  Given these challenges, let's investigate terrestrial solutions that directly convert kinetic impact energy to electricity on Earth.

To deliver kinetic energy projectiles to Earth, the projectiles must be able to withstand atmospheric entry.  One solution is to build a vacuum tube, extending from Earth's surface to space, precisely aligned to allow incoming projectiles to pass through unimpeded.  The tube is suspended from an anchor point as depicted in \autoref{fig:straw_way_to_heaven}.   Enthusiasts have proposed numerous structures that could in principle support an anchor hoisted above Earth's atmosphere. These include Lofstrom Launch Loops \cite{lofstrom_loop}, tethered rings \cite{swan2023tethered_ring}, global orbital rings \cite{birch1982orbital_ring}, space elevators \cite{pearson1975orbital_tower}, and space fountains \cite{hyde1985space_fountain}.  For an accessible overview see also \cite{isaac_arthur_megastructures_playlist}.  However, none has advanced beyond early conceptual or paper-study stages, so all remain far from practical implementation.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Straw Way To Heaven.png}
    \caption{Vacuum tube suspended from cables allows projectiles through the atmosphere}
    \label{fig:straw_way_to_heaven}
\end{figure}


Fortunately, PuffSat pulsed propulsion offers a promising path to position an anchor point above Earth. While this technology is at a low TRL, we would have already developed it to high reliability if we were using it to direct projectiles at Earth using the techniques from \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}.  

A heavy-lift launch vehicle such as SpaceX's Super Heavy \cite{starship} delivers the anchor assembly and its attached vacuum tube to the target altitude.   We then indefinitely hover the anchor point using equal mass PuffSats that collide their gas output head-on in a pulsed propulsion chamber (described in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}), redirecting thrust downward. The propulsion chamber exhaust can be angled slightly to provide thrust vectoring to counter atmospheric winds on the vacuum tube and support cables. From this elevated anchor point, we sequentially deploy tube segments and support cables downward until reaching the ground. A reaction control system on the anchor point adjusts the pulsed propulsion chamber so it stays precisely positioned for the next PuffSat collision.  If these PuffSats meet at $v_p=\SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ at an average mass flow rate of \SI{1}{\tonne\per\second}, they can theoretically suspend more than \SI{1100}{\tonne}.   

In reality, energy losses would reduce thrust.   For reliable operations, we would need to lift redundant anchor points and vacuum tubes.  Together, redundancy and energy losses imply we will need additional PuffSat propulsion collision mass per second.  To keep the vacuum tube aloft, PuffSat propulsion must remain continuously active even when incoming projectiles cannot enter the tube to generate energy. Alternatively, for maintenance, the tube could be lowered at night and then re-hoisted by a heavy-lift rocket the following day.  Compared to magnetic systems like Lofstrom Launch Loops, hoisting an anchor with rockets or PuffSat pulsed propulsion is energy inefficient. That inefficiency is acceptable here. Lifting a few tons per second of propulsion PuffSat mass from Earth (as in \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}) or the moon (as in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}) seems economically viable if we use our vacuum tube to cleanly produce all the energy required by humanity.

\subsection{Vacuum Tube Details}\label{sec:vacuum_tube_details}
To mitigate micrometeorite risk in the upper atmosphere, the vacuum tube incorporates sacrificial shielding or modular, swappable segments. Self-healing materials \cite{self_healing_material_survey} may repair small tube ruptures.  For example, a thin wire mesh embedded in the tube wall could detect conductivity changes caused by tears or punctures. Briefly increasing the current through these wires would cause localized heating, melting the surrounding material and heat-sealing small breaches. Redundant parallel tubes may also be deployed to ensure continuous operation despite localized damage.  

The tube's walls get thinner at higher altitudes, where less structural support is needed to maintain a vacuum.  At lower heights, ribbed or corrugated designs  keep the tube lightweight and flexible. Thin, high-strength tensile cables attached every few meters along the tube connect the tube and the anchor point.  Each attachment point includes several parallel tensile cables, ensuring structural redundancy in the event that one or more are severed by micrometeorite impacts.   Equipped with winching micro-actuators, these cables dynamically control their length to precisely tune the tube's curvature. The actuators shape the tube to counteract Coriolis, tidal, drag, and gravity-variation forces affecting the projectile's transit. The anchor point itself will rotate throughout the day to track the Sun.  Sensors along the vacuum tube measure and broadcast the tube's position and shape, allowing incoming projectiles to navigate through it accurately.

Additional sensors are placed along the cables and at the top of the tube to accurately measure any perturbations. These measurements help to precisely model the projectile's trajectory as it enters the tube. Once a projectile is inside, making adjustments with microthrusters would contaminate the vacuum and damage the walls. Instead, if the tube is metalized or partially conductive, we can steer projectiles using small magnets. These magnets generate Lorentz forces from induced currents in the walls, allowing for precise control. In its upper, near-orbital reaches the wall also meets atomic oxygen, which erodes bare polymer, and unfiltered ultraviolet. A continuous metallized or silica coating holds off both, the same technique that protects polyimide solar-array blankets in orbit \cite{ao_protection_banks}, and the metallized version doubles as the conductive layer the magnetic steering relies on.

Creating and maintaining a high vacuum in long tunnels is no simple task, but facilities like the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) prove it's feasible. Our tube, like the LHC's, uses non-evaporable getter (NEG) \cite{neg_coatings} thin-film coatings placed at intervals to chemically scavenge most atmospheric gases. These coatings, however, are ineffective against noble gases like argon. While we could embed cryopumps throughout the tube, that would add complexity and mass. Fortunately, our projectiles bury the argon, either in the tube wall or in their own skins.  A struck atom either rebounds off the projectile and lodges in the wall, or is driven into the projectile and leaves the tube with it.  Both happen at any speed.  Closing velocity only shifts the odds between them, and it shifts them toward the projectile as it rises.  At \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} most of the argon rides out on the projectiles.  Near Earth escape speed most of it ends up in the wall (\autoref{sec:death_star}).  This is the same mechanism a sputter-ion pump, a standard accelerator vacuum pump, uses to remove the noble gases that getter films cannot.  It buries them in a surface instead of binding them chemically \cite{schulz1999sputterion}.  These impacts also sputter atoms from both the walls and the projectiles. The NEG coatings scavenge sputtered volatile atoms, while non-volatile elements recondense onto the tube interior.

Burial is not a permanent trap.  In a sputter-ion pump, later impacts re-sputter buried argon and release it in cyclic pressure bursts, an effect called argon instability that sets in once the argon partial pressure passes roughly \SI{1e-6}{\milli\bar} \cite{schulz1999sputterion}.  Several features keep it in check here.  The projectiles bury argon across hundreds of thousands of square meters of wall rather than the few square centimeters of a compact pump cathode, so a given buried atom is unlikely to be struck again.  Argon is under one percent of the atmosphere, and the tube walls are built to be nearly impermeable, so its partial pressure stays well below that threshold.  In a pump the severity of these bursts is set by the ratio of cathode-atom mass to gas-ion mass \cite{vaumoron1970argon}, and a light aluminum wall sits on the unfavorable side of that ratio.  Two things offset it.  Slow projectiles leave the rebounding atoms far below the energy a pump cathode delivers, so they barely sputter the wall and disturb what is already buried even less.  Fast projectiles bury most of their argon in their own skins and carry it out, so little of it reaches the wall to be re-released.  A pressure excursion in a kilometers-long straw is far less damaging than in a small chamber, and redundant parallel tubes absorb any transient. To ensure an even better vacuum inside the tube, thin films of oxygen-specific chemical adsorbents  are applied to the top few kilometers. By limiting exospheric oxygen from building up in the tube, we make our vacuum comparable to low orbit environments.  Scheduled tube refurbishment or replacement will be necessary due to wear from micrometeorites, argon sputtering, and NEG chemical consumption. Even if we pessimistically need a replacement once per month, the costs are minor compared to the revenue from global power generation (see \autoref{sec:strawway_economics}).

Projectiles that catastrophically miss the vacuum tube would break up high in the atmosphere at their extreme velocity, rather than striking the surface intact. Even so, breakup is not automatically harmless: the much slower Chelyabinsk airburst still injured roughly 1,500 people \cite{popova2021chelyabinsk}. Siting the tubes in remote areas limits ground-level effects on populated regions, but the fragmentation altitude and blast footprint of a missed projectile would need to be modeled.   The vacuum tube itself is likely destroyed, but redundant vacuum tubes would then be moved into place.

\subsection{Tether Tapering And Countering Extreme Drag Forces}\label{sec:tether_tapering}

Incoming projectiles experience dynamic pressure approximately  $\frac{v_{\text{projectile}}^2}{v_{\text{PuffSat}}^2} = \frac{(150\,\text{km/s})^2}{(11\,\text{km/s})^2} \approx 185$ times greater than that faced by trans-lunar PuffSats, due to their much higher velocity. This amplifies drag forces far beyond those analyzed in \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}.

Because these projectiles spend only \SIrange{1}{2}{\second} at lower altitudes when arriving at such extreme velocities, we may be able to counteract the resulting terminal forces by briefly firing small chemical thrusters and vectoring their thrust. Despite the rarefied air at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} altitude, projectiles still face intense ram heating. Thin, thermally conductive skins can be cooled via convective heat transfer and latent heat absorption from phase-changing PuffSat volatiles (e.g., water vaporization).

Projectile guidance and thermal management are easier if projectiles enter the vacuum tube at \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} altitude, rather than the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} baseline used in \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}. That altitude exceeds Kevlar's self-support breaking length. SK99 Dyneema's instantaneous breaking length, near \SI{430}{\kilo\meter} \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}, is on paper the longest of the four, but polyethylene creeps under sustained load, which rules it out for a permanent column (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). The column therefore wants PBO, whose \SI{380}{\kilo\meter} self-support length \cite{zylon_technical_info} comes with low creep. The taper of the following paragraphs then supplies the safety margin (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). A continuous silica or metallized barrier holds off the atomic oxygen and ultraviolet the exposed column meets, and the dry vacuum largely suppresses PBO's hydrolysis pathway, so PBO earns its place in the coated column rather than the humid air near the surface.

Unlike the projectiles, PuffSats that provide pulsed propulsion to anchor the tube travel at only \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Their lower velocity enables more precise navigation and impact targeting at reduced altitudes. Therefore, we can place one anchor at the lower \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} altitude and connect the upper portion of the tube to a second anchor at higher altitude. Shorter cables anchored to surface vessels can serve as guy wires to counter winds on the lower \SI{20}{\kilo\meter} of the tube. These guy wires can be actively winched to adjust tension, minimizing peak wind forces the longer anchor cables must withstand.

Despite these optimizations, our current cable designs still lack sufficient engineering safety margin. Tapered cables offer a promising solution. The mass-optimal cross-sectional area of a tapered cable at distance $x$ from the anchor scales as $A(x) = C e^{-\frac{x}{b}}$, where $b$ is the material's breaking length and $C$ is a constant (see \autoref{sec:breaking_length_derived}). 

To maintain an engineering safety factor of 3 across a cable stretched to its full breaking length, the cross-sectional area at the anchor must be roughly 20 times greater than at the point where the cable connects to the lowest portions of the tube. Fortunately, this tapering requirement aligns with our existing structural strategy. We already deploy thin control wires every few meters along the tube. By progressively merging and intertwining these wires near anchor altitudes, we achieve the required cross-sectional increase without adding appreciable mass beyond our existing budget.



\subsection{Power Plant Chamber Details}
Beneath the vacuum tube, we propose excavating a steam chamber designed to vaporize incoming projectiles. These chambers are large construction projects, engineered to withstand sudden changes in pressure and temperature.  The chamber walls would be lined with steel backed by bunker-grade reinforced concrete, while internally mounted spring-loaded metal plates would absorb shock and mitigate structural stress.  

Suppose a \SI{0.1}{\cubic\kilo\meter} steam chamber containing \SI{50}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} steam receives a \SI{600}{\tera\joule} kinetic impact.   As a simplification, let's say the steam has a constant specific heat of \SI{3.5}{\kilo\joule\per(\kilo\gram\celsius)}.  In this case the temperature change is 
$\frac{\SI{600}{\tera\joule}}{\SI{5e9}{\kilo\gram}\times\SI{3500}{\joule\per(\kilo\gram\kelvin)}} = \SI{34.2}{\celsius}$.  An impact of this magnitude every 10 seconds produces the heat to continuously provide all humanity's energy.  This steam chamber would be connected to thousands of turbines and a massive cooling loop to harness and dissipate this energy.  

Releasing large amounts of heat into the atmosphere would lead to unacceptable local temperature increases. Using a coolant loop that exchanges heat with deep ocean water will also have environmental consequences. Spreading $\SI{40}{\tera\watt}$ of heat over $\SI{10}{\cubic\kilo\meter}$ of deep ocean water would increase its temperature by $\frac{\SI{40}{\tera\watt}\cdot\SI{3600}{\second\per\hour}}{\SI{1e13}{\kilo\gram}\cdot\SI{4186}{\joule\per\kilo\gram\per\celsius}} = \SI{3.44}{\celsius\per\hour}$.  This rapid temperature change could severely damage local marine habitats.

The mid-water column is not empty. Mesopelagic fish alone may total around 10 billion tonnes, likely the bulk of all fish biomass \cite{irigoien2014mesopelagic}. The case for tolerable harm rests instead on scale. The directly heated volume is a vanishingly small fraction of the ocean, so the disturbance stays local rather than basin-wide. When compared to the widespread impacts of global anthropogenic climate change or even the wildlife displacement caused by solar farms, the environmental harm from this specific heat rejection method appears relatively modest.

The projectiles would have explosive or water interiors that disintegrate quickly on hypervelocity shock contact with the chamber's high pressure steam. Made from carbon composites and polymers, their structural exteriors would vaporize into gases at impact temperatures. Inside the steam chamber, temperatures are so high that the carbon turns into a superheated plasma. Steam also decomposes in these conditions, releasing oxygen that combusts this carbon along with any air present. Although this process forms very little carbon soot before the carbon turns to carbon dioxide, any that does form can be filtered out with charcoal screens before reaching the turbines.   The remaining gases are released or sequestered as they travel along with the steam cycling through the system.   Solid residues that precipitate on chamber walls could be cleaned off either continuously or during maintenance windows.  Continuous processes might rely on trapping solids in cheap viscous liquids like silicone fluids, organic oils, or molten salts that slowly ooze down chamber walls and drain at the bottom.  

An alternative converter skips the steam. If the chamber floor is held at vacuum, each projectile arrives as fast plasma that a magnetic channel can decelerate and tap for current directly, the way a magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) generator draws power from moving conductive gas \cite{dominguez2024mhd}. This favors many rapid, small impulses over a few large ones, since a smaller pulse is easier to contain and clears before the next arrives.

\subsection{Vacuum Exchange And Airlock Details}\label{sec:vacuum_airlock}
An airlock separates the steam chamber from the rest of the tube as shown in \autoref{fig:power_plant_vacuum_exchange}.   A door with a closing speed of \SI{300}{\meter\per\second} could close a \SI{30}{\centi\meter} diameter aperture in \SI{1}{\milli\second}.  Even a \SI{5}{\meter} long airlock is long enough to prevent steam molecules from traversing the airlock in this short time.  

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Power Plant Cooling and Generators.png}
    \caption{Diagram of the power plant and vacuum airlock system.  New airlock segments are rotated in between impacts.}
    \label{fig:power_plant_vacuum_exchange}
\end{figure}

Designing an airtight door that can open and close at the necessary speeds is beyond current technology. However, a system of rapidly rotating disks with strategically placed apertures can create a quick-closing, leaky seal.

This system works like this:

\begin{itemize}
\item Two or more pressure-resistant disks with identical radii of perhaps \SI{10}{\meter} are closely mounted on an axial shaft.

\item These disks spin at slightly different speeds, and each has a \SI{30}{\centi\meter} aperture near their outer edge.

\item We add mass along the radial line of each disk's aperture to keep the rotating disks balanced.

\item When the apertures don't overlap, they block the vacuum tube and prevent steam from entering.

\item The apertures align every few seconds over the airlock opening.   This gives us time to close a second airtight door over the airlock opening.

\item The disk apertures may align more frequently than projectile arrivals.  Since projectiles might only arrive every \SI{10}{\second}, it's tricky to design fast spinning disks that only overlap at this low frequency.  As long as the secondary door can close between alignments, and the alignments are synchronized with projectile arrivals, the airlock system meets its operational requirements.
\end{itemize}

This rotating disk system offloads handling the pressure differential between steam and vacuum.  That simplifies the design for the separate, high-performance airtight door that seals the vacuum completely. The airtight door is still fast, closing on the order of a few hundred milliseconds.  Before this door closes, steam leaks through the clearance between the disks and the airlock.  This clearance is likely at least a millimeter since we must handle vibrations.  To further mitigate steam ingress, chemical desiccants are embedded in the disk housing and airlock walls. Thin films of desiccants are also used along the first kilometer or so of the vacuum tube.  The disk housing and airlock walls might also be cooled to freeze or at least slow steam molecules.

At the bottom of the airlock, the projectile releases enormous heat on contact with steam. Asteroid air-burst simulations suggest most of this energy is directed in the projectile's direction of travel, away from the airlock and the closest chamber walls \cite{tunguska_downwards}. If still needed to handle the high initial heat, sacrificial plastic segments can form the bottom part of the airlock pipe. These plastics should be strong at low temperatures but vaporize easily to avoid creating shrapnel. Their brief exposure to steam before projectile impact can be buffered by a coat of ice, which forestalls heating. To prevent the production of highly toxic gases when they decompose, the plastic should not be halogenated. Cheap polyethylene pipes may satisfy all these requirements.  The pipe can be ribbed for strength and corrugated to stop buckling under the steam's pressure.  These techniques reduce the pipe mass needed to withstand the steam's compressive load.  A thicker plastic plug at the bottom of the pipe could drastically quicken projectile disintegration, especially if the projectile primarily contains materials like water or explosives. The projectile's enormous kinetic energy would pulverize it on plug impact. The resulting intense heat and shock wave breaks any solid debris into smaller shards that rapidly vaporize in the steam far from chamber walls.  An added benefit of these sacrificial segments is that they lengthen the time the airlock door has to seal before steam can travel far enough to enter the vacuum tube.

A new airlock segment is swapped in between kinetic projectile impacts, providing time to restore the high vacuum offline before its next use. Sacrificial bottom sections are also replaced and reattached while the main airlock segment is out of rotation.

\subsection{With Economies Of Scale, Our Ambitions Can Scale Too}\label{sec:strawway_economics}

While a global power plant requires a massive initial capital investment, its costs scale favorably with output. The cost of suspending a large vacuum tube depends very little on the plant's generating capacity. The cost of deep-sea cooling loops would also be amortized across the entire planetary-scale facility.  A multi-terawatt installation could achieve per-watt cost reductions through bulk procurement of thousands of identical turbine and generator units. 

Mass production reduces the cost of projectile electronics and microthrusters. The cost of these components per projectile also remains nearly the same as individual projectile size increases.  Ten seconds of planetary-scale power corresponds to $\SI{20}{\tera\watt} \times \SI{10}{\second} \approx \SI{5.56e7}{\kilo\watt\hour} = \$555,556$ at 1 cent per kilowatt-hour.  This energy revenue would likely dwarf the material costs of the projectiles themselves, especially if their interiors are mostly water or explosives. Such cheap-bulk interiors push the production cost under \$1 per megawatt-hour (\autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal}), a tenth of the one-cent-per-kilowatt-hour selling price used above.      

Power from our plant could be globally distributed by supertanker-sized batteries that connect to urban power grids at coastal ports.  While the cost of utility-scale batteries is progressively declining, it remains a hurdle. Current battery designs prioritize efficient energy storage and release. However, if energy efficiency becomes less critical, we could design cheaper low-performance batteries. Given that our power source exhibits strong cost efficiencies, we could simply increase the power output to compensate for any energy losses incurred by using these more affordable batteries for distribution. 

Fossil fuels remain entrenched as the dominant energy source, even as cleaner alternatives like renewables gain traction. High-temperature heat from low-cost projectile impacts offers a novel route for synthesizing hydrocarbon fuels directly from water and carbon dioxide.

One promising pathway is the cerium oxide cycle \cite{CeriaThermoCycles}, where cerium oxide is reduced at high temperatures and low pressures. Operating at low pressures simplifies vacuum chamber and airlock design (see \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}). The reduced cerium oxide then reacts with carbon dioxide to produce carbon monoxide. The water-gas-shift reaction converts part of this carbon monoxide and steam into carbon dioxide and hydrogen, setting the synthesis-gas ratio. Fischer--Tropsch synthesis then converts the resulting synthesis gas into hydrocarbons \cite{water_gas_shift,fischer_tropsch}.

An alternative approach, adapted from chemical looping in blast furnaces \cite{iron_chemical_looping}, uses iron to reduce carbon dioxide to carbon monoxide. Part of the carbon monoxide stream can react with steam through the water-gas-shift process to produce hydrogen and adjust the gas ratio. Fischer--Tropsch synthesis then converts the resulting mixture into hydrocarbons \cite{water_gas_shift,fischer_tropsch}. The resulting iron oxide may be thermally decomposed to regenerate iron, forming a closed-loop cycle. Iron, being denser than iron oxide, naturally separates under gravity, much like the stratification of slag and molten iron during steel production. Incoming projectiles can be designed to gasify just prior to impact, allowing energy deposition without direct mechanical disruption. At velocities around \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, low-density gas atoms penetrate a few nanometers into the lava, transferring heat through shallow kinetic interactions. This reduces shock propagation and ejecta formation. To further enhance coupling, finely divided iron oxide powder can be injected into the vacuum chamber, presenting a much larger effective surface area to the incoming hot gas. The powder absorbs and redistributes the energy before it settles back into the molten pool. The chamber walls remain protected from peak loads by the intervening lava, which is both a volumetric heat sink and a radiative buffer, damping transient spikes.

Both cerium and iron oxides decompose only under very low pressures, requiring aggressive removal of oxygen from the reaction environment. An alternative approach is to use water-splitting cycles, such as the sulfur–iodine or copper–chlorine processes, to generate hydrogen \cite{sulfur_iodine_chlorine}. Hydrogen, together with carbon dioxide, forms an ideal feedstock for hydrocarbon synthesis. Although these water-splitting cycles run cooler than direct thermolysis, the sulfur--iodine acid-decomposition step still operates near \SI{850}{\celsius} and exposes reactor walls to hot sulfuric acid. SiC-based ceramics, including silicon carbide matrix composites, are an established candidate liner material for this stage, combining corrosion resistance with the thermal-shock and fracture tolerance needed under pulsed operation.  These liners could coat a shock-absorbing nickel superalloy chamber, which is externally heated by a molten bath of lava or liquid iron.  A common superalloy such as Inconel 718 retains useful strength only to about \SI{700}{\celsius} \cite{inconel_718_strong}, below this step's temperature, so a higher-temperature alloy or active wall cooling would be required, and the liner and chamber would need compatibility and fatigue testing under repeated acid decomposition and pulsed shock.  Projectile impact energy keeps the lava molten, indirectly providing the steady high temperatures that are optimal for the reaction chamber. This configuration also shields the ceramics from direct impact shocks.

By bypassing costly steam turbines and simplifying vacuum tube maintenance, this direct-heating approach could enable viable hydrocarbon synthesis before large-scale electricity generation becomes cost-effective.

Though the power plant and distribution infrastructure would cost billions to construct and operate, a planetary-scale plant would make trillions annually.  Turns out, if \textit{Superman} wants to be as rich as \textit{Lex Luthor}, he should get limitless energy not from the light of a yellow sun, but from its gravity \cite{superman2025}.

\subsection{Comparison To Project PACER}
Project PACER was a 1970s Los Alamos concept to generate power by extracting steam from nuclear bombs detonated in a salt cavern \cite{pacer_1975}.   Later improvements protected the cavern walls with an artificial waterfall of tritium breeding liquid salts \cite{pacer_revisited}.  

Unlike PACER, our proposal does not produce radioactive waste or encourage the mass production of nuclear weapons.   Should a liquid wall be needed to protect the chamber, it can be made from cheap materials like water.  Tritium breeding and extraction are not required.   Our design eliminates the need for a biological shield, which simplifies the direct coupling of steam to turbines. This direct steam cycle enables the rapid heat exchange with the environment necessary to build terawatt-scale power plants.

\subsection{To Solve Climate Change, Make The Earth A \textit{Death Star}}\label{sec:death_star}
A \textit{Death Star} \cite{death_star} is a giant sphere with a narrow 2-meter opening where you can shoot something down to create an explosion inside the main reactor.   Building a \textit{Death Star} from scratch  would be a huge undertaking.   Fortunately, if we build the Straw Way To Heaven described in \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}, we're living on one.  Although our Straw Way generator produces clean energy from incoming projectiles, its power production is restricted to daytime hours when the straw can face the sun.   Also, a limitless clean energy source would only slow climate change.   Industrial carbon emissions from steel, cement, agriculture, and other processes would still heat the planet \cite{steel_and_cement}.   

\subsubsection{That's No Moon .  Oh Wait, It's Literally Our Moon}\cite{kenobi1977moon}
Projectiles made from lunar materials could enter a terrestrial Straw Way from high angles of attack around the clock.  To generate \SI{60}{\tera\watt} of heat from lunar bombardment, we'd need an astonishing \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} of lunar mass to pass through the terrestrial vacuum tube, since lunar material arrives far slower than the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} solar projectiles and so carries far less kinetic energy per kilogram. Such a vast quantity is daunting, but the momentum for launching off the moon could come from kinetic projectiles inbound from the Sun (as described in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}). Beyond providing thrust, these projectiles aimed at the Moon could vaporize lunar regolith. The resulting thermal energy could be harnessed for electricity generation or to power chemical and industrial processes directly. This lunar power source could support large-scale mining and production of the oxygen gas used in PuffSat pulsed propulsion  (as discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}).

Slower arrivals do not cost the tube its vacuum. The argon pumping of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} works by burying stray argon atoms in a solid surface, and how fast the projectile is moving decides which surface is likelier to catch them. Argon is heavier than carbon or aluminum, so a light nose rarely sends it backward. A thin iron skin, being heavier than argon, does. A projectile arriving near Earth escape speed, about \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, meets residual argon gently. The argon tends to bounce off that skin rather than bury itself in it, then lodge about a nanometer into the tube wall. The \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} solar projectiles favor the other outcome, driving most of the argon they strike into their own noses to leave the tube with them. Neither outcome excludes the other, and both pull argon out of the vacuum, so the traffic that generates power also does the pumping. What the mechanism needs is a floor rather than a ceiling. Anything falling from more than a few thousand kilometers of altitude arrives fast enough. That covers lunar material, payloads returning from Ceres, and the secondary payloads the Jupiter-only cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} can boost into eccentric Earth orbits.

Helium is the marginal case. It is light, so a slow projectile leaves it with far less energy than argon, too little to knock an aluminum atom out of place. Helium is also small enough to slip between those atoms without moving any of them, which is how helium loads into tungsten walls in fusion devices at comparable energies, at impact energies too low to sputter the surface at all \cite{baldwin2008helium,kajita2009tungsten}. What fraction stays put is not something we can settle here. The gap costs little either way. Helium is a few parts per million of air against argon's nine thousand, and the metallized wall that holds off atomic oxygen also slows helium permeation. Whatever collects is cleared on the refurbishment cycle already planned in \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}.

Lunar regolith is primarily carbon-depleted rock \cite{mckay1991lunar}. After the regolith-derived impactors heat steam to drive a global-scale generator, the process yields a waste column of hot regolith and scalding water. If this waste is channeled through thermally insulated pipes into deep ocean water, the intense hydrostatic pressure compresses the regolith. Its large surface area and elevated temperature accelerate chemical reactions that convert silicates into carbonates, pulling dissolved carbon dioxide from seawater \cite{seifritz1990silicates}. Deep ocean pressure further shifts chemical equilibrium toward carbonate formation \cite{kelemen2008peridotite}. With an incoming regolith flow of $\SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} \approx \SI{31}{\giga\tonne\per\year}$, we could mineralize billions of tons of dissolved carbon dioxide annually. This would enhance the ocean's capacity to absorb atmospheric carbon dioxide, making lunar mineral sequestration a viable strategy to offset most of the world's industrial carbon emissions.



\subsection{Fusion Propulsion And Epstein Drives}\label{sec:epstein_drives}
The fast projectiles from \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} could propel a spacecraft around the inner solar system at relatively high speeds.   As our technology advances, we'd want to travel on brachistochrone trajectories under constant Earth gravity acceleration between the planets as envisioned in \textit{The Expanse} \cite{Corey2012Drive}.  Evaporative cooling, paired with high-reflectivity coatings inspired by NASA's yttrium-oxide ``solar white'' work for cryo-tank thermal control \cite{nasa_solar_white_y2o3}, could in principle enable spacecraft to approach the Sun to 2 solar radii. That's closer than the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii \cite{nasa2018parker_heat}.  At this close solar periapsis, prograde and retrograde projectile collision velocities would exceed \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025} in the reference frame of a fusion pulsed propulsion chamber on the target rocket.  

A 1979 Los Alamos Impact Fusion workshop concluded speeds of just \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} might be sufficient for precompressed gram sized fusion fuel projectiles to ignite \cite{impactfusion1979}.   Larger projectiles would likely be easier to confine long enough for high fusion energy gain.  Head-on thermalization alone still falls short of ignition. At \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed, each deuterium--tritium ion carries about \SI{2.4}{\kilo\electronvolt} of kinetic energy, which thermalizes near \SI{1}{\kilo\electronvolt} once electrons take their share. An igniting hotspot, the small region whose fusion output heats the fuel around it, needs more than \SI{5}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{murakami2005impact}. The gap is a factor of about 2.5 in velocity, and it must be closed only in that small region, not in the bulk fuel. Energy is not the constraint. Each kilogram of colliding mass carries about \SI{94}{\giga\joule}, while an igniter deposits only tens of kilojoules \cite{murakami2005impact}. The design problem is concentration in space and time, not supply.

Inertial confinement fusion long ago learned to separate the two jobs. Assemble the fuel cold and dense, then heat the hotspot fast. Both halves now have impact-driven precedents. First Light Fusion reported fusion neutrons in 2022, independently reviewed by the UKAEA, by firing a projectile at \SI{6.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} into a shaped target \cite{firstlight2022fusion}. The target's internal geometry converted the planar impact into a convergent implosion and drove the target material past \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Murakami and Nagatomo's impact ignition scheme supplies the spark. A small, dense fragment striking precompressed fuel at about \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter\per\second} converts its kinetic energy into a hotspot above \SI{5}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{murakami2005impact}. Osaka experiments later accelerated deuterated foils to \SI{600}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and measured a hundredfold neutron yield increase when the collision was timed to peak compression \cite{azechi2009impact}. Laser ablation strains to push igniters to such speeds. Orbital mechanics hands us \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} before the target does any work at all.

A single passive target can encode the whole sequence, with a tamper of tungsten or another dense metal surrounding a few hundred grams of fusion fuel. Layers graded from heavy to light launch a ramped compression wave into the fuel rather than one strong shock. A single shock compresses an ideal monatomic gas at most fourfold and sets a high adiabat, the entropy floor that resists further squeezing, while a ramp keeps the fuel cold so the same energy buys far more density \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. A tungsten anvil sits at the stagnation point. A recessed central fuel column, delayed by its own geometry as in First Light's targets, arrives at full closing speed only after the ramp has finished. Fuel stagnating against the anvil's high shock impedance (density times shock speed, which sets how velocity partitions at an interface) is re-shocked by reflection, reaching roughly twice the temperature of a fuel-on-fuel collision at the same speed. The tungsten itself must stay out of the fuel. Bremsstrahlung losses grow with the square of atomic number, so high-Z mix is the classic hotspot killer, and a thin low-Z liner such as carbon buffers the interface while preserving the reflected shock. The liner does not weaken the anvil because reflection strength is set by what stands behind the interface. The first reflection off the carbon is soft, but the shock reverberates between fuel and tungsten inside the thin layer, and after a few round trips the pressure at the fuel face converges to what a bare tungsten wall would deliver \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. A liner much thinner than the fuel column keeps those round trips short compared to the stagnation time, so the fuel reaches nearly the full re-shock temperature without touching a tungsten atom. The anvil's own heating is incidental. Shocked tungsten carries the most energy per atom and glows hardest in X-rays, and the carbon screens the fuel from that glow as well. Key elements of the anvil-on-fuel architecture already run in the laboratory, though driven by lasers rather than impact. Los Alamos double shell capsules for the National Ignition Facility drive a tungsten or molybdenum inner shell directly onto deuterium--tritium fuel, shock heating it toward volume ignition near \SI{3}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{montgomery2018doubleshell}. Later designs grade beryllium into the tungsten to tame instability growth and mix at that same metal--fuel interface \cite{stark2024gradedshells}, the same low-Z buffering proposed here. The surrounding tamper still provides inertial confinement and acts as a radiation case, keeping shock-generated X-rays off the cryogenic fuel during compression (see \autoref{sec:radiative_differences}). The design keeps the staging lesson of thermonuclear weapons, compress first and ignite second \cite{doe_hbomb_history}, but the spark plug is kinetic rather than fissile. Pulsed fusion propulsion would likely still employ the shaped charges Project Orion explored to redirect energy and thrust.

The same scheme scales to the shallower dives this paper plans. Closing speeds run about \SI{394}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii, \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii (see \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), and \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 2 solar radii. Against the roughly \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter\per\second} igniter threshold, the graded stack must amplify the central column's speed by factors of about 2.5, 1.6, and 1.15. Shock breakout from a heavy layer into a lighter one can about double the particle velocity, so one stage of grading covers the two deeper dives. Parker depth needs compounded stages whose fast layers grow thin and rarefied, costing the igniter the density impact ignition wants, so ignition there is marginal rather than excluded. The architecture degrades gracefully either way. A fizzle still delivers the full kinetic pulse, so fusion multiplies pulse energy rather than gating the propulsion.

The multiplier is not free, and its price is thermal. A deuterium--tritium pulse sends 80\% of its yield out as \SI{14.1}{\mega\electronvolt} neutrons that no magnetic nozzle can turn and no low-Z liner can stop, so the coils behind it need meter-thick shielding with radiators of its own \cite{orth2003vista}. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} works the comparison and finds that a purely kinetic pulse of the same energy costs neither the shield nor its heat rejection. Fusion has to buy back that mass in added thrust before it is worth carrying, which is the practical argument for the aneutronic fuel below.

With mostly aneutronic fusion fuel, projectiles could reach velocities on the order of thousands of kilometers per second.  Aneutronic fusion directly converts energy into charged particle kinetic energy, bypassing the mass penalty of neutron shielding.  A seed of deuterium-tritium could ignite a larger deuterium and helium-3 mixture to achieve aneutronic fusion rocket speeds.   As the spacecraft accelerates, it can also reach speeds where it triggers fusion by crashing into slower moving fusion fuel targets.   If carefully planned, these chains of fast-moving projectiles could create interplanetary highways, allowing people to travel between planets under comfortable artificial Earth gravity in time frames comparable to ocean liners crossing the Atlantic.  These highways adapt Kare's interstellar fusion runway (see \autoref{sec:pellet_prior_art}) to interplanetary trips \cite{kare1997buzzbomb, greason2020runway}.  

\subsection{The Sixth Question For Climate Change}\label{sec:sith_question}
Bill Gates' excellent book, \textit{How to Avoid a Climate Disaster} \cite{gates2021avoid}, details five practical questions \cite{breakthroughenergy_2021_five} any effective climate solution must address. Our Straw Way To Heaven (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) addresses Gates' questions better than other alternatives. It zeroes carbon emissions, reverses cement emissions, and supplies indefinite global power at marginal costs that decline with output.

Yet I propose a sixth question that could rally public support for a particular climate solution. Beyond just reducing our electric bills and helping the environment, how will this breakthrough improve daily life? An investment on the scale required to solve climate change should grant human society new abilities, such as hypervelocity transport and affordable space travel. PuffSat pulsed propulsion can achieve all of these things.  

\section{When We Get Greedy, We'll Go to Phoebe}\label{sec:greedy_phoebe}
In \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} we discussed using Sun-grazing Oberth maneuvers to accelerate projectiles to \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. These high velocities enable the large $\Delta V$ required to place the next suborbital rocket into prograde and retrograde colliding orbits near the Sun.  However, as detailed in \autoref{sec:periapsis_challenges}, such close solar approaches introduce severe engineering and operational challenges, increasing projectile costs compared to less extreme solar encounters.  Suborbital rockets used to launch from Earth might also produce unacceptable levels of pollution in the upper atmosphere.

Orbits with higher apoapsis require less $\Delta V$ to shed orbital angular momentum and lower their periapsis. For example, transferring to a solar impact trajectory from Saturn requires only about \SI{10}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, compared to roughly \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second} from Earth \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. This makes Saturn a more efficient departure point for reaching the inner solar system or the Sun.

Saturn's irregular moon Phoebe lies just \SI{6.6}{\degree} from the solar ecliptic plane (its orbital inclination of \SI{173.4}{\degree} falls \SI{6.6}{\degree} short of a perfectly retrograde \SI{180}{\degree}) \cite{phoebe}, giving it a favorable alignment for interplanetary transfers. Its high-altitude orbit around Saturn yields a low orbital velocity, enabling efficient low $\Delta V$ transfers to elliptical trajectories that match Phoebe's apoapsis and dip toward Saturn at periapsis.  From Phoebe, a prograde rocket could be launched to collide with retrograde payloads also originating from Phoebe. These collisions could take place in a pulsed propulsion chamber near Saturn, enabling highly efficient Oberth maneuvers. With careful targeting to avoid Saturn's rings, this method could launch spacecraft toward the inner solar system. Precisely aiming such collisions near Saturn is challenging, but likely easier than attempting similar maneuvers at the extreme conditions of close solar periapsis described in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. PuffSats launched from Saturn toward Earth could power a Straw Way generator (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) or deliver suborbital rockets into orbit (see \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}).

These rockets could also return to Phoebe at high speeds, launching additional mass toward Saturn via pulsed propulsion. This bootstrapped launch cycle from Phoebe mirrors the lunar exponential growth loop discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}, but benefits both from Phoebe's lower escape velocity and from Saturn's larger gravitational Oberth effect. Power for mining operations on Phoebe could come from solar energy concentrated using lenses made from Phoebe's native ice or locally produced plastic. Fission reactors transported from Earth are another possibility, as is thermal energy generated by returning impactors.  One key advantage kinetic impact energy offers is that its power doesn't decline quadratically with solar distance, unlike solar panels.

With a bulk density consistent with roughly 50\% ice by mass \cite{castillo_rogez2012_phoebe} and surface ice mapped by Cassini \cite{phoebe}, Phoebe offers nearly unlimited volatile resources to fill PuffSats. Unlike the Moon's permanently shadowed ice deposits, Phoebe's ice-rich regions receive direct sunlight. Although faint, this illumination enables solar concentrators to generate power directly adjacent to mining operations. Phoebe's abundant water could be used to wash regolith from equipment, mitigating the mechanical fouling that plagues lunar hardware. Its regolith, composed largely of frozen volatiles, is likely softer and less abrasive than the Moon's harsh dust, reducing wear on machinery. By contrast, lunar volatiles are scarce, and oxygen extraction from lunar regolith (see \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}) is highly energy-intensive.

Phoebe's ultra-low gravity makes it difficult to anchor mining infrastructure securely. However, its volatile-rich composition allows another approach: retro-rockets powered by in-situ steam or chemical propellants manufactured from Phoebe's volatiles. These thrusters press equipment against the surface and absorb the recoil of mining and launch operations, supplying the hold-down force that gravity cannot. To save energy, icy reaction mass could also be launched mechanically from Phoebe's surface without first converting it to steam. If Phoebe's gravity proves too low for certain operations, Iapetus could be a backup site. Similarly, Jupiter's moon Himalia may support analogous maneuvers if exploration reveals sizable volatile deposits.

Mining moons like Phoebe to supply PuffSat payloads is easier than extracting conventional raw materials for Earth delivery. PuffSats execute high-velocity flybys, eliminating the need for costly deceleration maneuvers or bulky reentry shielding near Earth. And unlike ice or gold, the ultra-high-energy-density pulses generated by PuffSat impacts are irreproducible. No known terrestrial technology, short of nuclear weapons, can produce such large-scale, high-density energy bursts.

\subsection{Mining Helium-3} \label{sec:mining_helium_3}
Helium-3 for aneutronic fusion may be available on the Earth's moon \cite{wittenberg1986lunar_he3}, but likely not in quantities to support large-scale fast interplanetary transport as discussed in \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}.   However, the gas giants contain vast supplies of helium-3 \cite{palaszewski2005atmospheric}.   A nuclear fission thermal rocket or scramjet could extract helium-3 and hydrogen while flying in Saturn's atmosphere and then jump a few thousand kilometers above the atmosphere.  It could then separate from a payload carrying mined helium-3, falling back to Saturn to mine more.  An accident on such a fission rocket would cause no harm, because the heavy fission byproducts would sink to Saturn's core, with no surface to contaminate. Propulsion PuffSats from Phoebe could then send the helium-3 payload into the inner solar system for use in fusion rockets.  

If fission rockets prove politically unpalatable, chemical rockets may also work.   Chemical rocket fuel powers a motor in Saturn's atmosphere for the energy to mine helium-3.  The rocket then fires its engines to hop above Saturn's atmosphere.   Propulsion PuffSats from Phoebe push the rocket into a stable orbit where it is refueled and releases its helium-3 payload.  Then new propulsion PuffSats decelerate the refueled rocket so that it falls into Saturn's atmosphere for another mining round.

\subsection{A Ceres-ly Good Alternative}\label{sec:ceresly_good}
Ceres may offer a Goldilocks environment for sourcing PuffSat materials. Pre-Dawn modeling of its low bulk density put Ceres' water-ice content at roughly 17 to 27\% by mass \cite{mccord_sotin2005ceres}, and the Dawn mission later found ice within \SI{1}{\meter} of the surface at high latitudes \cite{prettyman2017dawn_ceres_water}. Near-surface deposits may also contain nearly 20\% carbon \cite{marchi2019_ceres_carbon}, enabling in-situ production of methane and other volatiles. Nitrogen-rich phyllosilicates found on Ceres are promising for fabricating micro-explosives \cite{desanctis2015_ceres_ammoniated_phyllosilicates}. In short, Ceres contains all the essential chemistry for manufacturing icy PuffSats on-site (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}).

Ceres offers adequate solar illumination, gravity sufficient for mining infrastructure, shorter transit times than Phoebe, and a low escape velocity. A solar impact trajectory from Ceres requires only about \SI{18}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, considerably less than the roughly \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second} required from Earth \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. This lower $\Delta V$ supports Ceres-based launch cycles that harness the Oberth effect near the Sun with less extreme perihelion or increased payload capacity per cycle relative to Earth-based alternatives (see \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}).

Achieving centimeter-accuracy PuffSat interceptions around Ceres may prove easier than in most other prime solar system locations. In high-altitude, easily accessible orbits above Ceres, PuffSats would encounter negligible unmodeled forces. Atmospheric drag is absent, magnetic interference is small, solar illumination perturbations are modest, and deviations from modeled gravity are weak. Jupiter's influence is non-negligible but predictable. 

Rather than relying on active solar tracking in the dim sunlight around Ceres' ice-rich poles, solar power depots can be stationed in polar sun-synchronous orbits. These orbital platforms maintain continuous exposure to sunlight, bypassing the thermal cycling and power interruptions caused by Ceres' night. If we make transparent plastics like acrylic or polycarbonate from Ceres' raw material, we can produce thick concentrating optics that shield photovoltaic arrays from micrometeorites and space radiation. We favor UV-stable acrylic over polycarbonate, which yellows under ultraviolet \cite{polycarbonate_uv_aging}, and vacuum-bake the optic before flight so its outgassing does not fog the array \cite{outgassing_rp1124}. Outgassing rate also falls steeply as temperature drops, following an Arrhenius dependence \cite{outgassing_temperature_decay}, so at Ceres' orbital temperatures, more than \SI{200}{\kelvin} below the \SI{125}{\celsius} screening test, a bake-dried optic sheds volatiles far more slowly.
