Should plastic prove too lossy over time, a fused-silica optic drawn from the same regolith avoids both, drawing on fused silica's long use as the spacecraft window material from Apollo to the ISS \cite{fused_silica_windows}.  A reflective concentrator, an aluminized mirror instead of a transmissive lens, is a further option and avoids transmission losses altogether. This shielding extends solar panel lifespans without imported mass from Earth. Ceres' low gravity enables lofting mined carbon and water into orbit with minimal rocket propulsion. Once in orbit, these volatiles can be converted into fuel at solar-powered satellites.  This fuel maintains satellite orbits, enables return trips to Ceres, and powers mining operations on the surface.   We close the loop by mining more volatiles on Ceres and taking them back to the solar satellites for fuel conversion.

Converting those volatiles into fuel admits two chemistries. Methane and oxygen (methalox), both made from Ceres' carbon and water, are the workhorse and need far less cooling than hydrogen to store as liquids. Liquid hydrogen and oxygen are the higher-performance option, and the passive sunshade from \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} is what makes hydrogen practical this far out. A shaded radiator equilibrates with the attenuated sunlight, so its temperature falls as the inverse square root of heliocentric distance. Anchored on the JWST sunshield's \SI{40}{\kelvin} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} \cite{jwst_sunshield}, that is about \SI{24}{\kelvin} at Ceres near \SI{2.77}{\astronomicalunit}. Hydrogen's critical temperature, the warmest at which any pressure still holds it liquid, is \SI{33}{\kelvin} \cite{nist_webbook_hydrogen}, so a passive shade keeps hydrogen liquid only beyond about \SI{1.5}{\astronomicalunit}. At the Moon or Mercury no shade works at any pressure, while at Ceres liquid hydrogen sits at a few bar. The perihelion-lowering departure burn is made at Ceres' aphelion, with the inner leg flown ballistically or by PuffSat collision, so the hydrogen is produced and burned cold, never carried hot. That buys the higher specific impulse of hydrogen and oxygen, roughly \SI{450}{\second} against about \SI{360}{\second} for methalox \cite{sutton_rocket_propulsion}. The gain serves the local logistics rockets these operations already need: return trips, orbit maintenance, mining hold-down and retro braking, and the Saturn helium-3 miner (see \autoref{sec:mining_helium_3}). Closer to the Sun, where no shade can keep a cryogen liquid, the same stored water is electrolyzed on demand with onboard solar power. It feeds either a chemical thruster (Imperial's ICE-Cube \cite{imperial_ice_cube}) or a Hall-effect thruster (Imperial's water-electrolysis Hall thruster \cite{schwertheim2022wet_het}), the same make-it-on-demand approach as the PuffSat fine-control thrusters (see \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}).

Unlike Phoebe, Ceres lacks a nearby planetary body for Oberth maneuvers. However, PuffSats returning from solar periapsis can deliver momentum for high-$\Delta V$ trajectories, either back to Earth or towards close perihelion. These returning PuffSats also offer an alternative to solar power. Their controlled impacts can be used to explosively excavate rocky overburden and transiently melt large quantities of subsurface ice, enabling easy pumping to fill new PuffSats.

\subsection{Fast Money Sound Nice?  Why Not Tap Mercury's Ice?}
Mercury harbors an estimated 20 billion to 1 trillion tons of water ice at its permanently shadowed poles \cite{lawrence2013mercury_ice}, with graphite as the dominant darkening agent across its surface at a few weight percent \cite{peplowski2016mercury_carbon}. These resources enable in-situ fabrication of icy PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}), which can be filled with harvested water. Heat-resistant PuffSat skins and structural plastics could be synthesized from graphite-derived carbon and ice, forming ISRU-enabled projectiles.

PuffSats launched from Mercury into low-perihelion orbits (see \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}) would return at extremely high velocities, ideal for PuffSat propulsion. Mercury's proximity to the Sun offers abundant solar energy for surface operations. Its short orbital period also turns launches around far faster than at Earth or destinations beyond \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} such as Phoebe or Ceres, supporting exponential scaling of launch capacity.

However, landing on Mercury remains challenging due to its deep position within the Sun's gravity well, which demands large deceleration \cite{benkhoff2021bepicolombo}. PuffSats approaching an incoming craft opposite its direction of motion could fully decelerate the craft by crashing into its pusher plate.

Key disadvantages include Mercury's harsh regolith, extreme thermal environment, and the high $\Delta V$ required to launch into retrograde solar trajectories. To overcome this high $\Delta V$ requirement, projectiles would collide at somewhat lower perihelion, gaining additional return velocity.  Higher return velocities provide greater kinetic energy for a retrograde solar injection from Mercury.

\subsection{An Atmosphere That \textit{Mars} Dreams of PuffSat Propulsion}\label{sec:mars_skyhook}
Despite Mars' abundant raw materials, its atmosphere makes it a less-than-ideal environment for sourcing the water ice needed to fill icy PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}). A system is needed to get the mined resources above the Martian atmosphere before PuffSat pulsed propulsion can push them on their way. One solution could be to use a rotating skyhook, similar to the Boeing/Tethers Unlimited HASTOL proposal \cite{skyhook_hastol}.

A skyhook with a tip speed of approximately $\SI{3.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ could effectively match and cancel the orbital velocity of a low Mars orbit, enabling direct payload transfer. This tip-speed regime matches the tapered high-modulus polyethylene (HMPE) tether architecture analyzed in the HASTOL Phase II study \cite{hastol_phase_ii}, which examined an Earth skyhook using HMPE fiber \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}; the Mars scaling here is our own extrapolation.  A spinning skyhook holds steady tension for years, the regime where polyethylene creeps, so the tether wants a creep-free fiber. PBO carries about \num{1.6} times Vectran's specific strength at similarly low creep \cite{zylon_technical_info}, which holds tether mass down (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). A UV-opaque metallized coating is intended to shield the tip from the sunlight, atomic oxygen, and abrasive dust it meets at perigee, though such tether coatings still need orbital qualification \cite{hastol_phase_ii}, and Mars' dry environment largely suppresses PBO's hydrolysis weakness.  The skyhook itself could be reaccelerated by PuffSats returning at high speeds from solar perihelion, providing pulsed propulsion.

However, since Ceres does not require a skyhook and offers other advantages (see \autoref{sec:ceresly_good}), it appears to be a superior location for early PuffSat mining.



\section{War, Policy, And Pulsed Propulsion}
\subsection{Fusion Rockets And The Outer Space Treaty}
The Outer Space Treaty \cite{outer_space_treaty} prohibits nuclear weapons in space. The legality of pure fusion rockets like those discussed in \autoref{sec:epstein_drives} requires clarification, likely through international dialogue and new legal frameworks.  Large ``cruise ship'' vehicles traveling on brachistochrone trajectories at extreme velocities would possess enormous kinetic energies. A large cruise ship displaces on the order of \SI{1e8}{\kilo\gram} \cite{livescience_cruise_displacement}.  At \SI{500}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it would carry roughly \SI{1.3e19}{\joule}, about \SI{3000}{\mega\tonne} of TNT equivalent, some 60 times the yield of Tsar Bomba, the largest device ever detonated \cite{tsar_bomba_ahf}. As such, the deployment of these systems demands fail-safe, enforceable regulatory and safety protocols.

\subsection{Hackers Must Be Prevented From Causing Catastrophic Explosions}
As mentioned in \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}, the energy projectiles for the Straw Way To Heaven discussed in \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven} are intentionally designed to burn up high in the atmosphere without causing damage in case of a mishap.  However, a hacker could manipulate microthrusters on the projectiles to enter the atmosphere simultaneously rather than in sequence, leading to Tunguska-sized \cite{longo2007tunguska} multi-megaton explosions.  We can mitigate this risk with standard software security practices like using memory-safe languages and authenticated communication.

\subsection{Risk Of PuffSat Sabotage}
PuffSat pulsed propulsion might be useful for launching military assets like Golden Dome \cite{crs_golden_dome} or the Brilliant Boulders \cite{brilliant_boulders} discussed on the blog related to this paper \cite{aim2024}.   The PuffSats described in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel} are likely vulnerable to destruction from high-powered terrestrial lasers by a military adversary.  Nations will need to defend their payloads from sabotage by hostile actors.

\subsection{Aviation Exclusion Zone Needed For Straw Ways}
Since the Straw Way To Heaven (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) is a fixed structure in the sky, we will need regulations to ban aviation in its vicinity.   This seems feasible given the Straw Way will be placed in a remote area.   The host nation must also guard the Straw Way against sabotage.


\subsection{Overflight Of Third Countries And Strategic Stability}\label{sec:overflight_politics}
A suborbital point-to-point vehicle overflies countries between its departure and destination. Sovereignty above the Kármán line near \SI{100}{\kilo\meter} is not settled in law \cite{mcdowell2018karman}, so this overflight sits in a legal gray zone. Interception is one risk. Nations have shot down civilian airliners before, as with MH17 over Ukraine in 2014 \cite{dsb_mh17}. This violated the Chicago Convention, which prohibits the use of weapons against civil aircraft in flight \cite{chicago_3bis}. A vehicle on a ballistic arc can also be misread as a missile attack by early-warning systems.

Because the intercity vehicle reaches orbital velocity before reentry (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}), it can avoid a sensitive region by flying the long way around the great circle. The vehicle stays on orbit through the far arc and deorbits on the approach to the destination. This costs extra flight time rather than much additional $\Delta V$, so it is the simplest way to route around a contested region. If the long way is unacceptable, the vehicle can instead change course at a waypoint to steer around the region. That course change can come from an onboard correction burn or from a second PuffSat formation positioned along the path.

Flying most of the way around the planet at orbital velocity resembles a Fractional Orbital Bombardment System \cite{salt2_fobs}, which early-warning systems treat as a threat. This is best handled through policy rather than through routing. PuffSats are launched ahead of the flight into tracked orbits, and the vehicle departs a known airport on a published schedule. Monitoring systems then have time to tell a PuffSat-propelled craft from a surprise ground launch, given good-faith cooperation backed by shared tracking and prior notice. Pre-launch notification agreements already serve this purpose for ballistic missiles \cite{us_ussr_launch_notification}.

The real goal is a policy agreement not to extend armed conflict into civilian space transit. The norm against attacking civil aircraft \cite{chicago_3bis} should extend to suborbital and orbital passenger travel, consistent with the peaceful-use principle of the Outer Space Treaty \cite{outer_space_treaty}.

\subsection{Don't Sweat the Stratosphere (But Do Watch It)}\label{sec:ozone_policy}\cite{carlson1997smallstuff}
SpaceX's Earth-to-Earth Starship (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) brakes by hitting the atmosphere at orbital speed. The bow shock, the shell of compressed air ahead of the vehicle, heats the surrounding gas past several thousand kelvin and makes nitric oxide out of ordinary nitrogen and oxygen. Together, this nitric oxide and the nitrogen dioxide it forms (\ce{NO_x}) make up one of the largest ozone-depletion terms for present-day spaceflight, on par with the chlorine from solid rocket motors \cite{ryan2022rocket}. A PuffSat suborbital plane has another route. It sheds orbital velocity against a retrograde formation of reentry PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) and enters the air slowly, with a weaker shock and far less \ce{NO_x}. Their bulk of ice, carbon, and propellant gasifies to water, nitrogen, oxygen, and carbon dioxide, largely avoiding the metal-oxide pathway flagged for reentering satellites \cite{ferreira2024ozone, barker2026megaconstellation}, though a full accounting would still include the small electronics, battery, and thruster masses each PuffSat carries. The braking is not free. The reentry PuffSats are extra mass that had to be launched, and a slow entry means much less \ce{NO_x}, not zero. Removing the dominant ozone term by design is still a real gain over aerobraking.

Water is the residual worth watching. Both the suborbital rocket burn and the Starship that lofts the PuffSats inject water vapor into the stratosphere on the way up. At the scale of replacing airline travel, that input deserves monitoring. Stratospheric water mildly depletes ozone \cite{ryan2022rocket} and adds a modest greenhouse warming \cite{solomon2010stratospheric}, but it clears in a few years, up to about a decade for the highest injections \cite{hunga2026residence}. That is far below the roughly \SIrange{50}{100}{\year} atmospheric lifetimes of the CFCs \cite{wmo2022ozone} and the centuries over which carbon dioxide persists \cite{ipcc2021ar6}. The water the PuffSats themselves release sits near \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}, well above the \SIrange{15}{35}{\kilo\meter} ozone layer. There it photodissociates into hydrogen and hydroxyl, and the light hydrogen escapes to space, so most of it never reaches the ozone below, though some may. The short residence time and the avoidable \ce{NO_x} point to one policy. Monitor the stratospheric water and \ce{NO_x} burden as suborbital traffic grows, and gate expansion to the measured impact rather than capping flights in advance.

\section{Conclusion And Future Directions}
This paper introduced PuffSat pulsed propulsion. We've argued that a PuffSat's navigation, supported by a preliminary closed-loop simulation \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026} and by advances in formation flying, could make hypersonic intercity transport practical. PuffSats also offer cheap orbital launch, a path to reversing global warming, abundant affordable power, and solar-system settlement with fusion propulsion.

Next steps: cross-check the preliminary control and impact simulations against independent tools, extend the control simulation to the rocket side of the interception, and prototype the control software as flight code. We also plan to solicit feedback on current challenges impeding these concepts and assess whether they're economically and technically surmountable.

If the basic physics of PuffSat pulsed propulsion work, we'd likely proceed with different technologies in different time frames. An aspirational ``Elon time'' \cite{isaacson2023musk,benzinga2023elontime} roadmap of when each technology could be developed is proposed in \autoref{tab:schedule_future_work}.


\begin{table}[htpb]
    \centering
    \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|c|L|}\hline
        \textbf{Aspirational Time Frame} & \textbf{Action} \\\hline
        Now & Prototype satellite launch and hypersonic travel using PuffSats launched by large reusable rockets\\\hline
        Now & Prototype satellite launch using gravity assists with Jupiter or the inner planets as described in \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} \\\hline
        Near Term & Prototype launches using lunar volatiles or mined lunar oxygen instead of large reusable rockets \\\hline
        Mid Term & Prototype using close solar approaches or material from Phoebe/Saturn for PuffSat pulsed propulsion \\\hline
        Mid to Long Term & Prototype constructing a Straw Way To Heaven and energy infrastructure based on it \\\hline
        After the Singularity (Far Distant Future) & Prototype fusion rockets and interplanetary highways \\\hline
    \end{tabularx}
    \caption{Proposed schedule for future work}
    \label{tab:schedule_future_work}
\end{table}

Marc Andreessen once said ``software is eating the world'' \cite{andreessen_software}. If control software enables PuffSat pulsed propulsion, it will devour the solar system as well.

\appendix 

\section{PuffSat Cold-Gas Propellant: A Pessimistic Upper-Bound Estimate}\label{sec:estimate_cold_gas}

We scale GOCE to a geometrically similar \(\SI{25}{\kg}\) PuffSat composed of low-density ANFO (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}). This gives a rough, intentionally pessimistic upper bound for cold-gas propellant needs. We model GOCE as a cylinder and assume to first order that drag scales with surface area. Our conservative multiplicative assumptions follow.

\subsection*{Geometry And Geometric Scaling}
Approximate GOCE's slender drag-minimizing body \cite{goce_dragfree} as a cylinder with diameter \(D_0=\SI{1.0}{\m}\) and length \(L_0=\SI{4.0}{\meter}\). Then the radius \(r_0=\tfrac{D_0}{2}=\SI{0.5}{\meter}\), and
\[
V_0 = \pi r_0^2 L_0 = \pi\cdot(0.5)^2\cdot 4 = \pi\ \mathrm{m^3},
\qquad
S_0 = 2\pi r_0 L_0 + 2\pi r_0^2 = \tfrac{9}{2}\pi\ \mathrm{m^2}.
\]

Let the PuffSat mass be \(m=\SI{25}{\kg}\) and assume an ANFO density
\(\rho=\SI{840}{\kg\per\m^3}\). The PuffSat volume is
\[
V=\frac{m}{\rho}.
\]

If the PuffSat is geometrically similar to GOCE, the linear scale factor \(s\) satisfies
\[
V = s^3 V_0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
s = \left(\frac{V}{V_0}\right)^{1/3}.
\]
Surface area scales as \(S = s^2 S_0\), so the area ratio is
\[
\frac{S}{S_0} = s^2 = \left(\frac{m/\rho}{V_0}\right)^{2/3}.
\]

\paragraph{Numeric evaluation (ANFO, \(\rho=\SI{840}{\kg\per\m^3}\)).}
\[
\begin{aligned}
V &= \frac{25}{840}\ \mathrm{m^3} \approx 0.0297619\ \mathrm{m^3},\\[4pt]
s &= \left(\frac{0.0297619}{\pi}\right)^{1/3} \approx 0.2116,\\[4pt]
\frac{S}{S_0} &= s^2 \approx 0.0447721 \quad(\text{PuffSat area } \approx 4.48\%\ \text{of GOCE}),\\[4pt]
\text{Equivalently: } & S_0 \approx 22.335 \times S.
\end{aligned}
\]

\noindent\textbf{Remark:} geometric scaling predicts \(\sim22\times\) reduction in area. To remain conservative we \emph{assume} only a \(\times 10\) reduction in drag. Explicitly: although area scales \(\approx22\times\) smaller, we take drag to be only \(10\times\) smaller.

\subsection*{Baseline Thrust Assumption}
GOCE used up to \(\SI{20}{\milli\newton}\) for drag-free control (solar minimum) \cite{goce_dragfree}. We allow up to \(\times 10\) larger thrust during solar maximum. This gives \(\SI{200}{\milli\newton}\) as a worst-case GOCE-equivalent value. This solar maximum penalty is probably conservative, since we can time PuffSats to enter low atmosphere at night. Applying the pessimistic \(\times 10\) weaker drag for the PuffSat (above) yields a continuous required thrust of
\[
F_1 = \frac{\SI{200}{\milli\newton}}{10} = \SI{20}{\milli\newton}
\]
at the representative altitude (\(\sim\)\SI{250}{\kilo\metre}) used in this estimate. 

We further impose a short-duration surge case: an instantaneous increase in drag by a factor of \(20\) for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\) between \(\SI{250}{\kilo\metre}\) and \(\SI{200}{\kilo\metre}\). This deliberately conservative bound requires
\[
F_2 = 20\times F_1 = \SI{400}{\milli\newton}
\]
for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\).

\subsection*{Cold-Gas Mass Flow}
For a thruster with specific impulse \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}\) (seconds) and \(g_0=\SI{9.80665}{\m\per\s^2}\),
\[
\dot m = \frac{F}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}.
\]
We use a pessimistic \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\s}\), about half a typical CubeSat cold-gas value \cite{tummala2017cubesat} to account for miniaturization losses. This gives
\[
\dot m_{\text{per }1\ \mathrm{mN}} = \frac{1\times 10^{-3}}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx 4.079\times 10^{-6}\ \mathrm{kg/s} \approx 4.079\ \mathrm{mg/s}.
\]

\subsection*{Fuel Required For The Two Intervals}
\paragraph{Continuous interval.}
Required thrust \(F_1=\SI{20}{\milli\newton}\) sustained for \(t_1=\SI{300}{\s}\):
\[
m_1 = \frac{F_1 t_1}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}
= \frac{(20\times 10^{-3})(300)}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx \SI{24.473}{\g}.
\]

\paragraph{Short surge interval.}
Surge thrust \(F_2=\SI{400}{\milli\newton}\) sustained for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\):
\[
m_2 = \frac{F_2 t_2}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}
= \frac{(400\times 10^{-3})(30)}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx \SI{48.946}{\g}.
\]

\paragraph{Baseline propellant:}
\[
m_{\mathrm{base}} = m_1 + m_2 \approx \SI{73.4196}{\g}.
\]

\subsection*{Margins And Final Rounding}
Apply the following conservative margins. GOCE operated at a representative altitude of $\approx \SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ and successfully descended to as low as $\SI{229}{\kilo\meter}$ \cite{goce_229km}. Atmospheric density rises sharply at lower altitudes. We model an instantaneous drag jump as PuffSat descends from $\SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ to our \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} target. GOCE's designers treated $\SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ as nominal because it offered more reliable operational conditions.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Add \(\SI{20}{\g}\) for attitude/torque control: \(m_{\mathrm{a}} = m_{\mathrm{base}} + \SI{20}{\g} \approx \SI{93.4196}{\g}.\)
  \item Double for pulsing inefficiencies and other control limitations: \(m_{\mathrm{b}} = 2 m_{\mathrm{a}} \approx \SI{186.8392}{\g}.\)
  \item Apply an additional doubling factor to account for drag at PuffSat's higher velocity, which scales as \(v^2\) (since $\frac{(\text{PuffSat's speed})^2}{(\text{GOCE's speed})^2} = \frac{\left(\SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}\right)^2}{\left(\SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}\right)^2}
 \approx 2$): \(m_{\mathrm{c}} = 2 m_{\mathrm{b}} \approx \SI{373.6784}{\g}.\)

\end{enumerate}

Round up for presentation/engineering margin:
\[
\boxed{m_{\mathrm{req}} \approx \SI{374}{\g}\ \text{(computed value); rounded to }\SI{400}{\g}\ \text{for conservative margin}.}
\]

\subsection*{Notes And Explicit Assumptions}
\begin{itemize}
  \item We explicitly made two large conservative choices: (1) although geometric area scaling predicts \(\sim22\times\) lower drag, we assume only a \(10\times\) reduction (pessimistic), and (2) we include an extreme short-duration surge of \(20\times\) the baseline drag for \(\SI{30}{\s}\).
  \item The specific impulse \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\s}\) is pessimistic. A higher \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}\) reduces propellant need proportionally.
  \item The final \(\SI{400}{\g}\) figure is a rounded engineering margin. The computed baseline with margins is \(\approx\SI{374}{\g}\).
  \item What this appendix bounds is drag-cancellation propellant, not the tank load. The closed-loop simulation finds the mission dominated by aiming rather than drag, with the anti-drag leg costing well under a gram \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}, so the bound above overshoots that leg by more than two orders of magnitude. The tank is nonetheless sized at \(\SI{500}{\g}\) in \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}. The extra is not drag margin. The propellant doubles as the thermal reservoir that holds its own vapor pressure up through the terminal burn, and that job sets the load.
  \item Surface area calculations did not include a propellant tank. The baseline liquefied-propane tank of \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice} holds \SI{0.92}{\liter}, about 3\% of the PuffSat's \SI{30}{\liter} volume, so it changes the drag area negligibly. A high-pressure nitrogen tank would not be so kind. It needs \SI{1.32}{\liter} of internal volume plus a thick composite wall, a sizable fraction of the PuffSat's size, and drag would rise accordingly.
  \item The \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\second}\) assumption stays conservative for the baselined propellant. Flown CubeSat cold-gas systems deliver about \SI{43}{\second} on butane \cite{tummala2017cubesat}, and propane's lower molar mass puts it slightly above that, so the propellant figure here carries roughly a factor of two in hand.
  \item This is a conservative back-of-the-napkin upper bound. Second-order effects are not modeled.
  \item A preliminary closed-loop control simulation confirms this bound from the other direction. Where this appendix estimates propellant from GOCE drag scaling alone, the simulation flies the full interception: corrector burns, terminal guidance, finite-thrust execution, and a dispersed environment. It still holds the total guidance propellant under 2\% of the \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat mass, even in the worst sampled case \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}.
  \item \textbf{Acknowledgement:} ChatGPT extensively helped with this derivation and much of the text is copied from it verbatim \cite{chatgpt}.
\end{itemize}
% --- end ---


\section{Peroxide/Propane Bipropellant Burst Mode}\label{sec:peroxide_propane_biprop}

The burst mode of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters} can burn the oxygen from the peroxide's own decomposition against a small propane charge, instead of qualifying a separate green monopropellant. This appendix sizes the mixture, the flame temperature, and the specific impulse. Thermochemical data are from the NIST Chemistry WebBook \cite{nist_webbook_propane,nist_webbook_oxygen,nist_webbook_hydrogen}. Standard enthalpies of formation used below (\si{\kilo\joule\per\mole}): \ce{H2O2}(l) \(-187.8\), \ce{H2O}(l) \(-285.8\), \ce{H2O}(g) \(-241.8\), \ce{CO2}(g) \(-393.5\), \ce{C3H8}(g) \(-104.7\).

\subsection*{Basis And Decomposition}
Take \SI{1}{\kilo\gram} of \SI{60}{\percent} solution by mass: \SI{600}{\g} (\num{17.64} mol) of \ce{H2O2} and \SI{400}{\g} (\num{22.20} mol) of water. The peroxide decomposes as
\[
\ce{H2O2(l) -> H2O(l) + 1/2 O2(g)}, \qquad \Delta H = \SI{-98.0}{\kilo\joule\per\mole}.
\]
This yields \num{8.82} mol (\SI{282}{\g}) of \ce{O2}, adds \num{17.64} mol of water for \num{39.84} mol (\SI{718}{\g}) total, and releases \(17.64\times 98.0 = \SI{1729}{\kilo\joule}\). Fully vaporizing that water would cost \(39.84\times\SI{44.0}{\kilo\joule\per\mole}=\SI{1753}{\kilo\joule}\), slightly more than is released. The \SI{60}{\percent} decomposition therefore stays two-phase near \SIrange{373}{410}{\kelvin}, which is why the catalyst needs a preheat to light propane. A dry \SI{85}{\percent} feed clears that vaporization budget and emerges near \SI{890}{\kelvin}, hot enough to autoignite propane unaided.

\subsection*{Stoichiometry}
Burning propane with the liberated oxygen,
\[
\ce{C3H8 + 5 O2 -> 3 CO2 + 4 H2O},
\]
consumes \(8.82/5 = \num{1.764}\) mol (\SI{77.8}{\g}) of propane. The oxidizer-to-fuel mass ratio is \(1000/77.8 \approx \num{12.9}\), so propane is \(77.8/1077.8 = \SI{7.2}{\percent}\) of the propellant. Combustion to gaseous water releases \(1.764\times\SI{2043}{\kilo\joule\per\mole} = \SI{3604}{\kilo\joule}\). The products are \num{5.29} mol \ce{CO2} (\SI{233}{\g}) and \(39.84+7.06 = \num{46.90}\) mol \ce{H2O} (\SI{845}{\g}), for \num{52.19} mol total and a mean molar mass
\[
\overline{M} = \frac{\SI{1077.8}{\g}}{\num{52.19}\ \mathrm{mol}} \approx \SI{20.6}{\g\per\mole}.
\]

\subsection*{Adiabatic Flame Temperature}
Balancing reactant and product enthalpies at \SI{298}{\kelvin} with all product water as vapor,
\[
\Delta H_\mathrm{rxn} = \sum_\mathrm{prod}\! n\,\Delta H_f - \sum_\mathrm{react}\! n\,\Delta H_f \approx \SI{-3.58}{\mega\joule}
\]
per kilogram of solution. This heat raises the products from \SI{298}{\kelvin}. With mean molar heat capacities over the interval near \(c_{p,\ce{CO2}}\approx\SI{53}{\joule\per\mole\per\kelvin}\) and \(c_{p,\ce{H2O(g)}}\approx\SI{43}{\joule\per\mole\per\kelvin}\),
\[
T_f \approx \SI{298}{\kelvin} + \frac{\SI{3.58e6}{\joule}}{(5.29)(53) + (46.90)(43)\ \si{\joule\per\kelvin}} \approx \SI{1880}{\kelvin}.
\]
That is the low end of the \SIrange{1900}{2200}{\kelvin} range of the HAN and ADN monopropellants \cite{green_monoprop_review}, and well below the roughly \SI{2800}{\kelvin} of a dry stoichiometric propane and oxygen flame. The excess water in the \SI{60}{\percent} solution is what buys the difference.

\subsection*{Ideal Specific Impulse}
With \(T_c \approx \SI{1880}{\kelvin}\), \(\overline{M} = \SI{20.6}{\g\per\mole}\), and a product ratio of specific heats \(\gamma \approx 1.21\), the ideal exhaust velocity for a vacuum expansion with \(p_e/p_c \approx \num{e-3}\) is
\[
v_e = \sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}\,\frac{R_u T_c}{\overline{M}}\left[1-\left(\frac{p_e}{p_c}\right)^{\!\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}\right]} \approx \SI{2450}{\meter\per\second},
\]
so \(I_\mathrm{sp} \approx \SI{250}{\second}\) ideal. A more conservative expansion and the two-phase and boundary-layer losses of a millimeter-scale throat pull the delivered value toward \SI{230}{\second}, still comparable to the flown green monopropellants and far above a cold-gas blowdown.

\subsection*{Catalyst Mass}
At the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} surge and \(I_\mathrm{sp} \approx \SI{230}{\second}\), the total mass flow is \(F/(I_\mathrm{sp} g_0) \approx \SI{177}{\milli\gram\per\second}\), of which the peroxide is \SI{164}{\milli\gram\per\second}. Peroxide catalyst beds are sized by mass flux, with design values of order \SIrange{10}{55}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter\per\second} \cite{jung2021catalyst_bed_scaling}. The bed frontal area is then \(\SI{1.64e-4}{\kilo\gram\per\second}\) divided by that flux, or \SIrange{3}{14}{\square\milli\meter}, a core only a few millimeters across. A washcoat on a sub-cubic-centimeter bed at ordinary noble-metal loadings carries single-digit milligrams of platinum, so at the platinum spot price the metal is a fraction of a percent of a single PuffSat's cost.


\section{Derivation Of PuffSat Mass/Rocket Mass Continuous Approximation}\label{sec:PuffSat_ratio_approximation} We want to approximate the ratio of total PuffSat mass \(m_p\) to rocket mass \(m_r\). A sequence of PuffSats, all traveling at velocity \(v_p\), pushes the rocket from initial velocity \(v_{ri}\) to final velocity \(v_{rf}\). First, assume every collision is perfectly elastic in one dimension. We model the rocket as continuously bombarded by infinitesimal PuffSats, each with mass \(dm_p\), and solve for a closed-form expression. (Note: Grok \cite{grok} helped with some of the math for this derivation and parts of this derivation are copied from Grok results directly)

The initial velocities are
\begin{itemize}
\item Mass \( m_r \): Velocity \( v_r \).
\item PuffSat: Mass \( dm_p \), velocity \( v_p \).
\end{itemize}
After the collision, let:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Mass \( m_r \): Velocity \( v_r + dv_r \).
    \item PuffSat: Velocity \( v_p' \).
\end{itemize}
\subsection{By Conservation Of Momentum} \[
m_r v_r + dm_p v_p = m_r (v_r + dv_r) + dm_p v_p'
\]
\[
m_r v_r + dm_p v_p = m_r v_r + m_r dv_r + dm_p v_p'
\]
\[
dm_p v_p = m_r dv_r + dm_p v_p'
\]
\begin{equation}
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - v_p') \label{eq:momentum}
\end{equation}

\subsection[Velocity Change Of Rocket Mass]{Velocity Change Of \(m_r\)}
For an elastic collision
\begin{equation}
    v_p'\ = \frac{2m_r}{m_r+m_p}v_r + \frac{m_p-m_r}{m_r+m_p}v_p \label{eq:vb_prime_full_momentum}
\end{equation}
Since \(m_r \gg m_p\) we plug \(m_p = 0\) into \autoref{eq:vb_prime_full_momentum} and find 
\begin{equation}
v_p' = 2v_r - v_p \label{eq:vb_prime_result}
\end{equation} 

Substituting \(v_p'\) from \autoref{eq:vb_prime_result} into \autoref{eq:momentum}:
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - (2 v_r - v_p))
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - 2 v_r + v_p)
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (2 v_p - 2 v_r)
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = 2 dm_p (v_p - v_r)
\]
\begin{equation}
dv_r = \frac{2 (v_p - v_r)}{m_r} dm_p \label{eq:vchange_mr}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Integrate Over Many Collisions}
The velocity of \( m_r \) changes from \( v_{ri} \) to \( v_{rf} \) as more PuffSats collide. Integrate \autoref{eq:vchange_mr}:
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} dv_r = \int_0^{m_p} \frac{2 (v_p - v_r)}{m_r} dm_p
\]
The left-hand side is:
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} dv_r = v_{rf} - v_{ri}
\]
For the right-hand side, treat \( v_r \) as a function of the accumulated PuffSat mass \( m_p \). From \autoref{eq:vchange_mr}:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dv_r}{v_p - v_r} = \frac{2 dm_p}{m_r}\label{eq:dvr_velocity_relation}
\end{equation}

Since \(v_p\) is constant, \(dv_p = 0\) and \(dv_r = -d(v_p-v_r)\), so we can rewrite \autoref{eq:dvr_velocity_relation} as:
\[
-\frac{d(v_p - v_r)}{v_p - v_r} = \frac{2 dm_p}{m_r}
\]
Integrate both sides. The left side becomes
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} \frac{dv_r}{v_p - v_r}
= \left[ -\ln|v_p - v_r| \right]_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} = \ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right|,
\]
and the right side gives
\[
\int_0^{m_p} \frac{2\, dm_p}{m_r} = \frac{2 m_p}{m_r}.
\]
Equating the two:
\[
\ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| = \frac{2 m_p}{m_r}
\]
Solve for \( m_p \):
\[
\left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| = e^{2 m_p / m_r}
\]
\begin{equation}
m_p = \frac{m_r}{2} \ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| 
\end{equation}
\subsection{Interpret The Absolute Value} 
The absolute value accounts for the direction of velocities. When \( v_p > v_{ri} \) and \( v_p > v_{rf} \), both \( v_p - v_{ri} \) and \( v_p - v_{rf} \) are positive, so
\[
m_p = \frac{m_r}{2} \ln \left( \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right)
\]
\subsection{Compute PuffSat To Rocket Mass Ratio, With A Fudge Factor For Real World Losses}

Solve for the PuffSat-to-rocket mass ratio. Add a fudge factor $f$ in $(0,1)$ for restitution losses and imperfect volatile spread off the pusher plate. Assume $f$ is constant across collisions. In practice it varies with the relative PuffSat-rocket velocity. PuffSat sizes will in practice grow as the rocket nears $v_p$, since more mass is needed for the same acceleration.
\begin{equation}
\frac{m_r}{m_p} = \frac{2f}{\ln\left(\frac{v_p-v_{ri}}{v_p-v_{rf}}\right)}\label{eq:PuffSat_ratio}
\end{equation}

\section{Derivation Of Optimal Cable Taper}\label{sec:breaking_length_derived}

\paragraph{AI Acknowledgment}
Most of this derivation is copied directly from a solution worked out with a ChatGPT prompt \cite{chatgpt}.

\paragraph{Assumptions and notation.} 
Let $x$ denote the vertical coordinate measured downward from the top support ($x=0$ at the top, $x=l$ at the bottom). 
The material has density $\rho$ (mass per unit volume), subject to gravitational acceleration $g$. 

The ultimate tensile strength (stress limit) of the material is $\sigma$. 
Define the \emph{breaking length}
\[
b \;\equiv\; \frac{\sigma}{\rho g},
\]
i.e.\ a uniform vertical cable of length $b$ will break under its own weight. 

Let $A(x)$ be the cross-sectional area at height $x$, and $T(x)$ the internal tension at that section. 
A payload $P$ may be attached at the bottom ($x=l$). If no external load is present, set $P=0$. 

\paragraph{Equilibrium condition.} 
Static force balance gives
\[
T(x) \;=\; P + \rho g \int_x^l A(s)\,ds,
\]
where the integral accounts for the weight of the cable below $x$. 

\paragraph{Optimality condition.} 
To minimize mass for given $P$ and $l$, the stress constraint is active everywhere:
\[
T(x) \;=\; \sigma A(x).
\]

Differentiating with respect to $x$,
\[
\sigma A'(x) \;=\; T'(x).
\]
But
\[
T'(x) \;=\; -\,\rho g\, A(x),
\]
so that
\[
A'(x) \;=\; -\frac{\rho g}{\sigma}\, A(x) \;=\; -\frac{1}{b}\,A(x).
\]

\paragraph{Solution.} 
This linear ODE has the exponential solution
\[
A(x) \;=\; C\, e^{-x/b},
\]
for some constant $C$ determined by the boundary condition (e.g.\ the required cross-sectional area at the top or bottom).

\section{Deriving Effective Exhaust Velocity For Idealized 100\% Efficient Prograde/Retrograde PuffSat Collision Rocket Thrust, And The Real-Nozzle Correction}\label{sec:dv_effective}
The first subsection works the idealized case, in which the nozzle wastes nothing. The second introduces the loss parameter a real nozzle carries, and shows how the optimal mix moves with it.

\subsection{The Idealized Limit}
Suppose a retrograde PuffSat with mass $m_{rp}$ and a prograde PuffSat with mass $m_{pp}$ collide in a pulsed rocket propulsion chamber, as depicted in \autoref{tab:pulsed_combustion_illustration}. We want the maximum exhaust velocity $v_e$ for the prograde rocket, assuming 100\% engine efficiency. All the combined gas is expelled behind us, in the retrograde direction. For simplicity we say 
\begin{equation}
m_{rp} + m_{pp} = 1\label{eq:mass_is_1}
\end{equation} 
Each PuffSat has velocity $v_p$ so the retrograde PuffSat has velocity $2v_p$ in the reference frame of the prograde PuffSat. In the prograde reference frame, kinetic energy is
\begin{equation}
E = \frac{m_{rp} (2v_p)^2}{2} = 2m_{rp}v_p^2\label{eq:ke_PuffSats}
\end{equation}

Let $v_g$ be gas exhaust velocity expelled from the rocket, and assume the expelled gas mass is infinitesimal compared to the entire prograde rocket and its payload. Applying mass from \autoref{eq:mass_is_1}
 and kinetic energy from \autoref{eq:ke_PuffSats}, we have 
 \begin{equation}
 2m_{rp}v_p^2= \frac{v_g^2}{2}
 \end{equation}
 and solving for $v_g$ we get 
 \begin{equation}
 v_g = \sqrt{4v_p^2m_{rp}} = 2v_p\sqrt{m_{rp}} \label{eq:vg_result}
 \end{equation}
 Total momentum change is 
 \[(m_{rp} + m_{pp})v_g - 2v_pm_{rp} = v_g-2v_pm_{rp} = 2v_p(\sqrt{m_{rp}} - m_{rp}) \]
Differentiating and setting equal to zero gives
 \begin{equation}
 m_{rp} = \frac{1}{4}, \label{eq:max_m_rp}
 v_g = v_p,
 v_e= \frac{v_p}{2}
 \end{equation}

\subsection{Jet Efficiency And The Real Nozzle}\label{sec:jet_efficiency}
Two velocities appear above and they are not the same quantity. The gas speed $v_g$ is what the collision energy alone allows. It is the speed reached if every gram leaves at one speed along one axis. The effective exhaust velocity $v_e$ is the useful impulse per unit of total PuffSat mass spent. It is smaller because the retrograde PuffSat debits $2v_pm_{rp}$ of incoming momentum. At the optimum of \autoref{eq:max_m_rp}, the two differ by a factor of two, $v_g = v_p$ against $v_e = v_p/2$.

The step from $E$ to $v_g$ is where the idealization enters. Placing all the mass at one speed along one axis extracts the most momentum that its mass and energy allow. For real exhaust, the mass-weighted mean axial speed is smaller. We name its ratio to the loss-free gas speed the jet efficiency,
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \equiv \frac{\langle v_x \rangle}{v_g}, \qquad 0 < \eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \le 1,
\label{eq:eta_jet_def}
\end{equation}
where $x$ runs along the thrust axis, positive in the retrograde exhaust direction. The average covers the whole expelled mass in the rocket's frame. Cauchy--Schwarz bounds $\langle v_x\rangle$ by the actual root-mean-square speed. Energy conservation bounds that speed by $v_g$, giving the stated upper limit. Equality requires every gram to leave at the loss-free speed in the same direction. The square $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2$ is the fraction of available collision energy represented by coherent axial momentum. The single number therefore includes plume divergence, exhaust-speed spread, radiative escape, frozen ionization or dissociation energy, and mass the field fails to grip. It differs from the restitution factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}. That factor describes gas rebounding off a solid pusher plate and has no referent without a plate.

The energy is not all in one form when the nozzle receives it. Once the two PuffSats merge, the fireball's center of mass moves retrograde at $2v_pm_{rp}$. This bulk motion holds a fraction $m_{rp}$ of $E$, while the remaining fraction $1-m_{rp}$ is heat. At the three-to-one mix that is a quarter bulk and three quarters heat. The bulk part already points out the back and needs no nozzle. Its momentum equals what the retrograde PuffSat brought in, so a symmetrically expanding fireball delivers no thrust. All thrust comes from turning the thermal three quarters into one-sided flux. That is the nozzle's job.

Carrying $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ through \autoref{eq:vg_result} and the momentum balance gives
\begin{equation}
v_e = \eta_{\mathrm{jet}} v_g - 2v_pm_{rp} = 2v_p\left(\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}\sqrt{m_{rp}} - m_{rp}\right),
\label{eq:ve_general}
\end{equation}
which returns \autoref{eq:max_m_rp} at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$. Differentiating gives the general optimum,
\begin{equation}
m_{rp}^{\mathrm{opt}} = \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2}{4}, \qquad
\frac{m_{pp}}{m_{rp}} = \frac{4}{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2} - 1, \qquad
v_e^{\mathrm{opt}} = \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2\,v_p}{2}.
\label{eq:eta_optimum}
\end{equation}
The three-to-one split is the $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \to 1$ endpoint of a family, not a fixed number. A nozzle running at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.8$ prefers about five parts prograde to one retrograde.

The retrograde share arrives from outside the vehicle, so $v_e$ cannot enter the conventional rocket equation as though all spent mass came from onboard. Let $\mu$ be the total collision mass spent during a burn and $M_f$ the vehicle's final mass. Only the prograde share $(1-m_{rp})\mu$ is drawn from the vehicle's initial mass. For constant $m_{rp}$ and $v_e$, integration gives
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mu}{M_f} = \frac{\exp\!\left[(1-m_{rp})\Delta v/v_e\right]-1}{1-m_{rp}}.
\label{eq:external_reaction_mass}
\end{equation}
This distinction is small for the ratios used here, but it belongs in any cost or mass calculation.

\autoref{eq:ve_general} also sets a floor. Forward thrust vanishes at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$ and reverses below it. At three-to-one the floor is $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.5$. This is the sharpest difference between a nozzle and a pusher plate. A plate at low $f$ still delivers some forward momentum. A nozzle below its floor produces net backward impulse. Dilution buys margin against the floor, which falls as $\sqrt{m_{rp}}$. The twenty-four-to-one mix of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} has a floor of only $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.2$. That step therefore tolerates a poorer nozzle than the three-to-one step.

Because the floor sits close below the three-to-one optimum, the penalty curve is lopsided. Diluting past the optimum costs little and moves away from the floor, while running richer in retrograde mass walks toward it. Holding three-to-one costs about 1\% of the thrust achievable at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.9$, about 6\% at $0.8$, and about 18\% at $0.7$. A five-to-one split stays within 4\% of the achievable optimum across that whole range. We keep three-to-one because it answers the idealized question. The mission numbers elsewhere in this paper also assume a lossy nozzle at that mix. For a design rather than a derivation, erring toward more prograde propellant is the cheaper mistake.

Two places in this paper pin $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ without naming it. The lunar cycle of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} assumes $v_e = \SI{3}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ against an ideal \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at $v_p = \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. By \autoref{eq:ve_general}, this makes $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.77$. The cost cascade of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} needs about $0.89$ at its pessimistic anchor and about $0.7$ at scale. Both sit above the $0.5$ floor imposed by its three-to-one periapsis step. None comes from a nozzle model. They are requirements a magnetic-nozzle design has to meet, like the radiative-escape budget of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}.

\section{Flight-Path Angle And Downward Pulse Fraction At Interception}\label{sec:down_angle_geometry}
This appendix backs two figures from \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick}: the \SI{8.5}{\degree} flight-path angle at interception, and the growth of each pulse's downward fraction from about 15\% to roughly one-half as the craft accelerates.

A PuffSat rides a Keplerian ellipse with perigee radius $r_p = R_\oplus + \SI{50}{\kilo\meter}$, the disposal perigee of \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}, and a near-escape apogee, taken here as $r_a = R_\oplus + \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter}$ with $R_\oplus = \SI{6378}{\kilo\meter}$. Its semi-major axis, eccentricity, and semi-latus rectum are
\begin{equation}
a = \frac{r_p + r_a}{2}, \qquad e = \frac{r_a - r_p}{r_a + r_p}, \qquad p = a\,(1 - e^2).
\end{equation}
At the interception radius $r = R_\oplus + \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}$ the speed follows from the vis-viva equation, and the specific angular momentum $h = \sqrt{\mu p}$ is conserved, so the flight-path angle $\gamma$ below the local horizontal obeys the standard two-body relations \cite{vallado_astrodynamics}
\begin{equation}
v = \sqrt{\mu\!\left(\frac{2}{r} - \frac{1}{a}\right)}, \qquad \cos\gamma = \frac{h}{r\,v} = \frac{\sqrt{\mu p}}{r\,v},
\end{equation}
where $\mu = \SI{3.986e5}{\kilo\meter\cubed\per\second\squared}$ is the Earth's gravitational parameter. These give $v \approx \SI{10.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ and $\gamma \approx \SI{8.5}{\degree}$, so $\sin\gamma \approx 0.15$. The low perigee sets the angle, not the apogee. Sweeping the apogee from \SI{30000}{\kilo\meter} to \SI{300000}{\kilo\meter} moves $\gamma$ only across \SIrange{7.9}{8.6}{\degree}.

The same interception velocity resolves into a forward component $v\cos\gamma \approx \SI{10.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ and a downward component $v\sin\gamma \approx \SI{1.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. The craft climbs prograde, and near orbital insertion its velocity is nearly horizontal with speed $v_c$, which builds from rest toward the circular value $v_{\mathrm{circ}} = \sqrt{\mu/r} \approx \SI{7.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. Each pulse delivers momentum along the relative velocity $\vec{u} = \vec{v} - \vec{v}_c$. Its forward part is the overtaking velocity $v\cos\gamma - v_c$, which shrinks as the craft speeds up, while its downward part holds fixed at $v\sin\gamma$. The downward fraction of the pulse is then
\begin{equation}
\frac{v\sin\gamma}{\sqrt{(v\sin\gamma)^2 + (v\cos\gamma - v_c)^2}}.
\label{eq:down_fraction}
\end{equation}
At $v_c = 0$ the relative velocity is the PuffSat's own velocity, and the fraction equals $\sin\gamma \approx 15\%$. That is the floor. As $v_c$ approaches $v_{\mathrm{circ}}$ the overtaking velocity collapses to about \SI{2.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} while the downward \SI{1.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second} holds, and \autoref{eq:down_fraction} rises to roughly one-half, near 49\%. This worst case sits late in the boost, where the craft is fastest and the residual down-force after the plate's reflection is largest.

\section{Thermal Design For A 4 Solar Radii Periapsis}\label{sec:four_radii_thermal}
This appendix backs the claim in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} that dropping the periapsis from the Parker-class baseline to the 4 solar radii of the original 2005 Solar Probe design \cite{mccomas2005solar} holds the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth-crossing speed with a boost about 30\% smaller, and it sizes the projectile shield that survives the deeper dive.

A dive from far away reaches periapsis speed $v_p = \sqrt{2\mu_\odot/r_p}$, where $\mu_\odot = \SI{1.327e20}{\meter\cubed\per\second\squared}$ is the Sun's gravitational parameter and $r_p$ the periapsis radius. At the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii this gives \SI{197}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, matching the roughly \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} used in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. At 4 solar radii it gives \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Head-on closing speeds double these figures, \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against \SI{393}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so effective exhaust velocity, which scales with collision speed (\autoref{sec:dv_effective}), rises by 57\%. The stronger Oberth effect is spent on economy rather than speed. The worked example at Parker depth, a boost from \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{250}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at periapsis, coasts out to cross \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at \SI{160}{\kilo\meter\per\second} heliocentric. At 4 solar radii a boost of only \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, about 30\% smaller, leaves about \SI{147}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of hyperbolic excess, the speed kept after climbing out of the Sun's pull, because a fixed escape surplus costs less impulse the faster the periapsis where it is bought \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Holding the crossing speed near \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than pushing higher is deliberate. That kinetic energy at Earth is already enough to throw the next payload onto its own low-periapsis orbit, and the 57\% higher exhaust velocity means each \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the smaller boost also spends less PuffSat mass.

Sunlight at 4 solar radii carries \SI{3.9}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter}, 2890 times the solar constant and 6.1 times the \SI{0.65}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter} at the Parker perihelion \cite{nasa2018parker_heat}. Radiative equilibrium temperature scales with the fourth root of flux, so the Parker probe's \SI{1377}{\celsius} sunward face \cite{parker_thermal_protection} maps to about \SI{2590}{\kelvin} at the same optical properties. That exceeds the \SI{2327}{\kelvin} melting point of alumina \cite{crc_handbook}, a representative white ceramic of the class NASA uses on Parker's sunward face \cite{parker_thermal_protection}. A sun-facing flat skin like the one in \autoref{fig:parker_cross_section} is marginal at best here, and vacuum-ultraviolet exposure drives its absorptance toward the gray-body limit where it fails outright, so geometry must shed the flux.

The fix is the one the 2005 design chose, a shield shaped as a cone pointed at the Sun \cite{mccomas2005solar}. A flank inclined at half-angle $\theta$, the angle between the sunward axis and the surface, intercepts only $\sin\theta$ of the normal flux. We take $\theta = 15^{\circ}$, which cuts the flank loading to \SI{1.0}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter}. An alumina skin, taking a representative darkened solar absorptance of 0.3 and infrared emissivity of 0.85, then equilibrates near \SI{1590}{\kelvin}, below the \SI{1650}{\kelvin} the Parker face is rated for \cite{parker_thermal_protection}. The design does not depend on the coating staying white. Vacuum-ultraviolet exposure darkens shield surfaces in ground tests \cite{balat2012_vuv_darkening}, so we also size the limit case with absorptance equal to emissivity, a fully blackened flank. It reaches \SI{2060}{\kelvin}, still about \SI{270}{\kelvin} below the alumina melting point and inside the \SIrange{1800}{2400}{\kelvin} band over which shield-grade carbon--carbon was characterized for the 4 solar radii mission \cite{balat2006_cc_emissivity}. The coating can degrade gracefully but cannot fail abruptly. Two effects set the angle. The Sun is no point source here.  Its disk subtends a half-angle of $\arcsin(1/4) \approx 14.5^{\circ}$, so a flank tilted below that still sees most of the disk and slimming stops paying. A slimmer cone also narrows the conduction path that cools the tip, treated next.

Flight experience says the darkening and dust budgets close, at least at 9.86 solar radii. The Parker probe's own instruments measured a continuous rain of sub-micron impacts through the near-Sun dust environment \cite{szalay2020_near_sun_dust}, and the spacecraft reported nominal health and communications after its closest perihelion \cite{nasa2024_parker_survives}. A projectile spends hours near 4 solar radii on a single expendable pass, and the blackened limit above already assumes the reflectance is entirely lost.

The apex is the one place the cone offers no relief, and it is smaller than it looks. An ideal cone has no sun-normal surface at all.  Every flank element down to the apex sees the same $\sin\theta$ loading. Only a blunted apex of radius $a$ intercepts normal flux, and conduction clamps a small one to the flank temperature. The intercepted power grows as $a^2$ while the conduction resistance into the widening cone falls as $1/a$, giving
\begin{equation}
\Delta T \approx \frac{\alpha S a}{k \tan\theta},
\end{equation}
where $S$ is the normal flux, $\alpha$ the absorptance, and $k$ the conductivity. For a graphite apex with $a = \SI{50}{\micro\meter}$ and $k \approx \SI{50}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin}$ at temperature, the rise is about \SI{15}{\kelvin}. The clamp weakens linearly with bluntness. Near $a \sim \SI{1}{\milli\meter}$ the rise passes \SI{250}{\kelvin}, and by a few millimeters the apex decouples and tends toward the \SI{2890}{\kelvin} equilibrium of an isolated sun-normal gray surface. Dust can do the blunting mid-pass. Sub-micron grains arriving at \SI{300}{\kilo\meter\per\second} would crater tens of times their own size, and with the near-Sun dust density Parker measured \cite{szalay2020_near_sun_dust} the tip's \SI{e-7}{\square\meter} frontal area sees of order one strike per pass, an extrapolation beyond the roughly \SI{100}{\kilo\meter\per\second} impacts Parker actually recorded. One unlucky strike could seed a runaway hot spot. A tungsten apex insert, seated in fine-grained graphite because woven carbon--carbon cannot hold a \SI{100}{\micro\meter} point, retires the risk. Sharp, tungsten enjoys the same conduction clamp. Blunted, its \SI{3695}{\kelvin} melting point \cite{crc_handbook} absorbs the \SI{2890}{\kelvin} worst case with \SI{800}{\kelvin} of margin, whatever happens to its surface finish. Hafnium and tantalum carbides melt about \SI{500}{\kelvin} higher still \cite{uhtcmc_review}, but the tip is not melting-limited, so that headroom goes unused. Tungsten is preferred for the properties the tip does exercise. Its conductivity runs several times that of those ceramics, which tightens the same clamp, and as a ductile metal it survives the dust cratering that would spall a brittle ceramic point. Tungsten's high atomic number also serves the opacity requirement of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}, standing in for part of the iron dopant.

Bare carbon flanks would survive the blackened-limit temperature outright, so the alumina needs a different justification, and it is navigational. A surface subliming into vacuum recoils with a pressure of about half its vapor pressure. Carbon at \SI{2060}{\kelvin} sits near \SI{2e-4}{\pascal}, a recoil about 1\% of the \SI{0.013}{\newton\per\square\meter} of solar radiation pressure at this depth. The problem is not the size but the predictability. Carbon's vapor pressure doubles roughly every \SI{35}{\kelvin} at these temperatures, so a few-percent error in modeled flank temperature moves the recoil by tens of percent, while the solar output driving radiation pressure is known to a small fraction of a percent. Millimeter-scale terminal targeting (\autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}) has no room for an acceleration that uncertain. Alumina at \SI{1590}{\kelvin} and tungsten at \SI{2100}{\kelvin} both hold vapor pressures below \SI{e-8}{\pascal}, removing the term from the guidance model.

Behind the skin, Parker provides a construction precedent and flight thermal data, though not a directly transferable calibration. Its flown thermal protection system uses an \SI{11.43}{\centi\meter} carbon-foam core between carbon--carbon face sheets. At perihelia 17 and 18, corresponding to 354 solar constants, a JHU/APL thermal model placed the sunward surface near \SI{840}{\celsius}, while sensors on the barrier blanket facing the shield's backside read \SIrange{225}{230}{\celsius}, and the propellant tank stayed between \SIrange{20}{25}{\celsius} \cite{ercol2024_parker_thermal}. These are system-level results that depend on the shield, barrier blankets, and spacecraft geometry, so they do not by themselves fix an effective foam conductivity for the proposed cone. What they do show is that a passive stack of this class holds a near-room-temperature interior behind an \SI{840}{\celsius} face, which is the outcome the design needs. The last step is geometric, as it is on Parker. A box in the shadowed interior balances radiation from the hot rear face against its own emission to cold space, settling near $T_{\mathrm{box}} \approx F^{1/4}\, T_{\mathrm{rear}}$, where the view factor $F$ is the fraction of the box's sky the hot surface fills. Parker's barrier-blanket backside sits near \SI{500}{\kelvin}, so even a modest standoff, or one low-emissivity barrier sheet in between, holds the box under \SI{85}{\celsius}. That sheet can be a single aluminized polyimide layer of the kind stacked five deep in the JWST sunshield \cite{jwst_sunshield}. Aluminum reflects about 97\% of the rear face's mid-infrared emission, so with its bare polyimide face toward the box the layer floats near room temperature. A coupled thermal model and high-flux vacuum test must still fix the cone's rear-face temperature, foam thickness, and electronics view factors under both nominal and blackened-surface cases. The requirement, keeping the guidance electronics below \SI{85}{\celsius} throughout the pass, sits inside what Parker's stack already demonstrates.

The shadow extends beyond the interior. Behind the base plane of a cone of base radius $R_b$, the shield casts an umbra, the region from which the shield hides the whole solar disk. It narrows at the $14.5^{\circ}$ solar half-angle and closes about $3.9\,R_b$ aft, so a point at depth $z$ behind the base stays fully shadowed while its radial offset is under $R_b - 0.26\,z$. The 2005 design sheltered its whole spacecraft bus in this cone of shade \cite{mccomas2005solar}. Here it hosts the gram-scale terminal-guidance hardware of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}, a camera and a pulsed beacon tucked just inside the rim of the base. The corona itself does not relight the shadow. Thomson scattering off coronal electrons, at densities near \SI{e5}{\per\cubic\centi\meter} at this height \cite{leblanc1998corona}, redirects of order $10^{-9}$ of the local flux onto shadowed surfaces, tens of milliwatts per square meter. A shadowed device therefore sees the room-temperature barrier sheet on one side and cold space on the other. Its temperature is set by its own dissipation, and milliwatt heaters hold whatever setpoint the detector prefers. This also retires the thermal-distortion concern of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} for the periapsis leg. The radiative load on the optics sits nine orders of magnitude below what a sunlit aperture would absorb, so the smooth heating that star-differencing exists to cancel never develops.

The umbra's edge is a cliff, so the margin is geometric rather than thermal. A gray surface seeing a fraction $f$ of the disk equilibrates near $2890\,f^{1/4}$~\si{\kelvin}, about \SI{910}{\kelvin} at $f = 1\%$. A pointing error $\delta$ sweeps the shadow boundary sideways by $z\delta$ at depth $z$, so a device a few centimeters inside the boundary, mounted a fraction of a meter behind the base, tolerates pointing errors of several degrees, far looser than what the flank geometry already needs. Sight lines cooperate at periapsis, where the velocity vector runs perpendicular to the sun-line. Every camera in the terminal-guidance chain views a beacon along the approach corridor, about $90^{\circ}$ from the Sun, so the disk never enters a field of view, and keeping sunlight off the optics takes a straylight baffle rather than a coronagraph. The same baffle hides the one hot thing a rim-mounted device could see, the thin glowing edge where the sunlit flank meets the base. The coronal background behind a beacon, viewed at tangent heights of 4 solar radii and beyond, is what the narrowband filter and pulse gating of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} already reject.

\section{Earth Re-Intercept and the Phasing Loop}\label{sec:earth_reintercept}
The cycle in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} sends a projectile from Earth to a low solar periapsis, boosts it, and brings it back across \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} or more. Crossing \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} is not the same as reaching Earth. This appendix shows why the two differ by a wide margin, why the miss cannot be fixed at periapsis, and what the three practical fixes cost. The numbers are computed in the companion calculations repository \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Every boost below is a PuffSat collision, as elsewhere in this cycle, not carried propellant.

A boost that lifts the periapsis speed past the local escape speed leaves the projectile on an escaping hyperbola. Falling from a \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion, the projectile reaches the 4-solar-radii periapsis at about \SI{306}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, just under the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} local escape speed. A \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost lifts it to about \SI{342}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the outbound leg escapes with about \SI{147}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to spare, near the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth-crossing scale (\autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}). The wider-aphelion resonant return below arrives faster at periapsis and keeps the full \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The projectile crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} exactly once on the way out, and Earth has to be there when it does. There is no second pass.

The natural crossing lands far from Earth. A minimum-energy dive from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} to 4 solar radii is half the period of an \SI{0.509}{\astronomicalunit} ellipse, about 66 days, and the boosted climb-out adds about ten more. In that \SI{0.21}{\year} Earth advances about \SI{76}{\degree}. The projectile sweeps much farther. It covers \SI{180}{\degree} of heliocentric longitude falling from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} to periapsis, then about \SI{130}{\degree} more whipping back out, roughly \SI{310}{\degree} in all. It re-crosses about \SI{50}{\degree} behind the longitude it launched from, while Earth has moved \SI{76}{\degree} ahead, so the gap is about \SI{125}{\degree}. The miss comes from the projectile whipping around the Sun, and it barely depends on periapsis depth.

Neither periapsis knob helps. Turning the velocity vector by an angle $\theta$ near periapsis costs about $2v_p\sin(\theta/2)$, which at the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis speed is \SI{5.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} per degree. The miss is tens of degrees, so a periapsis re-aim runs to hundreds of \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} against the roughly \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of heliocentric $\Delta V$ that sets up the dive. Direction changes want the slowest point on the orbit, and periapsis is the fastest. Rotating the orbit's long axis fails too on a fast dive. Launching from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} onto an orbit whose periapsis is 4 solar radii forces Earth to sit near that orbit's aphelion at departure. That pins the periapsis direction opposite Earth, which pins the outbound crossing about \SI{50}{\degree} behind the launch longitude. The long axis is not a free parameter when the launch point is the aphelion of a deep diver, so the fix has to act on timing, not on aim.

The round trip is fixed near \SI{0.21}{\year}, but Earth returns to the crossing longitude only after it sweeps around to that point, which first happens near \SI{0.86}{\year}. Closing the gap means stretching the trip to that resonance, or bending it with a flyby. Three methods trade impulse, time, and network complexity differently.

\paragraph{Two-impulse phasing loop.} Insert a shallow dip before the deep dive. A PuffSat boost at Earth nudges the projectile onto an orbit with periapsis near \SI{0.50}{\astronomicalunit}. It returns to the launch point after \SI{0.65}{\year}, moving tangentially at the same speed it left, where a second PuffSat boost drops it into the deep dive. Both boosts point retrograde and lie along the same line, so the sequence \SI{29.78}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{24.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{5.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second} sums to the same \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} as a direct dive. The delay is free in total impulse, which holds the per-cycle doubling factor at two. Lowering periapsis beats raising aphelion, which points the two boosts in opposing directions and wastes about \SI{7.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for a longer \SI{1.86}{\year} resonance. The price is a second boost node. The loop returns to \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} far from Earth, so the network must field a retrograde PuffSat stream at that point and time. A mature network provides it, and an early one may not, which motivates the next two options.

\paragraph{Single-impulse resonant dive.} Fold the phasing into the single boost the projectile receives at Earth. Aim it outbound onto an orbit with aphelion near \SI{1.9}{\astronomicalunit} and periapsis at 4 solar radii. It coasts out, falls back through \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, dives, and re-crosses after about \SI{0.89}{\year} with Earth waiting. The aphelion is the free knob that closes the geometry. This needs only the Earth boost node, but the boost grows from \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to about \SI{37.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second}: the same \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} retrograde component as a direct dive plus a \SI{29}{\kilo\meter\per\second} outbound radial component that buys the phasing coast. A heavier boost spends more PuffSats per projectile, so the returning fleet launches a smaller next payload and the doubling factor falls below two.

\paragraph{Gravity-assist resonant return.} Bend the return with a Venus or Earth flyby rather than a boost. A well-placed flyby rotates the trajectory and trims its period for no impulse, the tool the initial low-periapsis orbits already use (\autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}). The cost moves into scheduling. A flyby helps only when the assisting planet is in place, so the cycle stretches to perhaps one or two years and the launch windows thin. Unlike the two-impulse and single-impulse fixes above, this gravity-assist option is not modeled in the companion repository, so its cycle time is an estimate rather than a computed trajectory. This is the impulse-free option when the timing cooperates.

The cost lands on the growth rate. The cycle that launches the next, larger payload cannot close faster than one Earth return, so the doubling interval is the re-intercepting cycle, not the bare dive time, and its floor is near \SI{0.86}{\year}. Twenty doublings reach a millionfold, so the payload grows by a factor of a million in about 17 years rather than the under-a-decade a \SI{0.5}{\year} cycle would imply. Only the two-impulse loop holds the doubling factor at two and reaches that 17-year figure. The single-impulse dive stretches it through a smaller factor, and the gravity assist through a longer cycle. Every case is a floor, since it assumes each cycle catches its resonance with no turnaround at Earth.

\paragraph{Loops without the dive.} Every option above phases the same \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} dive. Two gentler cycles skip it entirely: the Jupiter return of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth}, whose PuffSats cross Earth at about \SI{61}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and push about 8 times their weight per collision, and the apoapsis-raise loop of \autoref{sec:apoapsis_raise_loop}, which closes at about \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on carried propellant and multiplies the fleet by 1.55 each cycle.

\section{Spin-Axis Reorientation Cost For A Tethered Ring}\label{sec:spin_reorientation}
This appendix backs the re-pointing figure quoted in \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}. Two attitudes compete for the ring's spin axis. Centrifugal stirring wants it perpendicular to the Sun-line, so that no part of a body holds a fixed sunward face (\autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}). The arriving disk wants the ring plane square to the closing velocity, which puts the spin axis along that velocity. Whether the two agree depends on where the Sun sits relative to the interception track, and the worst case has them a full \SI{90}{\degree} apart. Every result below is closed form.

\subsection*{The Impulse Bound}
A rigid assembly spinning at rate \(\omega\) about a principal axis carries angular momentum \(L = I\omega\). Re-pointing that axis through an angle \(\theta\) at unchanged spin rate leaves \(|L|\) fixed, so the required change in the angular-momentum vector is the chord of an isosceles triangle,
\begin{equation}
\left|\Delta \vec{L}\right| = 2 L \sin(\theta/2).
\label{eq:reorient_chord}
\end{equation}
Thrusters mounted a distance \(r\) from the axis generate a torque no larger than \(r\) times the force they exert. For a total expended linear impulse \(J = \int \lvert\vec{F}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}t\), summed over both thrusters,
\begin{equation}
\left|\Delta \vec{L}\right| = \left|\int \vec{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}t\right| \le \int \lvert\vec{\tau}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}t \le r\,J,
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
J \ge \frac{2 I \omega \sin(\theta/2)}{r}.
\label{eq:reorient_bound}
\end{equation}
Expressing that impulse as the velocity change the same propellant would have bought on the assembly's total mass \(M\), and writing \(k^2 = I/M\) for the squared radius of gyration,
\begin{equation}
\Delta v = \frac{J}{M} \ge 2\sin(\theta/2)\,\frac{\omega k^{2}}{r}.
\label{eq:reorient_dv}
\end{equation}
The cost is set by \(k^{2}/r\), the mass distribution measured against the thruster moment arm, and not by either alone. Compact mass on a long arm is cheap. Mass spread out as far as the arm is not, which is why the figures below depend on the water staying near \SI{2}{\meter} while the thrusters ride at the tether ends.

\subsection*{The Path Actually Flown}
The bound of \autoref{eq:reorient_bound} is reached only by driving \(\Delta\vec{L}\) straight along the chord. That drops the spin rate to \(\cos(\theta/2)\) of its starting value at the midpoint, which slackens the spokes, and it leaves \(\vec{L}\) off the symmetry axis so the assembly nutates, its spin axis wobbling around a cone instead of holding still. The standard spin-stabilized maneuver instead fires short pulses at a fixed body azimuth, the same clock angle around the spin on every turn, once per rotation \cite{wertz1978attitude}. Each pulse tips \(\vec{L}\) perpendicular to itself, so \(\lvert\mathrm{d}\vec{L}\rvert = L\,\mathrm{d}\theta\) and \(\lvert L\rvert\) never changes. That walks the great circle rather than the chord, at a cost of
\begin{equation}
J_{\mathrm{arc}} = \frac{I \omega \theta}{r},
\label{eq:reorient_arc}
\end{equation}
which for \(\theta = \pi/2\) is \(\pi/(2\sqrt{2}) = 1.11\) times the bound. Holding the spin rate fixed is worth that 11\%, because spoke tension is what holds the ring's shape. Firing over a finite azimuth window \(\Delta\phi\) rather than in an instant costs a further factor. Only the component along the wanted direction survives the average, so the useful fraction is
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{duty}} = \frac{\sin(\Delta\phi/2)}{\Delta\phi/2}.
\label{eq:reorient_duty}
\end{equation}
A \SI{60}{\degree} window keeps \SI{95.5}{\percent} and a \SI{180}{\degree} window only \SI{63.7}{\percent}.

\subsection*{Moment Of Inertia Of The Ring}
Take the configuration of \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}, with ten \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} bags on a ring of radius \(R = \SI{2}{\meter}\) and one more on the axis. The two dry-mass packages of \autoref{sec:spinning_tension_detail} sit at the ends of the \SI{25}{\meter} tether bundle, so their moment arm is \(r = \SI{12.5}{\meter}\). Each carries half the \SI{250}{\gram} dry mass and half the \SI{500}{\gram} propellant load, or \(m_d = \SI{375}{\gram}\). Bags off the axis enter as point masses at their own radius, and the hub bag is a \SI{0.13}{\meter} sphere on the axis contributing \(\tfrac{2}{5}m a^{2} = \SI{0.06}{\kilo\gram\meter\squared}\), which rounds away. So
\begin{equation}
I = 10\,m_{\mathrm{bag}} R^{2} + 2 m_d r^{2} = 360 + 117 = \SI{477}{\kilo\gram\meter\squared},
\label{eq:reorient_inertia}
\end{equation}
against \(M = \SI{100}{\kilo\gram}\), a radius of gyration of \SI{2.18}{\meter}. The \SI{750}{\gram} of packages, under \SI{1}{\percent} of the mass, supply a quarter of the inertia, because inertia weights mass by the square of its radius.

\subsection*{Result}
At the \SI{1}{\radian\per\second} spin of \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}, a \SI{90}{\degree} re-point along the great circle needs \(I\omega\theta = \SI{749}{\newton\meter\second}\) of angular impulse. Divided by the \SI{12.5}{\meter} arm that is \SI{60}{\newton\second} of expended impulse, or \SI{63}{\newton\second} through a \SI{60}{\degree} firing window, an equivalent \SI{0.63}{\meter\per\second} on the full \SI{100}{\kilo\gram}. The drag-cancellation pass of \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas} models \SI{20}{\milli\newton} for \SI{300}{\second} plus a \SI{400}{\milli\newton} surge for \SI{30}{\second}, \SI{18}{\newton\second} before margins, so the worst-case re-point costs about \num{3.5} times that leg. The tank is the fairer measure, since that appendix carries wide margins. The \SI{500}{\gram} load of \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice} holds \SI{123}{\newton\second} at the pessimistic \SI{25}{\second} specific impulse assumed there, and \SI{211}{\newton\second} at the \SI{43}{\second} flown propane systems reach, so the re-point takes about half the tank on the first figure and under a third on the second. \autoref{eq:reorient_arc} is linear in \(\theta\), so a \SI{30}{\degree} correction costs a third of the \SI{90}{\degree} figure.

Timing turns on how the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters} is read. That figure is the peak drag force on the whole vehicle, so the conservative reading shares it as \SI{200}{\milli\newton} at each end. The two packages then form a couple of \SI{5}{\newton\meter} while firing, with no net force on the assembly. A \SI{60}{\degree} window delivers \SI{5}{\newton\meter\second} per rotation, so the maneuver takes 150 rotations, or \SI{942}{\second}. Sizing each package for the full \SI{400}{\milli\newton} instead halves that to \SI{471}{\second}. Impulse and propellant are unchanged either way, because only the firing rate moves. Widening the window to \SI{180}{\degree} halves the wall-clock time again and raises the propellant by half.

The spokes take a modest transverse load while the axis moves. Precessing at \(\Omega_p = \theta/t\) requires an out-of-plane force on each bag with peak \(2 m \omega \Omega_p R\), against a spoke tension of \(m \omega^{2} R\), so the ring cones out of its plane by
\begin{equation}
\tan\psi = \frac{2\Omega_p}{\omega} = \frac{\theta}{\pi N},
\label{eq:reorient_coning}
\end{equation}
where \(N\) is the number of rotations the maneuver takes. At \(N = 150\) that is \SI{0.19}{\degree}, and at \(N = 75\) still only \SI{0.38}{\degree}. The tension rises by under a part in ten thousand in both cases.

\subsection*{Choosing The Moment Arm}
Pushing the packages further out lengthens the arm but adds inertia as \(r^{2}\). Writing \(I = I_0 + 2 m_d r^{2}\), with \(I_0\) the water ring's own inertia,
\begin{equation}
J_{\mathrm{arc}}(r) = \omega\theta\left(\frac{I_0}{r} + 2 m_d r\right),
\qquad
r_{*} = \sqrt{\frac{I_0}{2 m_d}} = \SI{21.9}{\meter},
\qquad
J_{\min} = 2\omega\theta\sqrt{2 m_d I_0} = \SI{52}{\newton\second}.
\label{eq:reorient_optimum}
\end{equation}
The minimum is shallow. The \SI{12.5}{\meter} arm the tether bundle already provides sits \SI{16}{\percent} above it, and any arm between \SI{10}{\meter} and \SI{45}{\meter} stays within \SI{35}{\percent}. Mounting the thrusters on the bags instead, at \(r = R = \SI{2}{\meter}\), would cost \SI{285}{\newton\second}, nearly five times as much. The long tether the dry-mass disposal scheme already carries is what makes this maneuver cheap.

\subsection*{Why The Maneuver Waits}
Flying the thermal attitude through the coast and re-pointing in the last hours costs almost nothing thermally. The hub bag is the only body whose stirring depends on the Sun angle. It straddles the axis rather than sitting off it, so its own field runs from zero on the axis out to \(\omega^{2}a = \SI{0.13}{\meter\per\second\squared}\) at its equator. Where the Sun lands decides whether the heating meets that field or the null at its center. The \SIrange{10}{15}{\watt} of \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} is quoted for a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} body, and scaling it by projected area puts the hub bag near \SIrange{5}{8}{\watt}. The same figure follows from the \SI{38}{\watt} the \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} body of \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal} radiates in balance. Even with nothing radiated, \SI{8}{\watt} raises its \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} of water by only \SI{2.2}{\kelvin} in three hours, against a tolerated band a hundred kelvin wide.

The mean is the easy bound, and it hides which attitude is at issue. Through the coast the Sun lies in the hub bag's equatorial plane, so the heating lands where its own field is strongest and sweeps around once per rotation. Warm water at the rim sits at the bottom of an outward field and overturns, and the permanently cold poles are what the aluminum-out caps of \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal} already answer. That is the good case, and it is the one flown for all but the last hours.

Re-pointing the axis at the Sun is the bad case. It moves the sunlit pole onto the centrifugal null, and warm water floats toward the axis, so the cap has nowhere left to float and sits conducting. Crossing the whole bag that way is slow, taking \(a^{2}/\kappa \approx \SI{32}{\hour}\) at a \SI{0.13}{\meter} radius, where \(\kappa\) is the thermal diffusivity of water. Three hours is far short of that, so the heat stays in a skin \(\sqrt{\kappa t} \approx \SI{4}{\centi\meter}\) deep and the surface rise follows \(2q\sqrt{\kappa t/\pi}/k\). Averaged over the lit hemisphere, \SI{8}{\watt} is \SI{76}{\watt\per\square\meter} and lifts the cap \SI{6}{\kelvin} above the interior. A \(\cos\theta\) distribution peaks at twice that, \SI{11}{\kelvin} at the sub-solar point, and that reading is pessimistic, because the water sees the bumper's inner face across the gap rather than the Sun. Both figures ignore the vapor space's latent transport and the roughly \SI{0.5}{\watt\per\kelvin} by which the cap's net intake falls as it warms. Neither threatens the liquid range.

The ten ring bags are indifferent to the change. A ring bag's centrifugal field points outward from the spin axis and never changes direction in the bag's own frame. The Sun direction does change, and it cannot be averaged as a vector, because a surface element of outward normal \(\vec{n}\) absorbs as \(\max(0,\,\vec{n}\cdot\vec{s})\). Average that quantity instead. Over one rotation \(\vec{s}\) sweeps a cone about the spin axis, so the mean depends on \(\vec{n}\) only through its angle to that axis. The bag's outward and inward faces share the same angle, so they collect identical mean flux at every Sun angle, and no mean gradient forms along the field. The bag stays a side-heated cavity with no onset value to clear (\autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}).

\section{Tensile-Fiber Selection for PuffSat Tension Members}\label{sec:fiber_selection}
Three PuffSat structures carry organic fiber under steady tension above most of the atmosphere: the vacuum launch column (\autoref{sec:tether_tapering}), the Mars skyhook tether (\autoref{sec:mars_skyhook}), and the polymer net behind the pusher-plate sail (\autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}). The same four fibers are candidates in each case, but a different property decides the choice.
