The column turns on self-support length, the skyhook on creep and specific strength, and the sail net on shock toughness within a temperature-ceiling limit. \autoref{tab:fiber_selection} collects the numbers, and this section gives the reasoning the three sites share.

Self-support breaking length scales with specific strength. PBO, the fiber sold as Zylon, reaches a self-support length near \SI{380}{\kilo\meter}, about \num{1.6} times Vectran's \cite{zylon_technical_info}. SK99 Dyneema's instantaneous figure is longer still, an estimated \SI{430}{\kilo\meter} from its \SI{20}{\percent} tenacity gain over SK78 \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}, but it holds only for short loads.  Creep under sustained tension disqualifies polyethylene below. Kevlar, near \SI{250}{\kilo\meter} \cite{cambridge_materials_databook}, falls short of the \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} a tall column spans, and Vectran is shorter still. Taper closes that gap, but its mass penalty climbs steeply as self-support length drops, so the column favors the fiber with the most margin (see \autoref{sec:tether_tapering}). Creep separates the fibers under permanent load. Drawn polyethylene creeps even when carbon-modified for creep resistance \cite{sk99_tension}. Vectran, a liquid-crystal polymer, all but eliminates creep \cite{vectran_brochure}, and PBO also creeps very little \cite{zylon_technical_info}. A structure that holds steady tension for years, such as a spinning skyhook, rules out polyethylene on creep alone.

Temperature ceiling forms a ladder. Drawn polyethylene like Dyneema melts near \SI{147}{\celsius} \cite{uhmwpe_fiber_datasheet}. Vectran retains about half its strength near \SI{150}{\celsius} and useful strength to roughly \SI{200}{\celsius}, melting above \SI{300}{\celsius} \cite{vectran_brochure}. Aramid does not melt, keeps most of its strength to about \SI{250}{\celsius}, and decomposes above \SI{425}{\celsius} \cite{kevlar_technical_guide}. PBO tops the ladder and decomposes only near \SI{650}{\celsius} \cite{zylon_technical_info}. A back ply that runs hot must climb the ladder, while one that radiates cool between widely spaced pulses can sit lower.

Cold sits at the safe end of that ladder. A drawn or rigid-rod fiber loses strength as it warms, not as it cools. PBO holds its strength down to cryogenic temperature. A Zylon/epoxy composite tested at \SI{77}{\kelvin} keeps a fiber tensile strength above \SI{4.3}{\giga\pascal}, which is why it reinforces high-field magnet windings \cite{zylon_cryogenic}. A night-side pass on an Earth column or a Mars tether stays far warmer than that, so the cold does not weaken the load-bearing fiber. The property to watch is brittleness, and it is not a cold effect. PBO is a low-strain fiber at every temperature. Its elongation at break is \SIrange{2.5}{3.5}{\percent}, and it fails by axial splitting with little plastic flow \cite{zylon_technical_info}. As a rigid-rod aromatic polymer, it has no conventional glass transition in this range, so it shows no cold ductile-to-brittle transition like the one that embrittles ordinary plastics. The transition it does show is set by strain rate, not temperature. A fast pull or a shock load fractures it with little warning \cite{pbo_strain_rate_dbt}.

A static tension member tolerates a brittle fiber. The launch column and the skyhook tether hold a steady axial load, where strength and low creep decide and elongation does not. Brittleness bites only where the fiber folds, knots, bends over a small radius, or takes a shock. The architecture keeps PBO clear of those cases. Terminations spread load by potting or capstan wraps rather than knots. Bend radii stay generous. The curvature actuators of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} ramp slowly, so the line never sees an impulsive spike. Redundant parallel cables cover a fiber that nicks rather than yields, since it cannot shed a defect by flowing around it. Parts that must fold or flex, such as the corrugated lower tube wall or the pusher-plate sail net, use Vectran instead. Vectran resists flex-cracking when cold, the property that won it over aramid for the Mars Pathfinder airbags, where folded aramid cracked along its creases \cite{pathfinder_airbag}.

The sail net pushes that logic furthest, because it is the one tension member loaded by shock rather than steady weight. Each pulse arrives as a brief, high strain-rate impulse, the regime where PBO turns brittle \cite{pbo_strain_rate_dbt}. The net exists to absorb that impulse, so shock toughness decides the choice and PBO's specific-strength lead does not apply. Vectran takes the role. It holds the impulse without creep, so the sail stays taut across a long pulse train \cite{vectran_brochure}. Temperature is the one limit on that choice. Vectran keeps useful strength only to about \SI{200}{\celsius}, far below PBO's \SI{650}{\celsius} \cite{vectran_brochure, zylon_technical_info}. The stitched laminate exists to keep the back ply under that ceiling. The ceramic felt insulates it, and a brief, widely spaced pulse radiates away before its heat reaches the net (see \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}). While the back-ply equilibrium temperature stays below \SI{200}{\celsius}, Vectran wins outright. A hotter or more tightly spaced pulse train, or a thinner felt budget, forces the net up the ladder to aramid or coated PBO for thermal survival. That climb trades shock toughness for thermal headroom, paid back with a thicker net or wider pulse spacing.

Three environmental weaknesses shape whichever fiber gets exposed. Atomic oxygen erodes organic polymers in low orbit \cite{ao_protection_banks}. Ultraviolet weakens aramid, Vectran, and PBO. Moisture drives slow hydrolysis in aramid \cite{kevlar_technical_guide}, and PBO shares that weakness. PBO is the most exposed of the four. Bare PBO sheds about a third of its strength in a few hours of atomic oxygen \cite{pbo_atomic_oxygen}, and ultraviolet and visible light break its oxazole ring, and fielded PBO body armor lost roughly half its strength over a few years of service, combined with moisture, the failure that ended its use in police body armor \cite{zylon_body_armor}. One continuous silica or metallized barrier answers these weaknesses together. It reflects the ultraviolet and seals out the atomic oxygen at once, the same low-outgassing inorganic coating that has kept polyimide solar-array blankets intact in low orbit for years \cite{ao_protection_banks}, and behind it the fiber stays dry so the hydrolysis pathway is largely suppressed. A barrier helps only while it stays continuous. Micrometeoroid strikes and thermal cycling can open pinholes or hairline cracks, allowing atomic oxygen to undercut the fiber at each defect. A closer laboratory precedent is a UPy-functionalized polyhedral oligomeric silsesquioxane (UPy-POSS) coating on polyimide. It forms an atomic-oxygen-resistant silica surface and healed cracks at \SI{80}{\celsius} or during atomic-oxygen exposure while surviving repeated bending and thermal cycling \cite{wang2018_self_healing_ao}. The result does not qualify the coating on PBO or under tensile load and hypervelocity damage. A coated-PBO candidate therefore needs adhesion, ultraviolet and atomic-oxygen exposure, thermal-cycling, tension, and puncture tests before it can be credited with protecting the fiber. This coating remains the least-proven part of the PBO design. PBO is therefore the default where the environment is controlled and a liability where it is not, such as humid air near the surface.

PBO is not new to spaceflight. Metal-clad PBO is a stock product, and the fiber already carries the suspension loads of Crew Dragon's main parachutes as a space-rated tension member \cite{crew_dragon_parachute, zylon_technical_info}.

\begin{table}[htpb]
    \centering
    \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|l|L|L|L|L|}\hline
        \textbf{Fiber} & \textbf{Self-support length} & \textbf{Creep} & \textbf{Temperature ceiling} & \textbf{Main vulnerability} \\\hline
        UHMWPE (Dyneema) & $\sim$\SI{430}{\kilo\meter} instantaneous; creep-limited under sustained load & creeps, even carbon-modified & melts $\sim$\SI{147}{\celsius} & low temperature ceiling; creep \\\hline
        Aramid (Kevlar) & below \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} & low & no melt; to $\sim$\SI{250}{\celsius}, decomposes above \SI{425}{\celsius} & moisture uptake; hydrolysis \\\hline
        Vectran (LCP) & $\sim$\SI{240}{\kilo\meter} & none & to $\sim$\SI{200}{\celsius}; melts above \SI{300}{\celsius} & lower specific strength \\\hline
        PBO (Zylon) & $\sim$\SI{380}{\kilo\meter} & none & decomposes $\sim$\SI{650}{\celsius} & photodegradation; atomic-oxygen erosion \\\hline
    \end{tabularx}
    \caption{Tensile-fiber candidates for PuffSat tension members. Self-support length is the breaking length under the fiber's own weight in Earth gravity, and it scales with specific strength.  Vectran's entry follows from PBO's \SI{380}{\kilo\meter} and the \num{1.6} ratio. PBO's two vulnerabilities close together: the coating that shields it from atomic oxygen also blocks the light that drives its photodegradation, and a dry environment suppresses its hydrolysis.}
    \label{tab:fiber_selection}
\end{table}

 \section{AI And Citations Transparency Acknowledgement}
 This paper's ideas are my own. While I used AI tools to create some artwork and suggest writing improvements, any potential AI regurgitation of others' work is unintentional. Please inform me if you find any uncited material. In addition to ChatGPT \cite{chatgpt} and Grok \cite{grok}, I used Claude Code \cite{claude_code} to assist with editing this paper and with code in the companion calculations repository.

As a non-expert, I may have also overlooked relevant prior work or failed to choose the most appropriate citations. If you notice any concepts presented as novel that are actually from other sources, please let me know so I can correct the citations. I've been less formal with citing my own blog posts.

I previously worked at Netflix, which produces content related to some popular culture titles referenced in the paper. However, I am no longer employed there and am not a major investor. My blog is not monetized and exists primarily to explain this paper's ideas. I do not believe I have any current financial conflicts of interest.
